## FOUNDATIONS OF STRUCTURE AND FUNCTION

_Lucía Reed López_

*[PDF p. 2]*

#### Basic concepts of waves

**IMAGING TECHNIQUES**

   1st image: **normal x-ray radiograph,** where we can observe the lungs
and the heart, which will provide info about the size of the heart. X-ray
radiographs are helpful but dangerous as they are **ionizing.** They can
cause damage to DNA. It’s the **most dangerous** imaging system, but
the **simplest and cheapest** _(along with MRI)._

   - 2nd image: **x-ray CT scan (computed tomography)** . It has the
advantage that it provides very precise and accurate information,
regarding **location and size,** about the different organs of the body.

   - 3rd image: **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging).** It relies on a
**magnetic field** with a **very high intensity** _(1.5 teslas-5 teslas, 10^4_
_times the magnetic field of the earth)_ . There is a limit to the number of
magnetic fields a person can be submitted to without suffering
consequences. In MRI we **can differentiate all the different tissues,**
allowing us to observe the **thickness** of the walls of the heart.

   - 4th image: **Ultrasound.** It’s the **less risky** of the techniques. It also has
the advantage that it can be observed in **real time.**

We have to evaluate the risks and the benefits we will obtain by using each
imaging technique. The objective is to balance out the risks and the benefits.

Depending on what we want to observe, we will use one technique or another and assume

more or less risk.

We will focus on **EM waves** _(there is an electromagnetic field being propagated)_ and
**pressure waves** _(which are the base of Ultrasound)_ _**.**_

**ELECTROMAGNETIC WAVES (EM)**

A **wave** is an **oscillation of a physical magnitud that propagates in time and space,**
**transporting energy.**

There is a **source** of waves, where the creation of

the wave occurs. The created wave **propagates**
_(it will need to be characterized),_ and once it
reaches the **target**, it can go through it creating a
**transmitted wave,** be **absorbed** or be sent back
creating a **reflected wave.**

*[PDF p. 3]*

There are 2 main families of waves: X-rays and ultrasounds

   - **X-rays** _(radiography or CTs):_ It’s based on

the **transmitted wave.**

   - **Ultrasounds:** It’s based on the **reflected**

**wave**

We can find 2 other families which are closely

related:

   - **MRI:** _(the most complex system concerning medical physics)._ There is a process
which occurs, known as resonance: a person receives EM radiation (of the order of a
**microwave** ) and absorbs it, and after a time “t”, the **amount of radiation**
**accumulated** by the person **is emitted**, revealing an image. It’s a **2 step process.**

   - **PET:** a drug with a specific **isotope** is introduced in the patient, and it **emits**
**radioactivity** depending on the amount absorbed by each organ. These isotopes
can only be absorbed by cancer cells, so it’s very useful and precise to detect

tumors.

**PERIOD, WAVELENGTH AND FREQUENCY**

The trajectory of a wave is a straight line. A source
radiates waves, forming a radius from the source to the
objective.
Magnitude H _(positive or negative, depending on whether_
_it oscillates up or down)_ has the property of **periodicity.**
we move the same distance r each time, and we reach

the same H value. This distance “r” which reflects

periodicity is known as wavelength.

   **Period (T):** For a specific point r1, we can analyze the behaviour of the wave in
relation to time: there is a **periodicity in time,** a time t after which the wave is found
at the same H. This **periodic magnitude** is known as **Period.** Measured in **seconds.**
Angular frequency (ω) is equal to 2π/T

   - **Wavelength (** λ **):** reflects the **periodicity in space.** Measured in **meters** .
Wavenumber **“K”** is equal to 2π λ/ .

   - **Frequency** equals 1/T. It’s the inverse value of the Period, measured in **Hz or s-1.**

*[PDF p. 4]*

**HARMONIC WAVES**

Physical magnitude P: P(x,t) = p0 sin (wt - kx + φ0)

This is the most simple equation that describes the oscillation of a wave.

   p0= A _(Amplitude)_

   P(x,t) = indicates it oscillates in space

and time

**●** **How can we characterize a source?**

The **source generates waves,** which propagate energy. The most important point
to characterize the source is **how much energy is produced per second.** This is
known as the **Power (P)** of the source.

  - **How much of the energy arrives at each point?**

The energy is **distributed on the whole surface,** so only a small part will reach the patient.
We need to define a magnitude which describes **how much energy reaches each point in**
**space** . In other words, how the power is distributed in space. This magnitude is known as
**Intensity (I)** of the wave.

In order to characterize the source, we use P.

In order to characterize a wave, we use I.

**REFLECTED W., TRANSMITTED W. AND ABSORPTION**

A part of the wave will be **transmitted,** and a part will be **reflected.**
The **intensity** is a magnitude that has to be **preserved** during this process. Therefore, the
intensity _(energy)_ is **split into the reflection and transmission waves.** This property is
conserved in any interphase.
Each time there is an interphase, one part is transmitted and the other, reflected.

**●** **REFLECTED INTENSITY**

        - For **X-rays and MRI**, the **reflected intensity** is very very **small,** so we can
reject it.
        - But in **ultrasounds** the **intensity of the reflected wave is much bigger,** it’s
important as it is the wave that **will give us an image.**

We can distinguish two different **types of reflection:**

   - **Specular reflection:** if the direction of the reflected waves are **parallel**
_(smooth surfaces)_

   - **Diffuse reflection:** if the directions of reflected waves follow **different**

**trajectories** _(unequal surfaces)._

*[PDF p. 5]*

**●** **ABSORPTION AND TRANSMITTED INTENSITY**

2 different situations:

1. Incoming wave acts on **material B** with intensity Io, and it’s density makes

**If = ½ Io**

2. If we **double the thickness/density** of the material, we would think that
each half is absorbed by each material. But this is wrong. **If2 = ½ If = ¼ Io**

If we have 1 layer, 2^-1 ; 2 layers, 2^-2; 3 layers 2^-3,.... etc.

The take home message is that if a given thickness reduces the intensity by a factor k,
doubling the thickness of the material does not mean that the radiation is reduced by 2k or
that the radiation absorbed is 2(1 − k). Rather, the wave leaving the material has undergone
an exponential process, k^ 2 .
In conclusion, the **absorption** of a wave is an **exponential process.**

This exponential behavior is **characteristic of a large number of wave processes**
involving interaction with matter. In **other cases,** we will look at other physical magnitudes
that are **absorbed according to an equation of type:**

The constant **α** is called the **absorption or attenuation**
**coefficient** and depends on the physical process considered _(the_
_type of wave)_ and the material. The constant P0 represents the
value of the variable before entry into the absorbing material. The
expression describes the value taken by P after passing through a

thickness x of the material.

The **coefficient** is a direct indicator of **how strong the interaction is** : a **high value** means
that the material **absorbs a lot,** and a **low value** means that it **absorbs little.**

**DISPERSION**

The original **direction of the wave** can be **modified** after
passing through a **non-homogeneous material.** This is
known as **dispersion** . It appears due to the interaction

between the atoms in the material and the wave. We can

find:

   **Normal scattering,** if the degree of dispersion is very small. The beam broadens
around its original trajectory.
   - **Back scattering,** which is **NOT the same as reflection** . Reflection appears in the
interphase and back scattering, inside the material. It occurs when the degree of
dispersion is high. Therefore, a significant part of the beam can rebound backwards
producing backscattering.

*[PDF p. 6]*



*[PDF p. 7]*

#### Radioactivity

**RADIOACTIVE SOURCES**

Radioactivity consists in the emission by some nuclei of energy in the form of an
electromagnetic wave or of subatomic particles, in order to obtain a more stable state. We
can find natural and artificial sources of radioactivity.

   - **Natural sources:** natural radioactivity comes from
**isotopes present in nature.** These isotopes have

**unstable nuclei** which will emit radiation.

Nuclei are not static, they are always moving. If they
move too fast, they can become unstable. These
isotopes can **decay into other elements or isotopes**
**to become stable**, through a **nuclear reaction.**

   - **Artificial sources:** they are **created** mainly by **using electricity.** A charged particle
is accelerated through a device with a **differential voltage (dV).** This potential
difference exerts a force on the **electron,** causing it to be **accelerated.** Its velocity
increases a lot, and it’s released through one of the plates, allowing it to escape. This
newly accelerated particle can now interact with matter. In this way, X-rays are
formed. Artificial sources **can be switched off,** stopping the x-ray emission.

**WAVE-PARTICLE DUALITY**

**Particles and waves** are **different perspectives of a single entity,** which sometimes
manifests itself as a particle and others as a wave. They don’t exist in an independent
manner. This is referred to as **wave-particle duality.**

Radioactivity can be produced from a **source of waves or particles.**

  - **Waves:** different types

**-**
Natural waves: gamma rays

**-**
Artificial waves: X rays

**●** **Particles:**

**-**
protons

**-**
positrons (e+)

**-**
electrons (e-)

**-**
Alpha particles (He nucleus)

*[PDF p. 8]*

**PHOTOELECTRIC EFFECT**

The **energy** of EM radiation waves **travels in small packets of energy,** particles known as
**photons.** The energy per photon is determined by the frequency of the radiation:

If the **frequency** of the incident wave is **higher than a threshold value (E ion),** then there
are **electrons** that are **ejected from the metal.** This phenomenon is called the
**photo-electric effect** . In other words, if the incident radiation has a frequency inferior to the

threshold, electrons are not emitted whatever the intensity of
the wave. However, above the threshold frequency, **the**
**greater the intensity, the more electrons are dislodged.** If,
on the other hand, we **increase the frequency** of the
radiation, the **more kinetic energy** in the ejected electrons.

_Conclusion_ : when we increase the intensity of the wave, we modify the number of photons
which reach the target, NOT the energy of the photons _(which remains unchanged)._ The
final kinetic energy will remain the same, but more electrons will be dislodged _(Increasing the_
_intensity means more bonds are broken within tissue when the electrons reach the patient)._
If, on the other hand, we increase the frequency and therefore the energy of the incident
photons, the kinetic energy will increase but the number of electrons will remain the same.

**Radiation** that can dislodge electrons, and therefore **ionize atoms**, is referred to as
**ionizing radiation.** By exclusion, all other types of radiation are **non-ionizing.** In the case of
the EM spectrum, the transition between ionizing and non-ionizing occurs in the **UV region.**

   - Radiation that has energy of **over 10 eV** per photon is considered to be **ionizing**
**radiation.** Ultraviolet light, X rays, and γ rays are ionizing.
   - Radiation that has energy **under 10 eV:** visible light, infrared, microwaves, and radio
waves are therefore **non-ionizing.**

**TYPES OF IONIZING RADIATION**

Ionizing radiation can come from the nuclei of certain chemical elements that emit it
**naturally,** or it can be generated **artificially**, by accelerating charged particles until they
acquire high energy or by generating photons with high ionizing power.
Focusing on **natural sources of ionizing radiation,** there are 3 types:

*[PDF p. 9]*

- α **radiation:** radioactive process in which an unstable nucleus decays into a more

stable state, releasing a **Helium nucleus** _**(alpha-particle).**_ In this process, **E is**
**released and conserved** : the initial nucleus delivers energy to the resulting nucleus
and the alpha particle _(energy along with electrical charge are conserved)._ Most of

the E is transferred to the helium nucleus.

In all the different radioactive alpha decays, the **energy delivered to each alpha**
**particle is the same.** It’s therefore a monoenergetic process: all the particles have
the same energy. This is what is known as **monoenergetic radiation** _(this also_
_occurs in a gamma process)._

- γ **radiation:** a nucleus oscillates, and during this oscillation it **releases energy in the**

**form of photons** and in a **monoenergetic** way. The original nuclei had “excess”
energy that has been released in the form of EM radiation _(we can imagine that the_
_nucleons were at a level of energy higher than some baseline level, and that they_
_have undergone de-excitation)._

The initial nucleus is said to be in a _**metastable**_ situation. The **different states**

constitute **isomers** of the element.

If **photons of the same energy** are created in other ways , from **artificial**
**sources** ,  they are referred to as **X rays.**

- β **radiation:** we refer to “beta -” radiation as beta radiation. This type of radiation is

composed of **electrons deriving from the nucleus,** which arise from the
disintegration of a **neutron into a proton.**
In this type of radiation, the emitted electrons are not found to be monoenergetic; the
electron will sometimes receive more or less energy. Their energy can have any
value between zero and a maximum value: there is a **continuous spectrum of**
**energy,** because **any intermediate value can exist.** Therefore, this type of radiation
is **polyenergetic.** The **total energy** released will be an **average of all the energies.**

*[PDF p. 10]*

_How come the process isn’t monoenergetic?_
_This non-monoenergetic characteristic cannot be explained by a reaction in which an_
_electron is the only product. To explain it, Pauli postulated that another particle (one_
_with neutral charge and little mass) was created. This particle was called a neutrino,_

_which is now referred to as electron antineutrino._

β **+ radiation** is a process very similar to β- radiation. But in this case, an electron
antiparticle is emitted, known as a **positron.** It’s identical to an electron, but with an
opposite charge (+). The positron has become important in a method of diagnosis in
medicine, the **positron emission tomography (PET scan).**

*[PDF p. 11]*

#### Interaction of radiation with matter

**PROCESSES OF DECAY**

  - **Alpha process:** situation where an isotope will decay producing an **alpha particle,**
which corresponds to **2n + 2p = a Helium nucleus.** A **particle is created.**

  - **Gamma process:** Nucleons are found in a configuration with large amounts of
energy. This energy is released in the form of EM radiation _(photon packets)._ There is
**no modification in** the nature of the **initial particle** . **Photons are created.**

  - **Beta process: particles** are created which **didn’t previously exist** on the initial
particle, and are released.

        - **Beta + :** proton decays into neutron + positron + neutrino
        - **Beta - :** neutron decays into proton + electron + antineutrino

These types of decay are **ionizing radiations** which come

from **natural sources** .

The **energy released** is the one that will be **introduced into**
**the patient.** It will be absorbed by the surrounding tissue.
Therefore, we need to know the energy which is being
released into our patient.

**MONOENERGETIC AND POLYENERGETIC RADIATIONS**

**Alpha and gamma radiations** are **monoenergetic**
**processes.** For a **large set of atoms, all** the
different ones will **decay producing the same**
**amount of energy.**

**Beta processes** on the other hand are **polyenergetic**
_(continuous spectrum):_ if we have a set of n atoms and we
compare the energy of a beta particle for 2 different
radioactive decays, the **energy released will be different**
for beta 1 particle than for beta 2.

**Alpha and gamma** particle histograms present a **sharp distribution.**
**Beta particle histogram** presents a **broad distribution of energies.** We can determine an
average value for each distribution.

*[PDF p. 12]*

**HOW TO PREDICT THE BEHAVIOUR OF A RADIOACTIVE SOURCE**

A radioactive disintegration is a **stochastic process,** meaning that it's **impossible** to predict
the behaviour of a **single nuclide.** We don’t know when it’s going to disintegrate. But we do
know that the **probability of disintegration must be the same for all the nuclides of the**
**same isotope.** The evolution of the number of atoms which remain follows an **exponential**
**decay.**

If we have a sample with _N_ _0_ nuclides, then the number of nuclei
that have not disintegrated _N(t)_ after time _t_ will be given by:

This exponential curve is **determined by the decay constant λ** _(which_
_is specific to each element)_ or by the **mean lifetime** _**(TAU)**_ **τ0 = 1/** λ, which corresponds to
the inverse value of the decay constant.

   - **Mean lifetime** ( **τ0)** : The mean lifetime represents the time at which the **number of**
**nuclides which haven’t disintegrated** is **N** **0** **/e** _(in other words,_ _**37%**_ _)._ It can also be
interpreted as the **mean of the survival times** of the nuclides of the sample. It varies
enormously for different isotopes.

**-** **Half-life (t ½ ):** It’s NOT the same as the mean lifetime. For this big group of atoms,
we can determine the half life, which represents the **time** it will take **for half of the**
**atoms to decay** _(half of the isotopes have decayed and the other half remain stable)._
Only **N** **0** **/2** _(half)_ remain non-disintegrated _(in other words,_ _**50%**_ _)._

   **Activity (A)** : the activity of a sample indicates **how many atoms disintegrate per**
**second.** It’s value **depends on** the number of atoms **(N)** present in the sample. It’s
measured in **Becquerels** _(disintegration per second)_ or in **Curies.**

Knowing the Activity, we can know the **energy released per second**
by a radioactive source. The Activity of a sample can be obtained quite
easily if we know one of the three values. _(landa, half life or mean_
_lifetime)._

*[PDF p. 13]*

**ARTIFICIAL SOURCES** **.** **X-RAY TUBES**

All clinical devices that generate high-energy photons function on the basis of the same
principle. It’s all carried out in **2 stages:**

**1.** **Electrons are accelerated** in an X-ray tube until they have **high kinetic**
**energy.** For this, we employ 2 plates with a high voltage difference
between them (∆𝑉). Any charged particle placed between the two will be
affected by an EM force. We place an electron, and due to the setup of
the plates, the exerted EM force points in a specific direction. We can
calculate the Kinetic energy of the electron as it leaves the plates:

The **energy released** can be

calculated as:

_(I = electrical current,_
_measured in Amps)_

**2.** The released electrons are made to **collide with a target** made of
a very **dense material** _(heavy material),_ which implies there will be
a high probability of interaction between electrons and atoms from
the material. The impact stops the electrons, and the energy is

transferred.

As a result of these interactions, there is a radioactive process in
which **high energy photons are generated** _(same as in gamma_
_radiation)._

When the **electron approaches** an atom inside this heavy material, it will **interact** with each
of the particles of the atom _(through weak, strong or catastrophic interactions)_ . As it passes
close to other charged particles, it will be subjected to **forces** which will cause its **deviation** .
On deviation, the **e- emits some of its kinetic energy** in the form of EM radiation. Its
**velocity** therefore **drops.** This process is known as **braking radiation or Bremsstrahlung**
**radiation.** In addition to the interaction, the e- can be **completely stopped** if it undergoes
**direct impact, or** it can **collide with an orbital electron,** knocking it out if the atom (both eleave), In this case, the remaining gap will be filled by an electron of a superior layer,
releasing energy in the form of a photon.
The **type of interaction** will depend on the **trajectory** . After each interaction, **energy will be**
**released in the form of EM radiation** _**(photons).**_

   - **Big distance** = electron is **slightly deviated**, releasing a
**small amount of energy** in the form of photons.

   - When the electron **passes closely,** the deviation will be
much stronger, generating a much **higher energy photons.**

These first two interactions are both **Bremsstrahlung interactions.**

*[PDF p. 14]*

- If the **electron collides with the nucleus**, there is a direct hit which results in a

**catastrophic interaction,** in which the **electron** is completely **destroyed.**

   - In the last case, the incoming electron **collides with an orbital electron** . Said
electron is **displaced** and a gap is left; therefore, an **upper level electron**
**transitions to the lower level, releasing energy** _(photons)_ in the process.

**Ephot = h · frequency**

For gamma radiation, frequency = Ephoton/ h
For X ray frequency, f = Ephoton / h

They are the **same/equivalent**, but **gamma** particles originate
from a **natural source** and **X-rays** from **artificial sources.** It’s
impossible to tell them both apart.

**INTERACTION OF RADIATION WITH MATTER**

1. EM RADIATION

The most important characteristic of EM radiation is the **energy per photon.**
For very low frequencies on the spectrum, we have radio waves, then microwaves, infra-red,
visible light, UV light, X-rays and gamma rays. In the middle of the **UV band**, there is a value
**(10 eV)** that **separates the spectrum** into two parts:

  - Non-ionizing radiation _(can be measured with classical physics)._

We analyze 1 single process. The EM field is propagating;
when it reaches the atom, the **charged particles** begin to
**vibrate** and **oscillate at the same frequency as the field.**
The increase in frequency increases the temperature of this
material. Can be explained by classical point of view.

  - Ionizing radiation _(measured by taking into account quantum physics)_ . We analyze 3

different processes. For ionizing radiation, we cannot use the concept of continuous
wave. We have to apply the **wave-particle duality** principle, and divide it into
**photons.** There is **1 photon per interaction.** In general terms, this results in the
extraction of an electron located in a specific orbital.

**E** **k** **= E** **photon** **- E** **ionization**

*[PDF p. 15]*

- **Photoelectric effect:** this phenomenon can only be explained by quantum
mechanics. In the EM wave, energy travels in packets _(photons)_ which carry
a specific amount of energy. The **interaction** occurs between **1 photon and 1**

**electron.**

Main characteristics: this behaviour appears for **lower energies** _(close to UV_
_limits)_ ; after the interaction, the **photon is destroyed** ; the **electron is**
**dislodged and ejected**, and it's **E** **k** **= E** **photon** **- E** **ionization** **.** No other products
are released, only the electron.

        - **Compton effect/Compton scattering:** interaction once more
between a **single photon and single electron.** But the energy
carried by the photon is so large, it's impossible to transfer it to
a single electron.

Main characteristics: this situation occurs for **intermediate**

**energies.** The **high energy photon** will transfer **part of its**
**energy to the e-,** another will be derived to surpass ionization
energy, and the **remaining** energy is **released as a new**
**photon** _(though now it carries less energy than the original)._ In
this situation, the result isn’t only an **electron, but an electron**
**+ a new photon.**

        - **Pair production:** the interaction occurs between **1 photon and 1 e-.** In the
beginning we have a **high energy photon,** which after a spontaneous
process, is split into 2 new particles with mass: an electron and a positron.
The **large amount of energy is transformed into a small amount of mass.**
Main characteristics: pair production occurs when the energy of the photon is
at least **1.022 MeV** _(mega)._ The energy is high enough to spontaneously
produce a **positron-electron pair.** Therefore, **2 new elements** are formed

that didn’t exist before.

One of the particles is present in nature _(electron),_ the other
not _(positron)_ ; therefore, after the pair production, another
process occurs: the **positron** will move away and **collide**
**with an electron** in another position of the material. Both
particles **cancel each other out,** transforming themselves
into **two photons** each with energy of **511 keV.** These two
photons will propagate in **opposite directions.**
_This process of annihilation of positrons is the basis of_
_positron emission tomography, although in this technology,_
_the positrons derive from radiopharmaceuticals that generate β + radiation._

Overview:

   The photoelectric effect implies the destruction of the photon. Low energy photons

   Compton effect results in the production of 1 photon. Intermediate energy photon

   Pair-production results in 2 photons. High energy photon _(1.022 MeV)_

In the last 2 processes _(compton and pair production),_ there is **scattering of the trajectory**
**of the released elements,** which will be a **problem.**

*[PDF p. 16]*

_**●**_ _**Why are these processes important?**_

**Depending on the energy of the incident photon,**
we will observe one process or another. For energies
up to **30 keV** (low energy), the dominant effect is the
**photo-electric;** above this level, the dominant
process is the **Compton effect up to 20 MeV;** from
this point, **pair-production** becomes dominant.
_(this is true when the medium in which the interaction_
_occurs is water; for each different tissue, the value_
_changes slightly)._

In clinical applications, this **absorption process is exponential:** the **greater the thickness**
of the material, **the greater the absorption.**
Thus, we can calculate the intensity of the transmitted beam from the incident beam I 0:

Where **µ** **total** is the **attenuation coefficient** that **takes into account all of the processes**

discussed above.

It is possible to determine the attenuation coefficient independently for each of the
processes. **If we combine them,** we can obtain the **attenuation coefficient for X-rays** as a
function of energy:

Depending on the energy of the incident photons and therefore, the **attenuation coefficient**
**will differ with energy.**

   - In the case of **photoelectric effect,** the attenuation coefficient decreases very
quickly as energy increases. In addition to this, the absorption coefficient _(in other_
_words, the probability of interaction through photo-electric effect)_ depends on the
_**cube of the atomic number**_ of the material, **Z.** Therefore, for a **beam of photons** of
given energy, the **probability of interaction through the photoelectric effect**
**depends on Z** **3** . _X-rays in this range of energies are good candidates for identifying_
_the chemical composition of different tissues and differentiating them, as they create_
_good contrast._
The **photoelectric effect** is the one which has **more interaction with matter,** as
**photons with low energy** are **more absorbed** than those with high energy.

   - In contrast, in the energy range of the **Compton effect** there is
no clear dependence on Z or on the energy of the photon. But
because of the nature of the process ( _deviating photons),_ there is
**scattering** of the beams. **X-rays in this range** are **not good**
**candidates for medical imaging,** as the scattering of the beam
results in a **blurred image.**
It’s resolved and avoided by placing a led grid to stop the scatter
radiation, recovering a much clearer image.

*[PDF p. 17]*

**Both** photoelectric and Compton scatter **contribute to the total attenuation of a beam** as
it passes through material. The relative contribution of photoelectric and Compton
interactions depends on a few factors:

- As the **x-ray photon energy increases:**

   Significant decrease in photoelectric interactions; **Compton**
**scatter** becomes the **predominant** cause of attenuation at
**higher energies.**
   - There is a reduction in the total attenuation _(more photons are_
_transmitted through the material)._

- As the **atomic number increases:**

   There is no change in Compton interactions.

**-**
**Many more photoelectric interactions.**

   Greater attenuation of the x-ray photons _(less penetration)._

- As the tissue mass **density increases:**

   - There is an **increase** in both **Compton and photoelectric**

**interactions.**

   - **Greater attenuation** of the x-ray photons _(less penetration)._

2. CHARGED PARTICLES

When charged particles penetrate a material, they find themselves surrounded by other
particles that are also charged. Therefore, they experience **Coulomb-forces.**
A charged particle that interacts with matter can **lose energy through 3 process:**

1. Emitting photons: on interacting with other charged particles, the trajectory changes
_(the particle is deviated)_ and photons are emitted. _Bremsstrahlung_ interactions.
2. Collisions with electrons, knocking them out of their orbit.

3. Collisions with atomic nuclei.

An **electron** is very **small** and has **very little mass**
_(light particle)_ **,** and so it is very probable that in these
interactions it will **deviate** appreciably from its
trajectory, while the trajectory of protons or alpha
particles _(heavy particles)_ with which it collides will
hardly be modified.
The penetration length in the case of interaction with
charged particles is known as **range.** It represents the
distance that the charged particles can travel.

The result in both cases _(electrons and protons)_ is that each of the incident particles will
generate a **cascade of charged particles and photons** around the trajectory that the initial
particle has followed.

*[PDF p. 18]*

- **In the case of electrons** : **all three types of process occur** _(the most likely ones_
_being bremsstrahlung and electron-collision)._ As soon as an electron enters into
matter, it is affected by other particles and will interact with them, **deviating from its**
**trajectory** and releasing part of its energy to generate **photons and new electrons.**
The photons and new electrons will continue interacting, deviating and in turn giving
up more energy to generate even more photons and electrons until the remaining
photons and electrons can no longer ionize more atoms. Finally, the remaining
energy will be deposited as heat. We observe a whole **cascade of electrons.**
The energy is deposited in a more or less **droplet-shaped volume** around the initial
trajectory of the electron: the **energy is dispersed.**

**-** **In the case of protons:** the most common interaction is **Coulomb interaction** with
electrons. The procedure is similar in the case of protons, but in this case the **energy**
is **deposited along the length of the initial trajectory.** Protons do not deviate from
the initial trajectory and **do not disperse** in the same way.

The **absorption of charged particles** is **NOT exponential.** There is a maximum distance
where we can expect to find an interaction of these electrons and protons with matter.

   - **In the case of electrons:** the energy deposited by a
beam of electrons decreases rapidly with the depth

traveled.

   - **In the case of protons and alpha particles:** the
**maximum energy absorbed** occurs at a given depth
and then drops off rapidly to zero. The peak is referred
to as the _**Bragg peak.**_

**DOSIMETRY. BEHAVIOUR OF THE ABSORBED ENERGY INSIDE THE CELL**

The **Absorbed Dose** is the parameter that defines the **effect of radiation** on a given
medium. It’s calculated as the **absorbed energy per unit mass.**

The corresponding **unit for Dose** is **Joule/Kg = Gray** _**(Gy)**_

However, the dose doesn’t measure the biological effects on the patient, which depends on
many things such as the type of particle, tissue in which the dose is deposited,..
The first thing to take into account is the type of effects that we can expect to find in an
organism subjected to ionizing radiation: **Stochastic effects and Deterministic effects.**

*[PDF p. 19]*

- **Stochastic e�ects:** a stochastic effect is a random effect that appears for **lower doses**

of radiation, where an **increase of dose** brings about an **increase in the probability**
**of the effect occurring,** but **not** an increase **in the seriousness** of the effect. The
seriousness of the effect will be independent of the dose.
This is the case of a DNA mutation induced by radiation. Stochastic effects are
**purely probabilistic,** and have no threshold.

   - **Deterministic e�ects:** a deterministic effect is that in which the **seriousness**

**increases with the number of incident particles.** An example is _erythema_, which
can appear on the skin with radiotherapy treatment, or sun burns. Deterministic
effects appear for **higher doses.** Increasing the dose increases the seriousness of
the possible effects.

As said, the absorbed dose considers **only the physics** of the process, and is measured in
Grays. Therefore, **Grays don’t take into account the biological effects.**

To measure the **biological effect,** we use:

   **Equivalent dose (H)** _takes into account the relative damage that each type of_
_radiation can cause compared with that of a beam of photons that deposits the same_
_amount of energy. W_ _R_ _is a weighting factor that has no units and that depends on_
_each type of radiation._

   - **Effective dose (E)** _(takes into account that different tissues react to radiation. W_ _T_ _is_
_a weighting factor that is different for different tissues)_ **.**

The units for both equivalent and effective dose are **Sievert [H] = Sv.**

*[PDF p. 20]*

#### Conventional X-ray

_Radiology workshop I_

(Bibliography: “Learning radiology” (W. Herring); Webpage: Radiopaedia)

Radiology is a medical specialty, which deals with generating images of the inside of the
body employing different techniques. These images are read and interpreted by radiologists,
for diagnosis and treatment of disease _(although to a lesser extent)_

**History**

X-rays were discovered by **W.C. Roentgen in 1895,** for which he received a
nobel prize.
On the 8th of November, 1895, his wife put her hand under the machine he
was working on, revealing the first X-ray _**(Bertha’s hand).**_ It was the first
time a part of a human body was able to be seen through imaging.

Since then, radiology has hugely evolved, and we can now evaluate bone,
vasculature, organs,.. through many imaging techniques. We can see what
is going on inside a patient **non-invasively.**

**Generation of X-rays**

For conventional X-ray, we use an X-ray tube to
form the rays.

   - **Phase 1: Generation of electrons:** after generating an induced current in the X-ray
tube, we can accelerate electrons by using a differential potential.
   - **Phase 2: Displacement of electrons:** The cathode emits electrons into the vacuum
and the anode collects them, establishing a flow of electrical current through the tube
(known as beam). A high differential potential will accelerate the electrons, which

collide with the anode material.

   **Phase 3: Generation of photons:** the photons generated by the impact of electrons
on the anode will produce the **x-ray beam.** They escape through a small window
_(used to cause a unique direction)_ and impact on the area of interest, in this case, the
human body. The rest of the X-ray beam which doesn’t escape through the window is
absorbed by the metal housing surrounding the device.

*[PDF p. 21]*

**Conventional X-ray**

Area of radiology which uses X-rays to obtain images through a
flat digital detector.

X-rays go through the patient and are detected by a **flat digital**
**detector.** The energy which manages to go through the patient is
**quantified** by the detector, which **translates** the photons into an
electrical signal, and from there, into a **digital image.**

**Radiographic densities. Grayscale**

The **grayscale** emerges according to the amount of radiation detected. It’s inversely
proportional to the radiation absorbed by the material which is irradiated. This **differential**
**absorption allows us to produce an image.**

   - Structures which **retain the x-ray** will show as **white** (bone).
   - Structures and spaces which **don’t retain X-rays** are seen as **darker/black.**

Grayscale _(according to the amount of radiation detected):_

There are **4 densities** _(or 5, if we consider metal)_ considering
the scale of grey. Therefore, bones appear as white, while
cavities are seen as dark spaces.
The detectability of a lesion on the radiography will be based
on the contrast it generates with respect to the surrounding

tissue.

**CHEST X-RAY**

   There is a dedicated system for chest x-rays

   Usually taken from the back to the front **(posterior-anterior, PA).**
_Why is this done? To avoid magnification effect: the beam of electrons is like a fan. If_
_the person is very close to the source, the structures will be magnified when_
_projected over the detector. By doing its posterior-anterior position, the heart is_
_further from the source and therefore the heart is seen without enlargement,_
_maintaining its normal size. By doing PA, we_ _**avoid magnification of the cardiac**_

_**silhouette.**_

   - Distance is fixed: **150-180 cm**, to avoid mistakes when reading the image

   High kV

   - Low dose

   - **PA** (posterior anterior view) **and lateral views** can be

taken

*[PDF p. 22]*

**Anatomy of a chest x-ray**

   - In the centre, we can observe the **heart** . ⅓ of the heart is on the right, and ⅔ on the

left.

   On the sides of the heart, we can observe the **lungs**, which are seen in black as they

contain air.

   - The **ribs** are arched and curved from the back to the front. Therefore the anterior ribs

can be seen as more curved.

   - In the lower part we would find the **diaphragm** .
   - On the right hand side, the **liver**, and the **stomach** can be seen on the left _(seen_
_darker as it contains a cavity, the gastric chamber)._

**Importance of PA view and lateral view**

A mass is found in the mid-chest, specifically in
the mid portion of the right lung. But through the
lateral view we can additionally observe that it’s

found at the back. Therefore the mass is in the

back of the right lower lobe.
This shows the **importance of obtaining more**
**than one perspective.**

**Strategies to assess the quality of the x-ray**

**- Inspiration:** depending on the inspiration, the lungs and diaphragm
will be observed in very different ways. The x-ray has to be taken
while the **patient is taking a deep breath in.** This is assessed
through the **rib-diaphragm relationship** (6th ant rib-10th posterior).

**- Alignment:** to see that the person is straight, and not oblique, we
observe that the distance from the clavicles to spinous processes is

the same

**- Radiation:** adaptation to the thickness of the patient and evaluation
of grey areas. Higher thickness will imply higher attenuation.

*[PDF p. 23]*

**Portable X-ray systems**

Used when the patient is injured or recently operated, and has to stay in
bed. The **detectors** are put **behind** the **patient’s back,** and the picture
is taken in an **anterior-posterior view.**

   Lower X-ray quality

   - AP view

   Supine position

   Less inspiration is carried out

**ABDOMINAL X-RAY**

   - Distance: 110 cm

   - Normal kV

   Mid-high radiation dose needed

   Supine position (laying down)

This x-ray is more difficult to read as x-ray **doesn’t**

**differentiate the different soft tissues.**

However, they are very useful for assessing patients
with abdominal pain.

**Supine and orthostatic images**

Air always goes up. Therefore, in **supine**
**position** everything is **equally distributed.**
But in the **up-right position** the upper region
of cavities are seen as darker, as **air floats**

**towards the top.** For patients with
abdominal pain we take x-rays in both
positions.

*[PDF p. 24]*

**BONE X-RAY**

  - Skull radiology

        General system

        - PA and lateral view

        - Distance 110 cm

        - Normal kV

        - **Limited use:** generally employed in mild
trauma, sinupathy and dental diseases

  - Spine radiology

        General or dedicated system

        - Distance: 300 cm

        - Normal kV

        Mid-high radiation dose

        Projections: AP, lateral, oblique,...

        Can be performed of the **whole spine or**

**sections**

  - Limb radiology. Lower limbs

        General or dedicated system

        - Distance: 300 cm

        - Normal kV

        Mid-high radiation dose
        - Usually performed for **measurements** .
Indication: dysmetria or allignment loss

        - AP view

  - Limb radiology. Wrist and hand

        AP and oblique views + lateral view

        General equipment

        - Distance: 110 cm

        - Normal kV

        - Low radiation dose

  - Shoulder radiology

        Projections: AP, axial, lateral
scapula shoulder **(“Y” view)**,

transthoracic.

  - Radiology of the pelvis

        General equipment

        - Distance: 110 cm

        - Normal kV

        Mid-high radiation dose

        Pelvis: AP and oblique view; Hips: AP and axial view
The lateral projection isn’t always possible

*[PDF p. 25]*

**BREAST X-RAYS**

A **mammography** is an x-ray taken of the breast. Screening makes it able to detect early

tumors.

   There are dedicated breast systems

   - Low dose of radiation

   - Low kV

   High resolution for soft tissues

   - AP and lateral views

   - Distance: 30 cm

**CONTRAST**

Contrast can be used to further study regions such as the **digestive tract, nephro-urinary**
**system, female genital tract or any conduit in general.**
We can either create **positive contrast** _(by introducing barium or iodine)_ or **negative**
**contrast** _(air)._

   Urography: intravenous iodinated contrast is injected and
excreted by the kidneys
   - Bowel study: Double-contrast barium
   - Gastric study: oral barium contrast

**Interventional radiology**

X-ray based technique in which we subtract the contrast from other structures to focus on

the structure of interest.

In interventional radiology, X-rays can be used to observe
the **trajectory of a catheter.** It helps advance the catheter
to the area of interest, where it will inflate and open a
blocked vessel or insert a stent to prevent the vessel from
collapsing.
The catheter is inserted following the **Seldinger technique**
_(insert needle, insert guidewire, remove the needle, insert a_
_catheter, remove guidewire and inject contrast)_

**Digital subtraction technique**

*[PDF p. 26]*

**KEY CONCEPTS. kV and mAs**

**-** **kV: Kilovolts** = measures the amount of differential voltage present in the x-ray tube.
Differential voltage is what will accelerate electrons going from the cathode to the
anode. Therefore: ↑kV = ↑differential potential = ↑kinetic energy (velocity) = ↑ energy
electron that are going to transmit their energy to the anode = ↑ energy photons
In conclusion, **increasing kV increases the energy of the x-ray beam photons.**
It also affects intensity, but the main effect we focus on for kV is the effect on energy.
It’s the **only variable which will affect the energy of the beam.**

**-**
**mAs: milliampere second** = measure of radiation produced (milliampere) over a set
amount of time (seconds) via an x-ray tube, at a given kV.
mA = tube current; mAs = radiation released per second.
**Increasing mAs means an increase in intensity but NOT in energy**
An **increase in tube current (mA)** results in a **higher production of electrons** that
are inside the x-ray tube which will, therefore, **increase the quantity of x-radiation.**
We release more photons, but they have the same energy as kV remains
unchanged.

↑ kV = increase in x-ray photons energy
↑ mA = increase in current = increase in the number of e- released = higher intensity beam
(more photons), but NOT higher energy

In relation to contrast and noise (image quality):
↑ kV = decrease in contrast (structures will appear darker), but more penetration
↓ kV = higher contrast (structures will appear whiter), more differential absorption
↑ mA = decrease in noise, better quality

_Image 1: kV too low, too much contrast between tissues and little penetration_
_Image 2: kV is increased to improve penetration. But now kV is too high (high photon energy,_
_little absorption and much penetration. Photons with low energy are more absorbed)_
_Image 3: modify mAs to correct image density. Reduces amount of radiation_
_(milliamperes/second ↓) and corrects image quality._

*[PDF p. 27]*

#### Computed tomography (CT)

_Radiology workshop II_

_Lesion in back of right lower lung:_
Issues with X-ray can be resolved by using CT scans. It’s
the imaging modality of choice **to detect cancer and**
**neoplasms,** to **asses cancer development,...** but also
when you suspect patient with **pneumonia**, with **abdominal**
**infection,** to **asses the heart,** ... 80% of CTs are used for
cancer patients but it can be used for many other purposes.
It’s a very **fast imaging technique,** very **available** and **not**
**expensive** .
The big problem with CT is the **amount of radiation.**

HISTORY

CT scanners were first introduced in 1971 by **Godfrey N. Hounsfield** . The objective was to
create a three dimensional image of an object, taking multiple measurements of it with
X-rays from different angles and using a computer to reconstruct the image from hundreds of
overlapping, intersecting planes.
Initially, to obtain 1 slice it took 7 minutes. Nowadays, 1 slice can be acquired in 0.3
seconds, and reconstructed structures in 5 seconds.

PRINCIPLES OF CT

CT is a diagnostic imaging procedure that uses **X-rays** to acquire **cross-sectional**
**“slices”** of the body. These cross-sections are reconstructed to recreate a **3D**
**image** of the studied object. As it’s mainly acquired in an axial plane, it’s also known

as CAT scan.

The CT X-ray tube emits a **beam of**
**X-rays/photons** that goes through the patient
and passes through detectors which record the
intensity of attenuated radiation and convert it
into an electrical signal. This is processed on a
computer where the signal is transformed into a
**grayscale image** in which **brightness is**
**proportional to the density** _(in UH)._

As we are taking images from different angles, the tube is **rotating** around the patient where
**beam and detector move in synchrony.**

The slice is made up of small **volumetric elements** that represent
attenuation, called **voxels (3D).** When these elements are placed on
a plane **(2D)**, they become **pixels.**

*[PDF p. 28]*

The units used to measure the density within the pixel are the **Hounsfield units,** which
range from values of **-1000 to +3000.** Each pixel is assigned a shade of grey based on its

attenuation value.

However, the **human eye** can distinguish **only 30-40 gray values.** Therefore, the target
range of HU needs to be adapted to our eye by establishing
specific **windows.**
In conclusion, CT allows us to detect where things are, and
characterize them according to density by using Hounsfield

units.

Grayscale:

Black structures _(- dense)_ and white structures _(+ dense)._
The densities are determined within the gray scale. The **0 value** corresponds to **water,** and
**-1000 to air** . Dense structures appear white (such as bone), whereas air and fat appear
black or grey.

When we look at the picture, we can ask the machine to reconstruct the image by either
using a **soft tissue window or a lung window.** We normally order 2 CT scans with two
centered areas of interest: lung and soft tissue. Each window allows us to differentiate
specific structures _(same image acquisition, but different image reconstruction afterwards)._

   - **Soft tissue windows** allow us to observe and assess soft tissue

_(bone=white; muscle=gray; fat= darker gray)._ Soft tissues can be
observed nicely, but lungs are seen as completely black. We cannot
assess air or lung parenchyma. This reconstruction can be used to
evaluate the **chest and abdomen**, but not the lungs.

   - **Lung window** can be used to assess the **lung parenchyma.** We
can clearly observe the inside of the lungs, but we cannot

differentiate other structures. In CT we can also observe metal

density, by using iodine contrast injected through the vein, to look at
blood vessels and lung parenchyma.

CT SYSTEMS

There are different CT acquisition modes/systems:

**Sequential**

**Multislice/multidetector system** _(by placing_
_multiple detectors)_ : rotates while the patient
moves at the same time, with 4 detectors placed
within the machine. The bid advantage is that it
allows us to acquire images faster as it covers

more surface in less time.

**Spiral, helical:** rotation and
movement of the patient

occur at the same time

**Dual-source:** 2 tubes are

found emitting radiation.

Fastest CT on the market.

Specially good for
observing and assessing

the heart.

*[PDF p. 29]*

**Dual source imaging,** also known as **spectral imaging,** allows us to
differentiate the structures depending on x-ray uptake. It can be done using
contrast or not. It uses two separate x-ray photon energy spectra, allowing
the interrogation of materials that have different attenuation properties at
different energies .It allows to differentiate, for example, urinary stones
depending on energy uptake. We can tell if the stone has to be removed, or if
it can be dissolved with medication and no need for invasive surgery.

IMAGE QUALITY

CTs can be observed from the 3 different plains and perspectives _(coronal, sagittal, axial)_ .
Axial images are usually combined with other perspectives to assess diagnosis.
Images are obtained in the axial plain but they can be processed to observe other
perspectives.

**Factors that affect image quality**

   - Radiation

   Spatial resolution

   - Noise

   - Artifacts

**Concepts**

  - **Kilovoltaje (kV)**

Represents **photon energy.**
It reflects the energy in our image based on penetration capacity.

   - **More kV** = more radiation. By increasing kV, more energy is used to obtain high
quality imaging
   - **Less kV** = less radiation. Structures absorb radiation but we get image noise _(grainy)_

  - **Miliampere seconds (mAs)**

Represents the **amount of photons** released per second **(intensity).**

**-** **More mAs** = more radiation, but implies there is less noise

The throwback of using **high Kv and mAs** is the **high radiation dose.**

**Factors affecting image quality**

**1.** **Spatial resolution**

The ability to obtain small details with high contrast. It’s
influenced by system geometry, and determines image

definition.

*[PDF p. 30]*

Factors influencing spatial resolution:

➢ **Slice thickness:** the thinner the image, the

more anatomical imaging _(high spatial_
_resolution),_ but higher noise. Therefore, the
thinner the slices, the better quality in terms of
anatomical detail, but this is accompanied by

increased noise.

To evaluate something **small and specific** = thinner slices ( **0.6 mm)**
To evaluate more **big and general** structures = 1-2 mm slices

➢ **Pitch:** if a patient goes through the CT machine too fast, the detail can

decrease. Therefore, the choice of pitch affects both image quality and

radiation dose.

**P = 1.0,** x-ray beams are contiguous for adjacent

rotations

**P>1.0,** x-ray beams are not contiguous for adjacent
rotations (gaps in the x-ray helix)
**P<1.0,** x-ray beams overlap _(a volume of tissue is_
_irradiated more than once per scan)_

If we want detailed acquisition, the patient must go through very slowly; If we
want fast acquisition _(emergency),_ we have to sacrifice some of the detail.

➢ **Resolution** : reconstruction filters / reconstruction algorithms to increase

resolution

**2.** **Noise** _(ALARA principle)_

Factors influencing noise

➢ **Slice thickness and reconstruction filters**

➢ **Body size:** the noise level doubles every 8cm of increase in the patient’s

diameter.

➢ **kV and mAs:** noise can be modified by increasing or decreasing radiation.

          - The **higher the radiation** dose, the **less noise** and better image
quality _(when dose is doubled, the noise is reduced to a factor of ¼ )_
          - The **lower the radiation** dose, the **higher the noise.**

*[PDF p. 31]*

**3.** **Artifacts:** can interfere with X-ray absorption and affect image quality.

**- Motion, breathing,...** In this image, the data needed to
reconstruct each image has changed position due to patient

movement.

**- Metals:** the presence of metals such as gold produces
streak artifacts, as metals absorb radiation almost completely.

RADIATION DOSE

The radiation dose has to be **carefully evaluated.** The younger the patient, the more
susceptible to amounts of radiation.

We need to use the least amount of radiation possible. This is
done by following the **ALARA principle,** taking into account Kv

and mAs.

The **effects of radiation** the patient receives depend on:

   - Absorbed radiation _(determined by kV, mAs and pitch)_
   - The anatomical region that has been examined _(effective dose):_ each organ has a
characteristic factor, which indicates the probability of malignant radiation effects.

POST PROCESSING

**●** **Multiplanar reconstruction (MPR)**

  - **MIP and MinIP** _(max and min intensity projection)_

Only the pixels of maximum or minimum intensity in the
reconstructed thickness are shown in the image.
**MIP** is useful for evaluating **vessels**, and **minIP** to
evaluate **lung parenchyma and airway.**

**●** **Volume rendering technique**

It can be applied to obtain **images in**
**colour** and evaluate airways, organs in
the abdomen, bones,...

  - **Spectral imaging** can also be used to quantify more accurately the different

structures.

**●** **Cinematic rendering**

*[PDF p. 32]*



*[PDF p. 33]*

#### Radiobiology and contrast media

**INTRODUCTION**

Radiological examinations present bones as white _(as opposed to US, where they are_
_black)._ They can only distinguish **four basic densities:**

   - Air

   - Fat

   - Water

   - Calcium

Organs of different attenuation can be easily distinguished. However, **structures of similar**
**density cannot be distinguished. Contrast media** makes it possible to **establish**
**differences** between neighboring structures and **enhance** and differentiate organs.

**Indications for contrast media**

**●** **Lesion detection and characterization:** characterize cancer and lesions (malignant
or benign), depending on **how much contrast is taken up.** If it’s malignant, it will
clearly light up as it’s very vascularized and metastasizing, and therefore takes up
much of the injected contrast.

  - **Tumor staging** : It can serve to detect **metastasis** of the tumor all over the body, and
determine the stage in which it’s found.

  - **Response to treatment:** we can also detect if **chemotherapy** has worked, if the
tumor is dead and no longer takes up contrast.

  - **Evaluation of vascularization:** We can also use contrast to **enhance** specific
structures, mainly **vessels and vascular structures** _(detect pulmonary embolism,_
_rupture,...)_

**●** **Special studies** _(urography, arthrography, perfusion,...)._ 90% of studies are done
with contrast injection.

**CONTRAST MEDIA FEATURES**

   - They must be **safe** and **completely eliminated** from the body after
injection
   - Contrast media **take advantage of the absorption, transport and**
**elimination pathways** of physiological substances to be distributed
throughout the body to **produce the image**
   - The **duration** of a contrast medium in the body should be
**proportional to the type of study** and examination to be performed
   - Contrast media can be **positive** (they provide a hyperdense image
with respect to the adjacent tissue) **or negative** (they provide a
lower density image)

**HOW is CONTRAST MEDIA ADMINISTERED?**

**Depending on what we need to study,** we will need to travel faster or slower. Each product
should be administered as approved.

   - **Intravenous** : For **fast distribution** throughout the body. It’s injected through a vein
and travels throughout the body. Can also be introduced in other ways to observe
specific structures.

*[PDF p. 34]*

**-** **Intra-arterial**

   - **Oral:** enhances stomach, bowel, rectum,...

**-** **Intra-articular**

**CONTRAST MEDIA IN RADIOLOGY**

Used in **X-ray and CT,** and in **Ultrasound** and **MRIs** to a lesser extent.
Using radiographic techniques, we only have 4 densities. Therefore we use contrast.

RADIOGRAPHIC CONTRAST AGENTS _(CT scans and X-ray)_

Contrast can be either **positive or negative:**

  - **Positive contrasts:**

        - They are **metals**

        - Classification:

       - **iodinated** (water-soluble or oil based)

       - **Non iodinated.** For ex, **barium sulfate**

        They are **opaque to X-rays,** thus causing increased
attenuation in radiological examinations: enhance the image

and are **seen as white**

  - **Negative contrasts:**

        - There are usually **inert gasses** _(such as air, CO2,...)_ which
**don’t have x-ray absorption** and are **seen as black.**
        - These neg contrasts (ex: **air** ) are used for **digestive**

**examinations**

        Why? Because they allow the walls of the digestive tract to
distend, and separate the folds of mucous membranes to

detect alterations.

**Barium sulfate → conventional X-ray**

**Water-soluble iodine → CT**

➢ Barium sulfate. X-rays

        - It is used in examinations of the **digestive tract**

        It is a salt that is not absorbed and does not alter the physiology of

intestinal transit

        - It is administered in the form of a **porridge or suspension**
        - Administered **orally or rectally,** depending on which segment of the

GI tract is to be studied

        - It is **mixed** with **water and flocculants** until it has an optimal
viscosity to be distributed through the mucosa, to be able to detect

wall lesions.

➢ Water-soluble iodinated agents. CT

_In this image, we are using water-soluble iodinated radicals in CT,_
_which allow us to see the aorta and the liver, along with structures_
_within the liver: we can detect nodules (metastasis) within the liver and_

_assess liver size._

*[PDF p. 35]*

**Iodine properties**

   - The basis of the molecular structure is a **benzene ring,** to which up to **3**

**iodine atoms** can be attached

   - They make the molecule **opaque** = it increases the structure’s attenuation =
**whiter** _(as a reminder: attenuation is the reduction of the intensity of an x-ray_
_beam as it traverses matter. The reduction is caused by absorption)_

   The higher the iodine, higher opacity = higher the enhancement of the tissue.
   - Compounds attached to **C1, C3 and C5** of the contrast molecule **modify its**
**properties** such as solubility, osmolarity and viscosity
   - Its **absorption** occurs in **seconds** .

   Then, it will be excreted. Depending on the radicals found in position R5, it will be
excreted in one way or another:

    - **Organic compound** = through the **kidneys**

    - **H atom** = through **bile**
   - Iodine can be **cleared through the kidneys.** If they are not working
properly, the contrast will remain within the body, and this can be
detected on the CT. If we wait some time, we can assess how the

kidneys are excreting the contrast.

**Pharmacokinetics of iodinated contrast media**

   - **Injected intravenously,** they bind to plasma **albumin** and are **distributed**
throughout the body following the circulatory system

   They reach the **peak of plasma concentration within seconds** of their injection

   Contrast media diffuse into the extravascular space, reaching the interstitial space
through the pores of the endothelium
   - They **do not enter cells (except hepatocytes)** and have a **2-hour half life**
   - **Elimination** : as we mentioned, will occur though one secretion or another depending

on radical 5

    - Unchanged via the **kidney** (by glomerular filtration). At 24 h after
administration, 90% of the administered dose has been eliminated

    - To a lesser extent **biliary excretion**, which increases if there is renal failure

**Serves to:**

   - Observe **breast secretions**

   - Directly assess the **vasculature of an organ** (angiography) **:** we can
insert a catheter and **directly** inject contrast in the **area of interest** (in
liver, brain,..)
   - It can be injected to see the **distribution**

ULTRASOUND CONTRAST AGENTS

   - The agents used are substances based on **gas microbubbles,** which **interact with**

**US waves**

   - Products:

    - Sulfur hexafluoride - the most widely used

    - Octafluoropropane

   - The **differentiation** between the source of echoes from the **bubbles**

and the source of echoes from the **tissues** is the basis of the contrast

image. This requires **specific software**
   - Mainly employed in **cardiac assessment.** Contrast is used to better
see the cavity of the heart and the heart walls.

*[PDF p. 36]*

MRI CONTRAST AGENTS

**Classification of contrast media in MRI**

  - Effect on signal intensity

        - **Positive** (increase it)

        Negative (decrease it)

  - Behavior in the magnetic field

        **Paramagnetic: Gadolinium (Gd),** Manganese (Mn)

        Superparamagnetic: iron oxide

  - Mechanism and biodistribution

**-** **Extracellular: Gd chelates**

        Intracellular: mangafodipir trisodium (Mn-DPDP)

        Mixed distribution: gadoxetate disodium(Gd-EOB-DTPA), gadobenate
dimeglumine (Gd-BOPTA)

        Intravascular: gadofosveset trisodium

**Contrast in MRI. Gadolinium (Gd)**
Similar to iodine in CT. We use **gadolinium** to see **contrast uptake** by structures in **MRI** .
The MRI system is a big magnet, and when we introduce gadolinium, it **alters the magnetic**
**field.** Gadolinium itself is a **toxic metal,** so it has to be bound to molecules to reduce its
toxicity (chelators).

   - **Free gadolinium** in plasma is **toxic** . It is **necessary to bind it** to other molecules
**(chelators)** to reduce its toxicity

   It causes signal **intensity increase in T1-weighted sequences** and signal intensity
loss in T2-weighted sequences

   The difference between the molecules of different gadolinium chelates lies in:

    - Its **structure** _(determines chemical stability and ability to release Gd ions)_

    - **Charge** _(Ionic: improves the stability of the molecule; Non ionic)_

**ADVERSE REACTIONS TO CONTRAST MEDIA**

They are **very safe** drugs, but **can have adverse reactions** due to the person’s immune
system. These reactions are **usually mild,** but in some cases may be severe.
**Prevalence of adverse reactions:** up to **2.5 %**

**The risk to Gd contrast agents is significantly lower than for iodinated contrast agents**

Mechanisms of adverse reactions

  - **Primary - allergic or anaphylactoid reactions:**

**-**
**Unpredictable**
        - By the action of contrast on **cells of the immune system** that release
mediators such as **histamine and immunoglobulins**

        **Not dependent on the dose.** Just a drop can cause an allergic reaction _(mild_
_or severe consequences, such as cardiac and resp arrest and convulsions)_

        **Not dependent on iodine concentration.** Management is complex

**●** **Secondary - Chemotoxic**

        - Can be **predictable**, and effects can be minimized
        - Due to direct cellular toxicity. By action of the contrast on **cells of the**
**organism,** for example, the kidney

**-**
**Dose dependent**

*[PDF p. 37]*

**-**
**Nephrotoxicity:** the contrast is cleared through the kidneys, so if we inject
**too much** contrast, it **can damage the kidneys (nephrotoxic).**

If a person has poor kidney function, the patient could suffer from **acute renal failure.**
Contrast agents **should not be used in patients with a history of kidney dysfunction.**
**Oral administration can be done** as the contrast will not be absorbed and pass through the
body.

**RADIOBIOLOGY**

**Ionizing radiation** is a type of radiation with high enough energy to ionize matter, extracting
electrons. Can come from natural sources or be created artificially (X-rays).
**Radiobiology** studies the **interaction** of this radiation with matter **and its effects.**

Radiological magnitudes and units

   Traditional system: röntgen, rad, rem
   - International system units: **Coulomb/kg, Gray (Gy), Sievert (Sv).** International
agreement, created with the purpose of structuring a system as universal and as
suitable as possible for the different measurements

_Absorbed dose: measured in in Gray (Gy)_
_Equivalent dose: Sieverts (Sv)_

_Effective dose: measured in Sv or mSv._

Natural background radiation

It varies depending on the area we live in. We will receive more or less radiation. The
average person in the US receives an effective dose of **3 mSv per year from natural**

**radiation.**

Artificial radiation

**20% of the total radiation we receive comes from artificial radiation**

(radiological examinations, nuclear escapes,...). Nowadays, the **most important**
**source of artificial radiation** (produced by humans) is **medical diagnostic**
**equipment.** Each radiological imaging technique requires more or less radiation

dose.

*[PDF p. 38]*

Since being discovered, it has been observed that **X-rays (ionizing radiation)**
can produce **harmful effects on health.** Ionizing radiation has many beneficial
applications, but can have harmful effects.

Biological e�ects of radiation

Biological effects derive from radiation **damage to DNA.** DNA contains all the information for
growth and control, and is transmitted from cell to cell during replication. Damage to it will

therefore also be transmitted.

Radiation can **directly affect DNA,** inciding on it and breaking it, **or indirectly.**

**-** **Direct:** effect on **DNA molecules** (target molecule)
**-** **Indirect:** effect on other molecules that form **free radicals** (ionization of H 2 O=HO+H)
The **main action of radiation on the human body** (80% composed of water) is considered

to be **indirect** .

**Effects according to the affected cells:**

   - Somatic

   Hereditary (coming from damage to reproductive cells)

**Effects according to the cause-effect relationship:**

   - Stochastic (probabilistic)

   - Deterministic

Deterministic e�ects

   They are produced by the death of
many cells in a tissue or organ.
   - The **severity** of the effect

**increases with radiation dose.**

   - There is a **threshold dose** for the

effect to occur

   - Deterministic effects occur after

exposure to **relatively high doses**

**of radiation.**

**Acute radiation syndrome**
Periods of the syndrome: prodromal (nausea, vomiting, diarrhea) → Latency (days-weeks)

→ Overt disease

Stochastic e�ects

   - Consequence of **sublethal damage** (chromosomal mutation) **in one / few cells:**

        - Germ cells: inherited genetic disorders
        - Somatic cells: development of a cancer
   - The **probability** of their occurrence, but not the severity, **increases with dose**

   **Severity doesn’t depend on the dose.** Just one disruption of DNA is inherited by
dividing cells and the damage propagates. Therefore, **children are more prone** to
**stochastic effects,** as their cells are continuously proliferating and growing. Tumor
arises. _Theoretically, a child with cancer who has to be diagnosed through CT and_
_radiological systems, after the initial cancer disappears, can then manifest cancer in_
_adulthood due to this stochastic effect (mutations produced and accumulated during_
_rutinary cancer check ups during the childhood cancer)._

*[PDF p. 39]*

- There is **no threshold** for stochastic effects. Can occur by just one disruption
- Stochastic effects occur after **exposure to moderate-low doses.**
- The **main somatic stochastic effect** induced by ionizing radiation is the **increase in**

**the incidence of cancer**

Special caution should be taken in children and women at child-bearing age.

*[PDF p. 40]*

#### Nuclear medicine. PET I

_“Nuclear medicine is a medical imaging specialty that uses small amounts of radioactive_
_material (_ _**radiopharmaceutical**_ _) to_ _**diagnose**_ _or to_ _**treat**_ _many diseases”_

Radiology is destined to detect changes in size and anatomy;
Nuclear medicine focuses on metabolic changes and receptors,

etc.

It allows detection but has the disadvantage of being less
accurate. Therefore, it has to be complemented with other tests,
for example a CT scan, to observe the anatomical image.
**PET and CT** are **fused** to clearly define and **precisely localize**

**the abnormal structure.**

RADIOPHARMACEUTICAL

The main difference with other techniques is that, in nuclear
medicine, we are using a **drug and an isotope to create an**
**image.** The **drug** determines the **biological behaviour**
_(targets the site),_ and the **radioisotope** determines the
**physical characteristics** and allows the detection of the drug
_(acts as a flashlight)_ . We detect the emission of radioactivity.

  - _**Radioisotopes:**_ Isotopes can be **stable, unstable or**
**radioisotopes:** their nuclei emit energy in the form of ionizing radiation while
searching for a **more stable configuration.**

SOURCE OF RADIATION

In **radiology (X-rays),** radiation has its origin in the **atomic shell**
due to the deceleration of high velocity **electrons** when they **collide**
**and interact** with target material.

In **nuclear medicine**, the radiation comes from **radioactivity,** from
the **disintegration of the nucleus** accompanied by the **emission**
**of radiation,** as opposed to X-rays.

KINDS OF IONIZING RADIATION

   Alpha radiation

   - Beta radiation

   - Gamma radiation

*[PDF p. 41]*

_If it were necessary to detect a spinal lesion, we would select a radiopharmaceutical which_
_emits gamma, due to its greater penetration._
Any high amount of these radiations can be potentially dangerous for the patient, causing
irreversible DNA damage. Alpha-radiation can be stopped by paper sheets, beta by
aluminium foil and gamma by lead.
We need to use an emission strong enough to penetrate the structures of interest.

**Radiopharmaceuticals** are used in 2 situations: **treatment and**
**diagnosis.**

   **Therapy:** alpha and beta 

   **Diagnosis:** gamma or positron-emission (beta +).
Gammacamera or SPECT are both gamma emitters.

We can find 3 different types of equipment in nuclear medicine:

   **GammaCamera (scintigraphy)**

**-** **SPECT or SPECT/CT**

**-** **PET or PET/CT**

GammaCamera and SPECT/CT are both based on

gamma emission; PET is based on positron emission.

GAMMACAMERA (SCINTIGRAPHY)

We perform a **planar study,** which is enough to observe any
pathological uptake. To localize it better, we have to use **SPECT**
**study,** which observes the patient's 3 axes.

_Example: we see a spot on the vertebrae. But depending on the_
_location within the vertebrae, it will have one interpretation or_
_another. Metastatic or benign. To locate it, we use SPECT or_
_SPECT/CT (which allows the fusion of both images) to define and_
_localize the uptake._

Usually, **first** we do a **planar study,** and if an
**abnormal uptake** is observed, we perform a

**SPECT/CT** to **further and better localize the**

**spot.**

*[PDF p. 42]*

- BONE SCINTIGRAPHY

Studies are usually performed to detect any osteo malignancies.
The radioisotopes used are **Diphosphonates (MDP)+Technetium** **(99m)** **.**

Diphosphonates are absorbed by the surface of the bone in proportion
to local **bone vascularization and osteoblastic activity.**
We know there is an association between uptake and bone vascularization and remodeling;
most **pathological bone conditions** _(infectious, traumatic, neoplastic…)_ are associated
with an **increase in vascularization and bone remodeling** . In this way, we will be able to
detect benign or metastatic **tumors.**

Normal distribution

There is **physiological increased uptake** in
**joints, sternum and spine** _(ex: in the_
_sacro-ileac joints, due to the continuous_
_remodeling of this area),_ but also in the
bladder and kidneys.

_Clinical context examples:_

 - THYROID SCINTIGRAPHY

For **thyroid scintigraphy** there is **no need** for a **drug;**
we only use the radioisotope, as isotopes such as **iodine**
**incorporate naturally into the thyroid gland.** This is
because iodine is involved in thyroid hormonogenesis.

**Radioiodine follows normal iodine metabolism** in the

thyroid gland.

It is introduced in the patient and used to detect:

   - **size** of the gland

**-** **localization**

   - **function** _(example: a cold node means there is reduced uptake of iodine =_
_decreased function)_

*[PDF p. 43]*

In addition, iodine has an interesting feature: it **emits**
**both gamma and beta particles.** Therefore, it can be
used for **diagnosis**, but also as **treatment.**
It can be used as **Thyroid cancer Therapy.** The **beta**
**emission can destroy tumor cells** in the metastatic
regions.

 - RENAL SCINTIGRAPHY

For renal scintigraphy, we use radiopharmaceuticals such as **Technetium-MAG3** and
**Technetium-DTPA.** We can perform dynamic studies.

In **dynamic studies,** we acquire a series of images during a time of varying duration _(from_
_seconds to mins)._ Acquired data are processed and give out **activity-time curves,** which
describe **organ uptake/excretion rates.**

_Example of left kidney (PA view) dysfunction observed on renal scintigraphy:_

We obtain images during 20-30 minutes and perform an image every 2 minutes. In the end
we are left with images which reflect the **dynamic distribution of the radioisotope,**
**depending on its uptake/elimination rates.**

 - MYOCARDIAL PERFUSION SCINTIGRAPHY

For this type of scintigraphy, we use radioisotope **Technetium-MIBI.**

**With a myocardial perfusion**, we can detect
blocked coronary arteries which supply blood to
the heart: in normal/resting conditions the heart
works fine; but while performing high activity,
the heart has to pump more blood, as well as
increase its supply. An **occlusion in coronary**
**arteries** will mean an area of the **heart stops**
**receiving irrigation during high activity.**

*[PDF p. 44]*

To perform the test, we make the patient exercise, obtain an image, and do the same in
resting position.

   - In a **normal heart,** the distribution is **homogeneous.**

   - If we see **decreased uptake of the radiopharmaceutical** in the scan corresponding
to **high activity,** it means there is a **block of the coronary artery** in a specific area.
This situation is **typical of ischemia:** an area of the heart doesn’t receive blood
supply during exercise but is seen as normal in resting position.

   - If there is **total necrosis,** there is neither normal uptake in the resting images nor in
the active ones. This situation is **typical of an infarction.**

SENTINEL NODE BIOPSY

**Sentinel node** is used in those oncological processes in which **lymph node involvement**
has **consequences on the prognosis** of the patient _(melanoma,...)._

**Sentinel node** is the **first lymph node** or group of lymph nodes **draining cancer cells.**
**Sentinel node biopsy (SNB)** is the identification, removal and analysis of the sentinel lymph
nodes of a particular tumor.

To see if the tumor has reached the sentinel node, we inject a
**radiopharmaceutical** and observe if it reaches **axillary areas** where
the sentinel node is located. The radiopharmaceutical in question is

**Technetium-Nanocolloidal albumin.**

If the **lymph node is free of tumor:**

   Good prognosis

   No lymph node involved with tumor in the rest
of the body

If the **lymph node** is found to be **involved with the**

**tumor:**

   Bad prognosis

   Is it extended enough to need a
lymphadenectomy?

*[PDF p. 45]*

#### Nuclear medicine. PET II

In nuclear medicine, there are **different types of radiation** emitted by radioisotopes.
Different applications for each:

   **Therapy:** alpha or beta - emitters

   **Diagnostic:** image created through beta + _(PET)_ or gamma emitters
_(Gammacamera or SPECT)_

**RADIOPHARMACEUTICALS**

Therapy: alpha or beta emitters such as iodine

Diagnosis:

   - **PET** _**(positron emission tomography):**_ **Beta +** radiation emitters (release
positrons). Mainly fluoride isotopes **(F** **18** **)**

   **Gamma camera/SPECT: gamma** radiation emitters: Technetium, iodine,..
_Main difference between PET and Gamma Camera /SPECT: type of radiation they base_
_themselves on to produce a diagnostic image. Use different radioisotopes._

**PET. POSITRON EMISSION TOMOGRAPHY**

**PET** scans are based on **beta + radiation:**

1. We have isotopes with an **unstable nucleus**
2. They’re stabilized through disintegration and
emission of **positrons** _(electrons with a positive_
_charge (e+))._
3. When These positrons come across an electron,
they undergo **aniquilation:** the **mass of both is**
**destroyed** and transformed into **2 photons of**

**511 keV.**

Tomographs: system dedicated to the **detection** of these **high energy photons:**

   - We obtain a **sequential acquisition** of images as the patient advances through the

device.

   - We need to do **attenuation correction** of the photons and **quantification (SUV).**
_Attenuation = loss of detection of true coincidence events, leading to severe artifacts in PET_
_image. “Attenuation artifacts” are due to the reduction in the intensity / strength of the signal_
_when it travels through body tissues of different densities (specially bones and soft tissue)._
_Has to be corrected during image reconstruction._

   More precise localization of the focal uptake can be
done by **combining PET and CT,** for higher
diagnostic **precision** (anatomical location) and
contribution to therapy planning.

_Attenuation vs Non-attenuation_

_images in PET._

*[PDF p. 46]*

**PET/CT scan protocol**

We can compare PET images with CT,

and fuse them.

The patient enters a **CT scanner** and we
obtain the digital scout image and helical

CT.

Consequently, the PET image is taken.
After attenuation and scatter correction,
the fused image is revealed.

**RADIOPHARMACEUTICAL.** **18** **F - FDG**

Radiopharmaceutical **18F-FDG** is mainly uptaken along with glucose.
