Its concentration **measures glucose metabolism,** and thus, is an indirect measurement of
**cell proliferation** by observing glucose uptake _(more proliferation = more glucose uptake)_

**Neoplastic tissues** (tumors) uptake more glucose as they present **increased glycolysis**

thanks to:

   Higher **GLUT-1 transporter** expression
   - Increased **enzymes of glycolysis** such as hexokinase
All induced by **anaerobic conditions** (HIF-1) in which they are in during their early stages.
The enzymes for glycolysis incorporate FDG, and the molecule of glucose with fluoride is
retained by the tumors. This can be detected through imaging due to radiation emission.

Physiological distribution

We introduce the radioisotope and **wait 1h.** We indicate the patient to **fast** prior to detection

so food doesn’t interfere.

18F-FDG physiological distribution after 1h:
   - **Brain** (high uptake, mainly runs on glucose)
   - **Heart** (more or less depending on the metabolic
rate)
   - **Liver** (glucose metabolism → glycogen)

   **Kidneys and bladder** (through which fluoride is
excreted),..

Patient preparation

   - Non-diabetic: 6h fasting
   - Type II diabetic: 6h fasting + **metformin** (lowers sugar blood levels by improving the
way the body handles insulin)
   - Type I diabetic: breakfast (6h) + **insulin** to allow uptake

   Hydration: the day before and on the day of the PET scan. This increases
**micturition**, ensuring 18F-FDG won’t concentrate in the urine.

   Room temperature
   - Immobilization and correct positioning, avoids **fusion errors**

*[PDF p. 47]*

**Importance of blood glucose levels:** If the glucose levels are elevated, the level of **insulin**
will increase. When insulin levels increase, our **muscles** tend to “suck up” glucose, and the
radioactive tracer won’t go to the areas of interest. **Measure glucose levels before test.**

**High glucose availability, high insulin → muscle**
**uptake**

Most of the 18F-FDG is trapped at the muscles, and there
is less availability for the rest of the tissues. We can
underestimate the real distribution and diagnosis is

altered.

**Importance of room temperature:** if it’s too cold,
brown fat will enter **thermogenesis** to increase body
heat. **Brown fat** will increase glucose and therefore
FDG uptake = abnormal distribution of the radioisotope

Physiological and NOT pathological uptake

  - **Insulin** : high insulin will cause the **muscles** to uptake and
retain the radiopharmaceutical. Insulin must be measured as
to not diagnose a false positive

  - **Cough: respiratory tract** presents more uptake due to
possible inflammation of this area. In addition, there is high
uptake from the **muscles in the abdomen**, as they contract
when we cough. The uptake is not pathological, but
**physiological** .

  - **Inflammation** : inflammatory sites, same as cancer, are sites with
**high activity and cell proliferation** _(lymphocytes, immune system,_
_etc)._ The abnormal glucose uptake must not be mistaken with

cancer.

Example: FDG **bowel uptake** during infections:

        Colonoscopy colitis: inflammation = increased uptake
_(acquired during colonoscopy)_
        - Infectious colitis: increased uptake by the bowel

  - **Previous surgery (laparotomy):** cells in the **scar** have a very **high uptake of FDG.**

**●** **Previous surgery (laparoscopy):** same situation. **High uptake** by the **incision site.**

*[PDF p. 48]*

**●** **Anatomical variations:**

        Pelvic kidney

        Horseshoe kidney (it’s not the bowel, but the kidney). _In this particular image,_
_we can also observe a lung nodule, which, if it uptakes more than a threshold_
_value, has more risk of being a pathological tumor._
These are not pathological situations, but **normal anatomical variations.**

**●** **Breastfeeding: increased breast uptake** due to breastfeeding

Acquisition protocols

We also have many **different adquisition protocols:**

   Head and neck protocol: we perform head and neck PET

**-** **ARMS down**

   Body protocol: we perform an abdominal PET

**-**
**ARMS up.**
_In this case the patient has adenopathies. The PET was performed to_
_identify the primary tumor, which was found to be in the abdomen and with_

_metastasis in head and neck_

   Whole body PET _(multiple myeloma. Mass in the scalp. We can see the uptake in_
_the bone marrow)._

        Base of the skull to mid-thigh

        Head and neck protocol

When we see many focal uptakes in the patient, they can be physiological, pathological,...
so we have to perform further tests to confirm its pathological.

   Anterior-posterior projection _(bone metastasis)_
   - Posterior-anterior _(masses in the breasts)_

**Example:**
_In the patient on the left, we see an inflammatory pathology:_
_increased uptake in the lymph nodes. However, in the patient on_
_the right, there has been lymph node metastasis from a mass in_
_the lung. It has metastasized to lymph nodes in the mediastinum._

*[PDF p. 49]*

**LUNG CANCER**

Lymph node: size and morphology are normal, but PET can
detect tumoral cells due to the abnormal glucose uptake. We
are able to **detect the disease earlier than other techniques,**
**before there are morphological manifestations.**

**LUNG INFECTION**

_Tuberculosis patient_

**NECK CANCER**

Lymph node in the neck presents an **increased uptake,**
**even with normal size.** It's possible to detect the tumor
and adapt the therapy to the extension of the disease.

**ESOPHAGEAL CANCER**

Tumor in the esophagus and lymph node metastasis. It’s also visible on the

CT. Bilateral disease in this case.

**IMAGE ANALYSIS**

It’s also possible to **quantify the amount of disease** within the lesion.
**Semiquantitative analysis. SUV:**
In this patient with multiple myeloma, we can calculate the **SUV**
**(standardized uptake value)** through this formula in order to perform a
semi quantitative analysis. There are **values to diagnose malignancy.**

There are **different types of tumors**, and each is different.

   There are malignant tumors with **very high uptake,** which proliferate very fast and

are less differentiated

   We can also find malignant tumors with **medium to low uptake.**
   - There are also tumors with **very very low uptake** (these are
tumors which are highly differentiated, meaning they are
vascularized and not under anaerobic conditions = less glucose
uptake, not so dependent on glycolysis). Could give a **false**
**negative** depending on interpretation.

SUMMARY

   - **PET isotopes** used to label radiopharmaceuticals are produced in the **cyclotron** .
PET scans give complementary information to usual morpho anatomical techniques
   - PET scans are **more sensitive** than other medical techniques, BUT:

   Results have to be further confirmed, as they can detect non-pathological lesions
**(anatomical variations or benign lesions)** that could be interpreted as pathological

   Very good in **early cancer diagnosis,** prior to symptoms and morphological changes
   - It can be used for **staging cancers** (SUV analysis)
   - Can be used for **monitoring response to treatment.** Metabolic changes can occur
in the tumor even when there haven’t been morpho anatomical changes (changes in
size), or we can also detect new metastasis due to increased absorption in other
areas, which would lead the physician to change the treatment.

*[PDF p. 50]*



*[PDF p. 51]*

#### Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI)

MRI relies on the existence of a **magnetic field,** which is aligned with the orientation of the
patient _(depending on the company, aligned from head to feet or feet to head)._ We are able
to recover **3D images of specific organs** of the body, or even **localize active regions of**
**the brain** of the patient.
_(TED TALK: human brain mapping and brain decoding)_

**WHAT IS A MAGNETIC FIELD?**

We have a **magnetic field (B),** which consists in a **manifestation** of
the presence of **electrical particles in movement.**

**1.** What is its e�ect on electric particles?

The only particles that can interact with a magnetic field are **moving charged particles** _(+ or_
_-),_ which have a **velocity different than 0.**

These particles will suffer from a **force** whose origin is the magnetic field, which will have an
**effect on the particle’s trajectory.** This force, known as **Lorentz force,** appears **ONLY for**
**moving particles.**

The behaviour of this force is quite peculiar: if we have an
electric field, the force equals the electric field times the
charge. However with magnetic fields, **the Lorentz force is**
**proportional to the charge,** but **also to the velocity** and to
the **magnetic field.** The velocity plays a role in the generation
of the field. If v= 0, it won’t be generated. Magnetic behaviour
is more complex than electric behaviour.

In the case of the magnetic field, the force will be **perpendicular** to the **velocity and**
**magnetic field,** and its **orientation** will depend on the **orientation of the magnetic field,**
**the direction in which the particle travels (v)** and the **charge of the particle.**

As the **force is perpendicular to velocity,** it **will not provide energy to the particle.**
Therefore it won’t modify its kinetic energy. Its only effect is to **deviate the trajectory** of the
particle. This is opposed to the electric field, where the force can modify Ek.

_Lorentz force:_

From this equation we can obtain the unit for the magnetic field, which is the **Tesla (T).**
**1 Tesla = 10^4 Gauss (G).**

*[PDF p. 52]*

_The Earth’s magnetic field is 0.5 Gauss, quite small. There are magnetic resonance systems_
_of over 1-1.5 Teslas. The limit value used in medical application in the EU is 3 Teslas (for_
_clinical use). For research, they can be over these values, but not in clinical practice. 0.1 T is_
_the typical magnetic force we will find on the surface of a potent permanent magnet._

**2.**
How can we generate this magnetic field?

The **magnetic field** appears surrounding an **electric charge in movement.** The
appearance of any magnetic field is **based on the displacement of these charged moving**
**particles.**

In this example, a positive particle is moving along
the Y axis with a velocity “v”. As soon as it starts
moving, a **magnetic field appears around the**
**moving particle.** The field has the shape of
**concentric circles** which are found on a plane
**perpendicular** to the direction of the particle’s
velocity.

But this is in the case of a single charge.

If we take a large amount of charges and place them in a **ring, each of these**
**single particles will generate a small magnetic field.** When we combine all
of them, we obtain the second diagram _(cross section):_ the final magnetic field
we get for a circular ring will be pointing upwards within the surface, crossing
the surface of the ring and moving outwards.

The final result is there is a region in which the magnetic field lines **leave**
the circle **(north pole)** and another in which they are **entering (south**
**pole).** This is a **magnetic dipole (N and S),** the most simple
configuration of a magnetic field. North and south poles cannot be
created in an isolated way. This configuration is important as **at the**
**subatomic level, we observe this behaviour.**

At a subatomic level:

   - in each **electron** orbital, the oscillating electrons are rotating,
creating a magnetic dipole.
   - the **protons** inside the nucleus aren’t stationary, but also rotating.

Both systems will **generate their own magnetic field.** Because of the displacement of
these charges, there will be an **interaction with an external magnetic field.**
_Can we suppose the magnetic field has no effect on a person? NO. There is interaction_
_when we apply a magnetic field on a patient, due to the spin of these charged particles that_
_make up the molecules we are created by._

*[PDF p. 53]*

**3.**
How does the magnetic field interact with matter?

Response of the dipole to an external magnetic field: all **the dipoles will be aligned with**
**the external magnetic field.**

_Green arrow: external magnetic field_
_Two dipoles placed within this external_
_magnetic field. Charges are leaving and_
_penetrating the screen._

What is the effect of this external

magnetic field?
In both 1st and 2nd dipole, the **Lorentz**
**forces** acting on the charges are
**different directions, but same value.**
Therefore, there is **no displacement.**
The only effect the magnetic field can have is to reorient the dipole through **rotation.**

There are two **possibilities of reorientation:**

   - **Parallele orientaion**, N pointing upwards, S pointing downwards, in a parallel
orientation in relation to the external field **(more stable configuration)**
   - **Antiparallel orientation** in relation to the field, which is **more unstable** and requires
additional energy.

Naturally, the magnetic field tries to align the north and south poles.This **reorientation** is
known as **magnetization.**

TYPES OF MAGNETIZATION

Depending on the material, there are different types of magnetization and orientations.

  - **Ferromagnetic:** if we apply an external magnetic field, we induce a **global**
**magnetization.** There is a **significant degree** of magnetization, which remains
when the external field is removed: the dipoles will **retain magnetization and**
**remain oriented.** Seen in materials such as **iron and niquel.**

  - **Paramagnetic:** in this case, **magnetization** is **very weak.** The dipoles in the
material will align, but when the external magnetic field is removed, the
magnetization disappears. The dipoles disorganize and **do not remain oriented.**

Seen in the case of **O2 and aluminium.**

  - **Diamagnetic:** When we apply a magnetic field, the system responds in a way that
acts in the opposite way. There is a **resistance to being oriented.** The dipoles are
found pointing in the **opposite direction to the magnetic field.** Observed on **living**

**materials.**

Any material interacts with magnetic fields, in one way or another. The normal interaction
seen in the **human body** is the **diamagnetic magnetization/behaviour.**

*[PDF p. 54]*

**REAL MRI** (magnetic resonance imaging)

In real life, **precession moment** appears **due to rotation: the system doesn’t follow its**
**original trajectory.**
In the previous diagram: **if the rotation of the particles is relevant,** as is the case **light**
**particles,** this **rotation** _(omega)_ is **not “correct”**, meaning that the **trajectory indicated**

**isn’t correct.**

_What would be the real effect of the magnetic force when this system is rotating?_

Image shows the classical point of view of the behaviour of a proton _(without spin)_ .
**Protons** have a property known as **spin,** which is **originally placed in an antiparallel way.**
When **submitted to a magnetic field,** the **proton creates a magnetic field in the sense of**
**the spin,** found in an **antiparallel** fashion, which **will try to obtain the more stable**
**orientation by transitioning to the parallel conformation.**

   - If there were **no spin,** the expected result would be to follow the **classical trajectory**

**shown.**

   - However, **due to rotation**, it **will orient into the parallel direction** following a
**helicoidal/spiral transition** towards said orientation.
This spiral movement is accompanied by the **emission of an EM wave,** generated
because of the displacement of the protons when transitioning from antiparallel to
parallel orientation.

*[PDF p. 55]*

If this were the only action of the MRI device, after being introduced into the field, all the
dipoles would be oriented and nothing would happen. There is a **sequence of different**
**processes generated by the system,** whih will allow us to retrive an image.

1. **Patient is introduced into the system, into the magnetic field.** All the **dipoles** will
be **pointing in a parallel way** to the magnetic field.

2. **Excitation** . We will start applying a sequence of **stimulus:** first, we will send energy
in the form of an **EM wave,** which has an energy of the **magnitude of a**
**radiowave/microwave** _(low frequency)._ The **protons** will absorb the energy **and**
**reorient in the opposite, unstable configuration due to excitation.**

3. We then **let the system relax.** When retrieving the stimulus, the system will tend to
**return to its stable configuration.** This is done through that **spiral/helicoidal**
**transition** mentioned earlier, which is accompanied by the **emission of EM waves**
that are detected by the system.
This sequence is carried out repeatedly, which is what **gives out the MRI image**

**based on the emision.**

The spiral movement generates an **EM wave** with a frequency that depends on the electric
dipole. This **frequency** is **different** for the different protons found in specific substances in
the body.

   - It **depends on the type of particle.** Each particle will have its own specific magnetic
dipole.
   - Also **depends on the local value of the magnetic field.** Depending on what the
surrounding molecules are, the local magnetic field will be different.

**MRI** mainly measures **protons (hydrogen, H+) and their dipole’s emission.** Therefore, the
most important echo will come from **water,** a very abundant molecule inside the human
body.
**Other techniques can measure other nuclei,** but
only those with an **odd number of nucleons** _(for_
_example, O16 will not be detected by MRI as it_
_won’t interact with the EM field, due to lack of spin)._

*[PDF p. 56]*

COMPONENTS OF DIPOLE BEHAVIOUR

We can split the **behaviour of the dipole into 2 parts:**

  - **Longitudial magnetization** : the part that is **parallel to the**
**external magnetic field** is known as the **longitudinal**
**magnetization.** Longitudinal magnetization **increases in time**
_(asintotical increase)._
**T1** corresponds to the time by which **0.67** of the spin has
returned to the longitudinal magnetization value.

  - **Transverse magnetization:** the behaviour of the **component perpendicular to the**
**external field** is known as the transverse magnetization. For the transverse
movement, there is a **decrease of magnetization in time.**
**T2** corresponds to the time you have to wait for the **0.37** transverse component to
have transitioned nto the parallel orientation.

On an **MRI device, we measure T1 and T2.** Depending on the
material, T1 and T2 will be different.

   - They can be **very large,** as is the case of **water**
_(meaning it takes a long time to rearrange the dipoles)._
   - However, in **solids** this reorientation is done really fast,
meaning T1 and T2 are **very low.**
Due to many studies, we can **determine which tissue** is being
detected **depending on the obtained T1 and T2** for each
region, and therefore, this will allow us to **classify the**
**structures in the image.**

**SAFETY CONCERNS MRI**

   - There is a **maximum magnetic field** a person can be subjected to. The max is **40**
**mT.** Only in specific situations can this value be increased.

   - We have to be aware of **metallic components,** as this can imply **mechanical and**

**thermal issues.**

   - The key point for the device to work is that **we absorb EM waves.** This means that
the **temperature can increase.** There is a magnitude defined, known as **SAR** (P/M)
_(represents the energy absorbed by the body of the patient. Measures the_ _**ability to**_
_**transform this energy received into thermal energy**_ _)_

*[PDF p. 57]*

#### MRI basics

WHAT IS MRI?

**MRI** is an imaging method based on the **nuclear resonance phenomenon.**
It obtains sectional images, in any plane, using a very strong **magnetic**
**field, gradients and radiofrequency pulses.**
The machine detector is similar to CT, but longer and more tube-like.

**Advantages:**

   - It **doesn’t use ionizing radiation.** Therefore, there is no cancer risk.

   It can obtain anatomical, functional and other information.
   - It achieves **very high contrast between different tissues**, while in
CT, as it is based on density, when tissues with similar density are
close together they’re difficult to differentiate.

**ELEMENTS**

 - Magnet

The magnet creates a **magnetic field.** Its power is measured in **Teslas** _(T, in IS)_ **or Gauss.**
1 T = 10000 G. Nowadays, the vast majority of equipment is **1.5 - 3 T.**
The magnet **never turns off** : whenever we enter the room, we must chek we’re not wearing
ferromagnetic material.

According to quantum mechanics, each element of an atom has an angular momentum
called **"spin."** For us to understand it, it is usually compared to a sphere rotating on an axis.
Every **electric charge in motion** _(in this case_ _**spinning**_ _)_ causes a **magnetic field.**

**MRI** is based on **hydrogen atoms,** as they interact with the magnetic field _(uneven number_
_of protons)_ and there are many H atoms in our body _(found in water)._

Function of the magnet:

   - Under **normal conditions,** each atom is oriented randomly in parallel or antiparallel
ways and the **total magnetic moment is 0.**

   - Under a very strong magnetic field (referred as **B** **0** ), the
**hydrogen atoms will align themselves** in a **parallel**
**or antiparallel direction** to the field _(this will only affect_
_atoms with an uneven number of protons, as they will_
_have an electrical charge)._

   There are always **slightly more atoms in parallel,**
making the **neat magnetic moment** (M) is **different**
**from 0.** This magnetic moment is what we will modify in
order to obtain a signal and create an image

*[PDF p. 58]*

We send a low frequency wave to induce the antiparallel state in all
of the dipoles.
Once the **excitatory stimulus is removed,** the particle will tend to
return to its stable state. This is done through the process of
**precession** _**(spiral/helicoidal transition).**_ It precesses at a
**constant angular velocity** (omega) which is known as **Larmor**
**frequency.**

This Larmor frequency depends on the **constant** γ, which is **specific for each atom;**
and depends on **B,** the static magnetic field. _Each material = specific Larmor frequency_

 Radiofrequency (B1)

When additional energy in the form of a **radio wav** e is added to the magnetic field, the
**magnetic vector (M) is deflected.**
The radio wave frequency/ **radiofrequency (RF)** that causes the hydrogen nuclei to resonate
is dependent on the **element** sought _(hydrogen the case of MRI)_ and the **strength of the**
**magnetic field (B = Larmor’s frequency).**

Due to the phenomenon of **magnetic resonance,** RF is “absorbed” by the hydrogen atom,
altering its state to another of higher energy, flipping the M angle.
When the RF stops, the atoms return to their initial state, **emitting that extra energy as RF**
that **provides us the signal for the image.**

Initially, there is no magnetic moment in the transverse (Y) plane
_**(magnetization is purely longitudinal).**_ After **radiofrequency** is
applied, we modify the spin, finding components in the Z axis and
in the transverse plane. After a certain time, the spins end up
being all oriented in the transverse plane _**(magnetization is**_
_**purely transverse).**_
But when we retrieve the radiofrequency, the atoms return their
spin to the initial orientation following a spiral transition
(precession), through the **emission of a RF wave.**

When we stop radiofrequency (precession), **T1 relaxation and T2 relaxation both occur at**

**the same time**

*[PDF p. 59]*

**During precession:**

   - **T1 or longitudinal relaxation:** during precession, we are **gaining momentum in**
**the Z axis,** which is **measured by T1.**
T1 relaxation is the process by which the **net magnetization (M) grows/returns** to
its initial **maximum value (M0), parallel to B0.**
**T1 =** can be viewed as the time required for the z component of M to reach _**63%**_ of its

maximum value.

During this process, there is transfer of energy from the spins to **nearby molecules,**
while they go back to the Z axis.

   - **T2 or transverse relaxation:** during precession, **components in the transverse**
**plane lose momentum** and decrease until reaching 0, **measured by T2.**
Therefore, T2 relaxation is the process by which the **transverse** components of
magnetization _**(Mxy)**_ **decay or dephase.**
**T2= i** s defined as the time required for the transverse magnetization to fall to
approximately _**37%**_ **(1/e)** of its initial value.
**Spins dephase** and begin to **desynchronize**, losing overall signal.

**Each tissue,** depending on its composition, has **different T1 and T2** relaxation times, which
have been previously studied and identified. This allows us to **classify tissues and create**
**an image.** Depending on the **sequence** used, a signal from the same tissue changes
radically. We can attenuate the signal of a tissue to obtain the image of interest.

 - Gradients

Gradients generate slight **alterations in the homogeneous**
**magnetic field,** making the magnetic field intensity at each
point slightly different. In other words, it will make the
**waves/energy coming back from each point slightly**
**different.** This will allow the machine to locate in the space
where the signal comes from, and therefore identify and
recreate the structure in the 3D space into an image.

*[PDF p. 60]*

- Antenas or coils

For **optimal image quality,** the **antenna/coil** that receives the signal
should be **as close as possible to the area to be studied.** For this
reason, there are normally multiple antennas, each designed to study
a specific area. The correct use of the antennas and the positioning
of the patient in them is a determining part in the final quality of the
image.

**THE SEQUENCES (“SOFTWARE”)**

Each tissue behaves differently according to its composition and according to its excitation
and relaxation parameters. We can **program "sequences"** where we **adjust different**
**parameters** to **"enhance" specific aspects of the image/tissues.**
Main sequence parameters _(TR, TE, TI, slice thickness, flip angle,...; no need to know them)_
can be modified to obtain more signal from one tissue or another.

Each pixel is assigned a **shade of gray based on the intensity of the received signal.**
However, the **signal intensity** depends on the tissue, the equipment and the sequence
used. Therefore, ulike the HU, its value is **variable** : there is **no absolute value** for each
tissue, as the signal intensity can be modified depending on the **tissue, the energy of the**
**incident wave and the selected sequence.**

**●** **Tissue factors:**

        - H1 concentration

        - Relaxation time T1

        - Relaxation time T2

        Dynamic state

**●** **Operational factors:**

        - SEQUENCES: TR, TE,
matrix, FoV, flip angle,...

        - Number of excitations

*[PDF p. 61]*

**ACQUISITION**

   - It usually takes about **20 mins-half an hour** to acquire the different sequences. But
you adapt to the time you have.
   - **Antennas** are very important in acquisition to study the area of interest. IV contrast.
   - Movement artifact! **Claustrophobia** . We can sometimes opt to sedate the patient
_(claustrophobic, patients with dementia,...)_ to obtain the image.

**SAFETY**

   **Very safe** technique. It does not use ionizing radiation.

   Can be performed during **pregnancy**
   - It has **no known effect on the tissues** of the body. No cases have been described
that show that static magnetic fields produce alterations on the structure of DNA,
reproduction and/or cell growth.
   - The main risk is related to the **presence of external metallic/electronic materials**,
which can produce the **projectile effect**, interaction with metal components present.

MRI SAFETY LABELLING

The main risk derived from the static magnetic field is due to its **interaction with**
**ferromagnetic materials** _(iron, nickel and cobalt)_ and the consequent **"projectile effect".**
This also applies to patients with **ferromagnetic foreign bodies or transplants.**

The production of **heat** due to **RF absorption** is also a potential risk. It can cause **burns** on
the patient. Levels are controlled by **SAR limits. SAR levels** are estimates of the amount of
energy absorbed by each region of the body, and vary significantly depending on the patient
and the equipment.

*[PDF p. 62]*



*[PDF p. 63]*

#### Ultrasound waves

**MECHANICAL WAVES**

A **mechanical wave** is any wave that can produce a **deformation** of a material through

**contact forces.**

The result will be a **perturbation** on/in the material: some of the atoms will be moved from
the previous position to a new position. In the case of material deformation, the concept of

**stress** is introduced.

**Stress** is the **force that acts per unit of area** on the material. It indicates how intense the
force acting on each atom will be.

   - When the force is applied **perpendicularly**, it is said to be **normal stress.**
   - When the force acts in **parallel** to the surface, it is said to be **shear stress.**
   - When the stress acts to **shorten bonds**, it’s a **compressive stress.**
   - When it acts to **lengthen bonds,** it’s a **tensile stress.**

If **stresses are small,** the **deformation** produced is **proportional** to the stress and is also
**reversible.** In this case, the process is said to be an **elastic process.** In contrast, if the
**stresses are very large,** they can end up producing **permanent deformation or fracture.**

TYPES OF MECHANICAL WAVES

  - **Transverse waves:** as a result of perturbations in the **X or Y axis.** Chemical bonds
between all the atoms are displaced and the oscillation occurs in a **perpendicular**
**direction** to the direction in which the wave propagates/advances.
Therefore, transverse mechanical waves are **shear waves** _**(transversal):**_ the atoms
involved move in a direction that is perpendicular to wave propagation.

  - **Longitudinal waves:** as a result of a perturbation in the **Z axis.** The oscillation
occurs in the direction of the atom chain, one after the other. In other words, in the

**same direction** in which the wave advances.

Longitudinal mechanical waves are therefore **waves of compression and tension**
**(** _**normal):**_ the atoms involved move in the same direction as the propagation of the

wave.

*[PDF p. 64]*

The **state of matter** affects the type of wave that can exist. In fluids it’s impossible to have
shear stress, only pressure. In solids, all range of possibilities:

   - In the **solid state** there can be **longitudinal and transverse** mechanical waves.

   - In the **fluid state** _(liquid or gas)_, **ONLY longitudinal waves are possible.** A fluid
cannot produce a deformation to counteract the action of shear stress: the elements
of a fluid are simply displaced when subjected to shear stresses. As they are
displaced, they rub against other elements in the fluid, and thus energy that is
injected in a transverse manner is rapidly dissipated in heat. In this case, in which
**only normal stresses are possible, waves are referred to as pressure waves**
_(act by pushing and pulling)._ The force that produces a pressure is always
perpendicular to the area of the system, as it’s a normal stress.

Equation which characterizes a wave

   - Ambient/Atmos pressure usually corresponds to **1 atm** _(know the conversion to cm_
_H2o and mmHg)_

   **p0** corresponds to the altitude of the wave **(Amplitude)**

   The absolute pressure will always be larger than 0 (p>0)
   - ω = angular frequency

   From here, the velocity of the wave is defined as:

_What is negative pressure? It makes reference to the behaviour compared to ambient_
_pressure. Negative pressures are those pressures below ambient pressure. It has an_
_absolute value, not negative._

*[PDF p. 65]*

WAVE CATEGORIES ACCORDING TO FREQUENCIES

Mechanical waves are **categorized into three groups according to their frequency:**

  - **Infrasound:** mechanical waves where the **frequency < 20 Hz**

  - **Sound:** waves with frequencies between **20 Hz< f < 20 KHz** _(max a human can_
_hear)_

  - **Ultrasounds:** mechanical waves with **frequency > 20 KHz**

Because **propagation velocity** depends on the elastic properties of the material involved, it
will have different values in different types of material.

   - Inside the **human body (water),** they travel at **1500 m/s**
   - In **air**, they travel at **340 m/s**

**MEDICAL ULTRASOUND**

Ultrasound is used in medicine in **diagnosis and treatment.** Equipment that
uses ultrasound for diagnosis is based on very different principles to other
wave-based diagnostic equipment, such as X-ray machines. In the case of
ultrasound, it is the **wave reflected by the interior of the body** that is
analyzed; in contrast, with **ionizing radiation,** it is the **transmitted wave** that
is analyzed.
**Medical devices** work with waves travelling at **1-20 MHz** _(landa = 1.55 mm)_

An example of treatment by ultrasound is _**lithotripsy**_, in which ultrasound waves generate
pressure waves and shear waves in **kidney stones,** breaking them into smaller parts that
can be eliminated naturally. Another example is _**high-intensity focused ultrasound (HIFU),**_
which makes it possible to destroy certain **brain cells** responsible for **trembling in**

**Parkinson's disease.**

*[PDF p. 66]*

EFFECT OF ACOUSTIC IMPEDANCE

Let's suppose that we have a flat ultrasound wave propagating through **medium A,** and it
arrives at a different **medium B.** These two mediums are separated by an **interface.**
Given the density and elastic properties of the medium (Z), we might find that it is very
difficult to generate a displacement of atoms, even if the incident pressure is very high. Such
reasoning suggests that if **medium A is air and medium** **B is water,** which has a density
about 1000 times greater than air, it should be **difficult to transmit a sound wave from the**
**air into the water** _(This can be experienced in a swimming pool: when we put our heads_
_below the water, external sounds are greatly attenuated)._

More rigorously: there is a **series of magnitudes that are**
**conserved on either side of the interface.** There must be continuity
in velocity and pressure, and intensity must be conserved.
From these relationships, it is found that, if a pressure wave _pi_ is
incident from medium A to medium B, then the reflected wave p R and
the transmitted wave p T are obtained by:

Where **zA and zB** are the **impedances of each medium.**

**Z** is **constant** and **specific** to each material, known as **Acoustic impedance** . It reflects the
resistance of a material to being displaced.
The acoustic impedance **depends on the number of atoms in a region (density) and the**
**elastic behaviour** of a material _(which is reflected by the velocity of sound within it)._

**z = density · velocity of sound in the material**

From these equations, we find that the **energy (the intensity) is conserved.** That is, the
**incident intensity** is **divided between the reflected wave IR and transmitted wave IT.**

To sum up, we can conclude that **at any interface** between two materials, **there will be a**
**reflected wave and a transmitted wave. The relation between incoming pressure wave**
**and reflected wave will depend on the impedances of both materials.**
There are **two limiting situation** s:

   - If the impedances of the two mediums are **similar** (zA and zB), there is practically **no**
**reflected wave,** and 100% is transmitted.

   - If there are very **different** impedances, practically 100% of the energy is reflected,
and **almost nothing is transmitted**

*[PDF p. 67]*

When a pressure wave _(constant)_ arrives at a material:

   - If the **impedance is high**, the **velocity** will be quite **low.**
   - If the **impedance is low,** the resulting **velocity** will be **high.**

_* Remember: all these waves propagate energy. The more atoms we have to displace, the_
_smaller the displacement._

ULTRASOUND IMAGING

_How and why is an ultrasound wave reflected? How are the different shades of grey_
_generated in the image?_

The ultrasound is generated by a device that contains a **piezoelectric crystal.** This crystal,
when subjected to a voltage, oscillates and **transmits its vibration** to the medium around it.

This device is called a **transducer** and it acts both as a **source and a receiver for waves.**

The transducer generates and emits the US wave. This wave is generated in
air _(very small impedance)_ and impacts on the human body _(high_
_impedance)._ **To avoid** there being **no transmission** ( _which would mean the_
_wave wouldn’t reach the rest of the tissues inside the body),_ the **wave is**
**generated inside a material whose impedance is very close to the**
**impedance of the human body.** For example, a **gel.**
In this way almost all of the energy is transmitted and nothing is reflected, so
the **ultrasound gets into the body.**

The wave propagates through the body and it will **encounter different surfaces** on different
organs. At each surface, part of the wave will be **reflected and detected by the machine.**
The **intensity** of the reflected part of the wave **will depend on the impedance** values.
_At the extremes of the range of possibilities are bones and large cavities full of air. where the_
_impedance is very different from that of the rest of the body, and so a mechanical wave will_
_find it difficult to penetrate them._

The reflected wave will go back towards the
**transducer**, where the **signal is registered.**
By measuring the journey time **∆trip** _(from_
_when the wave was generated until the echo –_
_the reflected wave - is received),_ it is possible

to **determine the distance at which the**

**surface is located.**

This is the operating principle behind echography by ultrasound: by **sweeping** through
different directions and **collecting the various echoes** from each surface, it is possible to
**construct an image** that **represents, in gray-scale, the intensity of the reflected wave** .

   - White/bright lines are observed when there is big echo; maximum intensity

   - Dark/black lines are observed when there is no or almost no echo

*[PDF p. 68]*

**Echoes and gray-scale**

A material that does not generate an echo is said to be **an-echoic or**
**non-echogenic.** In contrast, materials that do generate an echo are said
to be **echogenic.**

   **Inhomogeneous** materials generate more echo. For this reason,
**bones** result in **brighter images.**
   - **Fluids** and **air-filled cavities** don’t generate any acho, and
appear **black** in the echography image.

**Ultrasound studies and frequency**

**-** **Low frequency:** used in **studies at depth**, in which great detail is not necessary.
**-** **High frequency:** used for **surface or near surface studies**, in which greater image
detail is required.

DOPPLER EFFECT

Ultrasound can be used to measure the **velocity of blood.** This is based on the modification
of the frequency of a wave by a body in movement: the **Doppler effect.** It’s used to **study**
**the reflected wave coming from a moving body.**

**Doppler effect** :

   When a stopped ambulance generates a sound, it
propagates in the same way in all directions _(same_
_wavelengths)_

   However, when velocity is different from 0, the result is
that the wavelengths are clustered in front of the
ambulance and separated in the back part. The
wavelength length in front is lower than that at the back.
Therefore, people hearing the ambulance when it
approaches them hear it with a higher pitch than those
watching it while it moves away.

Applied to medicine: **RBC reflect waves, producing this doppler effect.** This higher
frequency can be detected by the machine. We can calculate the **velocity of a RBC** by
paying attention to the **frequency of the reflected wave.**

Inside the human body, the frequency f R of a wave reflected by an erythrocyte will be:

_Where f0 is the original frequency of the ultrasound wave, v_ _ult_ _is the component of velocity_
_deriving from the erythrocyte along the ray followed by the ultrasound waves, and c is the_
_velocity of sound in the body._

*[PDF p. 69]*

**Doppler displacement**

Doppler displacement/shift can be observed due to the **effect of the angle**

formed between the transducer and the vessel.

Within the same vessel, when using ultrasound, we can measure the
velocity of erythrocytes in different ways. **According to the angle formed**
between the **transducer and the vessel,** we will find a **Doppler shift** that is
either **positive** (bottom) **or negative** (top), **depending on v** **ult** **:** whether the
erythrocytes are approaching or moving away from the transducer.

   Approaching: f > fo (+ shift, increased value)

   Moving away: f < fo ( - shift, decreased value)

ULTRASOUND - SAFETY CONCERNS

The **energy** which is being transported by mechanical waves is **absorbed** by the human
body.

  - **Thermal index (TI):** reflects that there is an energy being absorbed by the system,
and thus, increasing its temperature. If it’s being absorbed by a specific tissue, the
temperature will increase in said tissue. There is a **limit temperature.**
The **TI reflects the power absorbed in a specific tissue compared to the power**
**needed to increase 1ºC the tissue of that specific organ.**
This ratio has to be smaller than one. By using this limit, we
can be sure no dangerous increase in temperature will be
produced.

  - **Mechanical index (MI):** The pressure of the US will oscillate around an ambient
pressure. Is there any limit to pressure values? In the case of the maximum, we have
already seen that we can cause “damage” or heat material if the pressure is very
high. It turns out that there is also a risk of damage if the minimum pressure goes too

low.

If the oscillation minimum falls below the vapour pressure, we are going to produce
something that will locally increase the pressure **(bubbles).**
There will be a **spontaneous transition from the vapour to the liquid phase** when
the **pressure falls below P** _**vapour.**_ This process is called **cavitation**, the generation of
a vapour phase in a liquid due to the fall in pressure.

The mechanical index is defined as:

It’s proportional to the peak negative pressure (p0)
and therefore, to local pressure p. MI has to be
**lower than 1.9.** If this MI is too large, there is a
higher possibility of producing cavitation inside the
patient _(creation of vapour phase bubbles when you_
_try to induce a pressure on a liquid which is below_
_the vapour pressure of said liquid)._

*[PDF p. 70]*

_* Concept of vapour pressure - we introduce a small amount of a liquid in a closed_
_cavity. If there is no gas, the P = 0 as there is nothing exerting a force on the_
_material. Therefore, in principle the pressure in the liquid is equivalent to the vapour_
_pressure, which is 0._
_However, the liquid has a temperature which is different than 0. Therefore the_
_molecules are vibrating. This vibration causes some of the liquid particles to switch_
_(jump) from the liquid to the vapour phase._
_Therefore there is a region of molecules in liquid phase and a region containing more_
_and more molecules in vapour phase._
_The pressure in the upper part will increase until there is an equilibrium between the_
_molecules evaporating and those recondensing._
_This behaviour occurs even for temperatures below the boiling point of the liquid._
_There is a point where there will be an equilibrium, so the vapour pressure will be_
_equilibrated with the liquid pressure. The pressure in the equilibrium depends on the_
_temperature of the system._

*[PDF p. 71]*

#### Ultrasound

_Ultrasound workshop I_

**Ultrasound waves** are mechanical waves with frequencies **> 20 kHz** (audible range: 20 Hz 20kHz)
Its main application in medicine is the **generation of images of the body.**

HOW IS IT GENERATED?

The generation of US waves is based on the **piezoelectric effect.**

The thickness of some materials can be modified by an electric current
In this case, **Quartz crystals** will **modify their thickness** when struck with an **electrical**
**signal.** These changes in thickness will generate a **mechanical vibration** and produce **high**
**frequency wave** s (US waves).
In conclusion, an electrical signal will be transformed into a mechanical ultrasound.

The ultrasound waves will be transmitted to the body, where **some will penetrate more**
**than others.** Some waves will be **transmitted** _(penetrate)_ while others will be **reflected.**
These **reflected waves** are received and transformed into an electrical signal by the
**transducer,** which will process the data and give us an **image.**

ULTRASOUND EQUIPMENT

   The waves are generated by a transducer/probe. We use different probes depending
on the part of the body we want to scan.

   - Patient contact

   Connector (cable) to the US machine

   Images can be seen on a monitor in real time, and in any plane

↳ **Transducers/probes**

There are **different types:** (vary in shape, size,...). Some are
used for tendons, for neonates _(brain)_, transvaginal transducers
_(gynecology, to scan uterus and ovaries),_ in the surgery room,...

        - Curvilinear transducers are used to check the **abdomen** .

**Low frequencies** are used, to obtain **more penetrance** although with **less**
**resolution** (DEEP in the abdomen. Used for in depth studies).

        - Linear transducers: are used on superficial structures such as the skin.
High frequencies are used, which give **lower penetrance** but **higher**
**resolution** (SUPERFICIAL structures, for superficial, detailed studies).

_“the higher the frequency, the more the wave is absorbed and the shallower the penetration”_

*[PDF p. 72]*

ATTENUATION IN ULTRASOUND

The **amplitude and intensity** of ultrasound waves decrease as they travel through tissue, a
phenomenon known as **attenuation.**
Given a fixed propagation distance, **attenuation affects high frequency ultrasound waves**
**to a greater degree** than lower frequency waves, as the higher the frequency, the more the
wave is absorbed and the shallower the penetration.

**Amplitude and intensity** of the wave therefore **depend on the attenuation.**

        - **Materials with low attenuation/low absorption** = **water and fluids.** They appear
**black** on the image, and are said to be **anechoic** _(don’t produce an echo)_ . When
present as artifacts, these structures will present **posterior acoustic enhancement.**

        - **Materials with intermediate attenuation** = **solid tissues.** They present with
different scales of **gray**, depending on if they’re **hypo, iso or even hyperechoic.**

        - **Materials with high attenuation/high absorption** = **bones, calcium, metal,..** They
appear **white** on the image, and are said to be **hyperechoic** _(produce the highest_
_echo)._ When present as artifacts, they produce **posterior acoustic shadowing.**

_The calculi (calcium) in the kidney and gallbladder cause posterior acoustic shadowing_
_The hepatic cyst ( *cystic structures: closed capsule or sac-like structures, typically filled with_
_a liquid) demonstrates the opposite phenomenon of posterior acoustic enhancement._

*[PDF p. 73]*

**Shadowing** occurs when there is a region that does not allow
ultrasound to pass through. This is the case with bone, a gallstone,...
In each of these cases, there is a region in which there is a **big**
**difference in impedance** _(bone, stone, air)_ that makes the wave
**reflect**, and consequently we **cannot see what happens behind**
such a region. These regions appear darkened, and are like a
shadow of the zone that has reflected the ultrasound; the zone itself

being bright.

DOPPLER COLOUR ULTRASOUND

Another property of the US is that it gives info about the flow direction of the structures that
are moving _(arteries, veins,..)._ They present the known **Doppler Effect:** the frequency varies
depending on the direction of the moving particle. Therefore by using this colour technique,
depending on the **direction and velocity,** we will see **red or blue colours.**

It’s used to **codify flow direction.**

   - Flow **towards** the transducer: RED

   - Flow **away from** the transducer: BLUE

Example 1: **liver US.** We can observe the **portal vein**, with
blood flowing towards the liver.
In **cirrhotic patients**, the liver is very rigid, with no elasticity.
A sign of this in the US is that the portal vein receives a
**reverse flow,** as the flow cannot go towards the liver.

Example 2: **carotid artery and jugular vein**

COLOR AND POWER DOPPLER

Example: **right kidney ultrasound.** Patient comes in with **renal failure,** so we want to

check the **vasculature.**

Colour doppler identifies moving structures, such as those present in vasculature.
But we can also use **Power doppler** to check vasculature, which is better as it’s **very**
**sensible for movement.** However, it is not specific for the direction of the flow and therefore
**doesn’t codify the flow direction.** Only serves to see if there’s movement or not.

*[PDF p. 74]*

COLOR AND PULSED DOPPLER

Pulsed doppler allows us to **quantify velocity and determine flow direction.**

When US waves contact with blood:

   - if it flows **towards** the transducer, we register a POSITIVE VELOCITY
   - if it flows **away from** the transducer: NEGATIVE

VELOCITY

Example 1: we can see the **morphology of the arteries in the**
**right kidney.**

Example 2: **carotid-jugular**

**blood flow** . Arterial flow

_(pulses)_ and Venous flow _(it’s a_
_continuous flow, barely any_
_pulses)_

LOCALIZATION OF STRUCTURES

LIVER US ABDOMINAL US THYROID-GLAND US

CARPAL TUNNEL US EYE US TESTICLES US

*[PDF p. 75]*

DETECTION AND LESION CHARACTERIZATION

We can **classify tumors**, detect **metastasis**, detect the **presence of fluid,...**

DIAGNOSIS-CLINICAL CORRELATION

US can be used to detect **acute appendicitis.** A patient refers
having pain in the right iliac fossa. While checking this area,
the patient refers increased pain once we stop compression.
This is indicative of acute appendicitis. It’s **important to**
**know, in the moment, how the pain evolves in real time.**

COMPARATIVE IMAGES

In this ultrasound image we can see the **left and right thigh.** Each
image shows **normal and abnormal muscle in the thigh,** after
running. By **comparing** the images, we are able to detect an
abnormality in the right thigh, caused by a **Hematoma** within the

muscle.

VASCULARIZATION AND INFLAMMATION

GUIDED BIOPSY

To **know where the lesion is** while introducing and guiding the
biopsy device, as it allows us to observe the image in **real time.**
Example: needle entering liver and going towards the lesion.

*[PDF p. 76]*

**Ultrasound advantages**

**-**
**Doesn’t use ionizing radiation**

   Availability, portable and fast

   - We can scan in **real time.** Allows **immediate visualization**

   - **Guidance** for invasive procedures
   - We can obtain **many perspectives and angles** _(good for lesions and localization)_

   **Cheaper** than other imaging modalities. Therefore, nowadays, they are readily
available in primary care.

**Ultrasound limitations**

**-**
**Operator experience dependent**
   - Unable to penetrate **bone or air-filled structures** _(as they have very high_
_attenuation_ )

   Limited in patients with **obesity**, as there is very low penetrance and we can’t reach

to observe all the structures

*[PDF p. 77]*

#### Ultrasound workshop II

1st scenario

Parkinson’s patient is treated using _**HIFU**_ _(high intensity focused_
_ultrasound)._ It’s an example of the applications of ultrasound to
treatment. _**HIFU**_ makes it possible to destroy certain **brain cells**
responsible for **trembling in Parkinson's disease.**

Effect of US = to **increase temperature locally**, in order to destroy said part of the brain.
Patient’s head is surrounded by a hemisphere where **many many transducers** are located.
The **US waves join** at the centre part of the brain.

   _**What are the expected results when the US waves transduce?**_

Using a single transducer we would need a huge amount of intensity, which could potentially
damage all the crossed tissues; in addition, the intensity will decrease when it goes inside of
the patient, all along it’s path prior to reaching the centre of the brain. We would hit a whole
trajectory of tissue on the brain of the patient.
When we **combine many transducers** we reduce the intensity that needs to be generated
by each and can be sure that **only the central part will be affected** by the combination of

intensities.

Another problem: before reaching the brain, the US wave is directed onto the skull which will
absorb a large part of the energy and reflect it. Therefore, it’s temperature will increase. This
is why patients refer pain when inside the machine.
To solve this problem, the skull is recovered by a layer of water. The presence of water
serves to **match the impedances,** but also to **refrigerate the head of the patient.**

2nd scenario

We are presented with a **carotid artery stenosis.**
When **blood vessels narrow,** the **blood has to circulate faster** to make the same amount
of blood reach the tissues in the same amount of time as it used to _(smaller diameter =_
_increase the velocity of erythrocytes)._

In order to **diagnose** carotid artery stenosis, **velocity of blood cells** needs to be measured.

We take 2 measurements:

**-**
**Peak systolic velocity (PSV)**

**-**
**Diastolic velocity (EDV)**

If we modify the angle between the transducer and the vessel, the effect on PSV
and EDV is that their values will change. If we place the transducer in such a
way that the erythrocytes are travelling towards the transducer, we will observe

*[PDF p. 78]*

the Doppler shift phenomenon: it’ll be either **positive** (bottom) **or negative** (top), **depending**
**on v** **ult** **:** whether the erythrocytes are approaching or moving away from the transducer.

**If the measured velocity overgoes a threshold,** the **patient has stenosis**, which will be a
problem. Therefore, we can’t be playing with the angles of the transducer, as we will recover
different velocities which don’t correspond to the real velocity.
The velocity that we measure taking into account the Doppler effect is always smaller than
the real erythrocytr’s velocity. Therefore, if the measured value is higher than the threshold,
we can confirm that the real velocity is higher.

3rd scenario

A doctor wants to examine the heart of a patient using US _(echocardiogram)._ But the **heart**
is **surrounded by bone, air in the lungs, soft tissue,...**
We need to cross the different tissues to reach the heart. Therefore, we’ll have to decide
where the best location to place the transducer is, in order to obtain an image of the heart.
The best: **in between the ribs, pushing against the skin** of the patient, to reach the heart
and be as close as possible.

*[PDF p. 79]*

#### Dosimetry workshop

1st scenario

Man suddenly fell ill due to a **polonium** intoxication, which decays through **alpha decay.** It's
very toxic due to its ionizing capacity, and as it’s soluble in water, can cross through
biological membranes.

_How much of the energy released by the material was absorbed by this man?_

Because of the size of the alpha particles, specific properties appear in the case of alpha
particles, as well as for protons. The whole amount of energy released inside the body is
absorbed, as it is found inside the body.

An external alpha source producing 5 MeV can seem to be much more dangerous as the
energy per decay is higher. However, that is not correct. An external alpha source isn’t
dangerous as this radiation has to cross a given amount of air + the outer skin. So all the
energy is absorbed before the radiation reaches the tissues, on the surface. Nothing will
penetrate.
**Alpha sources are dangerous when ingested and introduced inside the body.** From
here, the **whole of the energy released will be absorbed.**

2nd scenario

   - **Increasing the intensity** (electric current, mAmp) **increases the number of**
**electrons released,** and therefore, the number of photons.
   - The **average energy per photon** only depends on the **voltage value.** This
increases the probability of radiation, not the effect per photon.

_What will be the effect on the absorbed dose (dose=energy per mass)?_
The absorbed dose will increase, as we have more photons and the **energy absorbed is**
**proportional to the number of photons.**

3rd scenario

Lead apron = 0.25 mm of lead which attenuates approx 90% of the incoming radiation.
Mainly designed for **x-ray beams of 100KeV.** For this case, the lead apron is effective. Even
if it doubles the time of exposure _(dose increases with time of exposure)_

However, in the case of **PET, 511keV photons** are released. Therefore the lead apron has a
thickness of 4mm in PET scanning. This means it is not truly effective _(time-thickness wise)_

Therefore, we will have to use **other alternatives to reduce the absorbed energy.**

*[PDF p. 80]*

Factors that affect the absorbed dose

   - Time _(try to optimize the time spent in the room. Effective personnel training)_

   Amount of energy

   - Distance to the source

   Increase thickness of shielding _(we can increase the shielding, in case of PET not of_
_the apron, but of the walls in the room)_

4th scenario

**PET scans** release **511 keV photons;** therefore, they are found in the **Compton region.**

In **X-ray CT** we try to prevent Compton scattering _(as we are interested in retrieving quality_
_images)_ and therefore use **energies below the 30 Kev limits.**

For **radiotherapy**, we are interested in the radiation reaching all of the tissues with tumors.
We are interested in **penetrating** all the structures, therefore we will use **energies in ranges**

**between 30 keV and 20 MeV.**

**COMPTON SCATTERING**

   Occurs at all energies ( = Led grid) but is more predominant at high energies

   It increases patient dose (as it causes more ionization)

   Decreases image contrast, thus decreasing the quality

*[PDF p. 81]*

#### FIRST WEEKS OF DEVELOPMENT

*[PDF p. 82]*

#### Gametogenesis

**What are gametes?**

**Gametes** are highly specialized sex cells, born only for **reproductive purposes** . If not
fertilized, they degenerate in 24h (oocytes) or 2-3 days (sperm) after being produced. They
are **haploid cells.**

**Where do they come from?**

Gametes originate from **primordial germ cells** .
**PGCs** are diploid cells destined to perpetuate the species, giving rise to
gametes. These are the only cells that undergo **meiosis.**

**Gametogenesis** consists in the conversion of the PGCs into male or
female gametes.

**Where does gametogenesis take place?**

Gametogenesis occurs in the **sex glands** or
**gonads.**
During the end of the 3rd week of gestation, PGCs
originate in the wall of the **yolk sac** and migrate to
the gonads in the second month of gestation.
This occurs both in males and females. In males,
these PGCs remain inactive until puberty

**SPERMATOGENESIS**

Spermatogenesis begins at **puberty** in the **seminiferous tubules of the testis.**

_- If we did a cross-section of a sex cord in a_

_neonate male, we would observe inactive_
_**primordial germ cells (spermatogonia)**_
_surrounded by_ _**sertoli cells**_ _and a basement_

_membrane._

_- If we did a cross-section of the testis during_
_puberty, we would observe that these_ _**tubules**_
_**acquire a lumen.**_ _Spermatogonia begin dividing_
_and sperm are released and transported through_

_the tubules._

*[PDF p. 83]*

**Spermatogenesis** entails two events:

   - **Genetic reduction of PGCs:** from the **primary spermatocyte,**
there are 2 meiotic divisions which give place to **4 spermatids.**
   - **Spermiogenesis:** process by which spermatids mature into **4**
**spermatozoa.**

**How does spermatogenesis occur?**

Spermatogenesis is characterized by **3 specific functional phases:**

**1.** **Mitotic proliferation:** from 1 first **spermatogonium**, there are
successive mitotic divisions. But these cells remain **connected**,
and we obtain a **synchronic progeny** _(they all began dividing at_
_the same time and will continue to do so, synchronously)._
**2.** **Meiosis I and II,** through which primary and secondary
spermatocytes give **early spermatids** _(still joined together)._
**3.** **Differentiation / Spermiogenesis,** phase in which spermatids
**mature** into **spermatozoa** . In the process of _spermiogenesis:_

               - Nuclear condensation occurs

               - Acrosome formation

                - Formation of neck, middle piece and tail

                - Release of cytoplasmic material

_10% of ejaculated cells are grossly_
_abnormal. There are problems when_
_this percentage goes over 20%_

                  - This whole process takes around **64 days.**

                            - It **begins at puberty** and it’s a **continuous** process,

which continues until the death of the individual.

                  - Spermatogenesis is **coordinated radially** within the
tubule cross-section. We can find spermatogonia at
**different stages of development.**

*[PDF p. 84]*

**Blood-testis barrier**

An important function of **sertoli cells** is the **formation of the**

**blood-testis barrier.**

They form a physical barrier that **isolates** meiotic and post-meiotic germ
cells **from immune and lymphatic systems**, protecting them.
This is done because, as they are different from the rest of the cells of the
body, they could be recognized as foreign cells and be attacked.

   - **Sertoli cells:** create the **blood-testis barrier** and **favour**

**spermatogenesis** _**(FSH)**_

   **Leydig or Interstitial cells:** located in the space between the
tubules. Cells responsible for the production of **testosterone** _**(LH)**_

**How is spermatogenesis controlled?**

It’s controlled by the **anterior pituitary gland,** through **FSH** _**(follicle-stimulating hormone)**_
and **LH** _**(luteinizing hormone)**_ . This gland is stimulated by the **Hypothalamus.**

   - **FSH** is going to act on **sertoli cells,** making them promote spermatogenesis by
activating spermatogonia.

   - **LH** acts on **leydig cells,** which will start producing testosterone. Testosterone will
also reinforce spermatogenesis.

*[PDF p. 85]*

**OOGENESIS**

**Oogenesis** takes place in the **ovaries** _(which are the female gonads/sex glands)._

**Oogenesis vs spermatogenesis**

Same as in spermatogenesis, oocytes are derived from **Primordial Germ Cells** _**(oogonia).**_
Oogonia, same as sperm, originates in the wall of the yolk sac. They migrate towards the
gonads in the 2nd month of gestation.
But in contrast with males, **oogonia become very active** and start dividing by **mitosis**
during the 5th month of gestation.

Many PGCs are lost during development. If
PGCs don’t reach the undifferentiated gonad,

and instead reach and accumulate in other

tissues, they give rise to **bizarre tumors**
known as **teratomas** (as they are rapidly
dividing cells).

Contrarily to spermatogenesis, **from each primary oocyte** _(coming from oogonium),_ only
**one secondary oocyte** is obtained, and from there, only **1 mature oocyte** is formed if
oogenesis is completed _(formation of polar bodies)._

**How does oogenesis occur?**

**1.** **Before birth, oogonia proliferate** and **peak by mid-gestation.** Some of
them will enter oogenesis and differentiate into **primary oocytes** which

enter meiosis I.

But they will remain **arrested in diplotene of prophase I** until puberty.

The **rest of oogonia** which don’t differentiate, **degenerate before birth.**

*[PDF p. 86]*

**Primary oocytes** arrested at prophase I are **surrounded by**
**follicular cells** _(sustentacular cells),_ found forming **primordial**

**follicles.**

A primordial follicle contains the nucleus of the primary oocyte + flat

follicular cells.

**Ovaries** at birth only contain a **given number of oocytes,** arrested in
meiosis I. Only a few of them will undergo ovulation, and the rest will

die.

**2.** From **infancy to puberty,**
primary oocytes remain **arrested in**
**diplotene of prophase I.**

**3.** **Oogenesis is resumed at**
**puberty.** But it doesn’t continue as a
continuous process, but a **cyclic** one:

_A few follicles start maturating, and_
_**only 1 primary follicle will obtain**_
_**maturity.**_ _From puberty to_
_menopause, every_ _**28 days**_ _, one_
_follicle fully matures and_ _**resumes**_
_**meiosis I, entering meiosis II**_ _where_
_it once again_ _**becomes arrested**_ _until_

_fertilization._

**4.** If **fertilization** occurs, the

secondary oocyte **continues meiosis**
**II** until it gives place to the **mature**
**oocyte.**

**THE OVARIAN CYCLE**

The ovarian cycle makes reference to
changes taking place in the ovary.

   - Maturation of follicle

   Ovulation and oocyte release

   Corpus luteum

   Degeneration of corpus luteum

*[PDF p. 87]*

**1.** **Follicular phase**

This development and resumption of oogenesis begins at **puberty.**
It involves the formation of the primary follicle, granulosa cells, the zona pellucida, the

formation of an antrum and theca cells.

**●** **Formation of the primary follicle**

Flat follicular cells of the primordial follicle become

**cuboidal follicular cells.** These stratified cuboidal

follicular cells, known as **granulosa cells**, form a
**several layer membrane** .
**The zona pellucida** also arises _(a membrane of_
_glycosaminoglycans and glycoproteins which separates_
_the oocyte from the granulosa cell layer)_ **.**
This gives place to the **primary follicle.**

**●** **Formation of secondary follicle**

Under the action of **FSH** _(pituitary g.)_, several changes take place in
the follicle: a cavity **(Antrum)** forms around granulosa cells and **fills**
**with liquid**, pushing the granulosa cells. The primary oocyte
becomes displaced and pushed onto the wall of the zona pellucida.
As the antrum evolves, it gives place to the structure of the
**secondary follicle.**
**Theca cells** are found surrounding granulosa.

**●** **Formation of tertiary follicle or Graff follicle**

The secondary follicle **becomes mature at midcycle,** forming the
tertiary follicle. **Theca cells** form a **bilayer** _(theca-interna and_
_theca-externa)_ around the granulosa cells. **Theca-interna** cells produce
**precursors of estrogens**, which are transported to the granulosa cells
and are transformed into **estrogens.**

_* During this whole maturation, the oocyte is still arrested at diplotene of prophase I (primary_
_oocyte). It enters meiosis II when released from the follicle during ovulation._

Functions of the follicle

   - Production of hormones _(estrogens and progesterone (during luteal phase))_

   Enabling ovulation, as it expels the oocyte out of the ovary when the time comes

*[PDF p. 88]*

**2.** **Ovulation**

Ovulation **takes place at mid-cycle (day 14).**

The secondary oocyte which is arrested at metaphase II, is
expelled at day 14 during ovulation.

**Granulosa cells and theca cells**, which have been left
behind, undergo morphological changes under the **influence**
**of LH** : they are converted into **secretory cells** known as
**luteal cells,** which will now mostly secrete **progesterone.**
_(In addition to still secreting small amounts of estrogen, they_
_secrete lots of progesterone)._
Together, these luteal cells from the **corpus luteum.**

   - In the **1st half** of the ovarian cycle: primarily **estrogen**
   - In the **2nd half** of the ovarian cycle: mainly **progesterone**

**3.** **Luteal phase**

These **luteal cells** form a body known as the **corpus luteum**, which is
released at the same time as the secondary oocyte.
It’s made up of secretory cells whose function is to secrete
**progesterone.**
It finally degenerates if not fertilized, and the process is restarted.

Only if the secondary oocyte is
fertilized, it will complete meiosis II.

**Characteristics of ovulation**

The expulsion of the oocyte is the result of **intrafollicular pressure** and
possibly, the **contraction** of smooth muscle in the **theca externa** .

   It’s a hormone-mediated process (this stage is regulated mainly by
LH)

   The LH surge resumes meiosis I and the secondary oocyte enters
meiosis II, where it is arrested at metaphase

   Ovulation consists in the release of this 2ary oocyte

*[PDF p. 89]*

**How is oogenesis controlled?**

The **hypothalamus** acts on the **anterior pituitary gland,** promoting the release of **FSH** and

**LH.**

  - The amount of **FSH** is larger in the **1st half**
**of the cycle.**
FSH stimulates granulosa cells in the ovarian
follicles to synthesize **aromatase**, which
converts androgens produced by the theca
cells to estradiol, to produce **estrogen.**
As the levels of estrogen increase in the
blood, it has **negative feedback** on the
**pituitary glands.**
This is so until estrogens peak and acquire a
**certain concentration in plasma**
**(200 pg/mL)** around days 12-14. _(This high_
_amount of estrogens informs the pituitary that_
_the oocyte is ready to be released)._
From this threshold, estrogen creates a **positive feedback** and **induces the**
**pituitary to increase** the **amount of FSH, but mainly LH.**

The increased amounts of **LH favour the resumption** of meiosis and therefore

**induce ovulation.**

  - The amount of **LH** increases mainly in the **2nd part of the**
**cycle** .
It acts on luteinized theca and granulosa cells which will
begin to secrete **progesterone.**
The high amounts of LH produced _(LH surge)_ after the
estrogen threshold has been surpassed, trigger ovulation.
**Ovulation** normally occurs **24 to 36 hours after the LH**

**surge.**

_Estrogens and progesterone both act outside of the ovary, but not_
_on the ovary. They act via the vascular system. FSH and LH act on_
_the oocyte and structures which contain it._

*[PDF p. 90]*

**THE MENSTRUAL CYCLE**

It makes reference to the cyclic changes that take place in the **endometrium,** produced by
**estrogens** and **progesterone** .
Cyclic changes in the endometrium are accompanied by changes in the uterine tubes,
vagina and cervical mucus. This is controlled by estrogens and progesterone:

   **Estrogen** promotes proliferation and cell growth _**(proliferative phase)**_

   **Progesterone** increases secretory activity _**(secretory phase)**_

➢ **Days 4-14.** _**Proliferative phase**_

After the previous ovulation, from day 4 to day 14, the endometrium has to be
**regenerated** . This is **stimulated by estrogen,** which induces **proliferation and renewal.**

This is known as the _**proliferative phase:**_

   Epithelial cells grow and proliferate

   Blood vessels extend and accumulate blood in case there is implantation

   Glands are formed which secrete glycogen to nurture the future embryo

   Watery cervical mucus _(penetrable)_

➢ **Days 14-21.** _**Secretory phase**_

**Progesterone** is released, glycogen accumulates and blood vessels continue to grow
_**(secretory phase)**_ **:**

   Increases secretory activity

   There is less contractility of the uterine tubes
   - Cervical mucus becomes thick and non elastic _(non-penetrable, to protect the_
_oocyte)_

➢ **Day 25.** _**Menstruation**_

If there has been **no fertilization,** the **corpus**
**luteum** degenerates and **stops secreting**
**progesterone.** Therefore blood vessels strat
becoming necrotic and constrict due to the lack
of progesterone, which is the hormone which

maintains the endometrium.

*[PDF p. 91]*

#### Fertilization

Fertilization **marks day 1 of** the **life** of a human being.

**FERTILIZATION PROCESS**

**Fertilization** consists in the **fusion of gametes** in the **ampulla** of the uterine tube.

PHASE 1. PENETRATION OF THE CORONA RADIATA

   - The **secondary oocyte arrested at**
**metaphase II** is **released on day 14.**

   - There is activity from the **fimbriae** in the
tube, finger-like projections which capture
the released oocyte through.

   Meanwhile, after sexual intercourse,

sperm enter through the vagina and into

the uterus.

   - The **vagina** provides a **very acidic environment,** which is incompatible with sperm.
Therefore, sperm are released along with **semen**, which **alkalizes the pH** so sperm

can survive.

   Sperm make their way to the uterine tubes. **During this journey** they undergo a
process known as **capacitation** : it consists in the **removal of some glycoproteins**
which, if present, don't permit fertilization.
**Only capacitated sperm will be able to fertilize the oocyte.**

   - The few sperm which reach the oocyte **penetrate the corona radiata** and reach the
zona pellucida.

PHASE 2. PENETRATION OF THE ZONA PELLUCIDA

   Sperms need to penetrate the strong barrier
which is the **zona pellucida.** This is achieved
through **binding in a ligand-receptor fashion**

   - **Molecules on the surface of the sperm** are
specific binding sites for the **ZP3** ( _zona_
_pellucida-3)_ **receptors** found on the **zona**
**pellucida.**

*[PDF p. 92]*

This ligand-receptor reaction **(acrosome reaction)** triggers the **breakage of the**
**membrane which contains the acrosome** and the **release of enzymes** such as
_**acrosin**_, which carry out extracellular **digestion of the zona pellucida.**

PHASE 3. FUSION OF THE SPERM AND OOCYTE MEMBRANES

   - After digestion of the zona pellucida, the two **membranes contact one another and**

**fuse.**

   - This **fusion** causes a **weak membrane depolarization** and leads to a **calcium**
**wave** that triggers **resumption of meiosis II.**

   **In the oocyte** there are enzymes contained in vesicles, known as **cortical granules.**
The induced calcium wave also triggers a **cortical reaction**, the fusion of these
cortical granules with the oolemma _(oocyte membrane)_

   - The **cortical granules release their content,** and degrade and inhabilitate all
receptors to **prevent other sperms from entering.** This is known as the **zona**

**reaction.**

   - The **zona reaction** hydrolyzes ZP3 receptors, **blocking** the possibility of
**polyspermy.**

RESUMPTION OF MEIOSIS II

   - The **entry of sperm** resumes **meiosis II**,
which is quickly completed (giving place to
Polar body II)

   - **Only the sperm’s nucleus enters** and
fuses with the oocyte’s nucleus. This gives
place to a unicellular embryo, known as
the **pronuclei stage zygote.**

   - Female and male pronuclei **duplicate** their
nucleus and carry out the **first mitotic**
**division,** giving place to the **two-cell stage zygote** .
These are the **first two cells** of the future organism, known as **blastomeres.**

*[PDF p. 93]*

This whole process, from fertilization till the formation of the two-cell stage zygote, **takes**
**around 30 hours.** If there’s no fertilization, the oocyte usually **degenerates after 24h** .

**The main outcomes of fertilization are:**

   - The **resumption** of **meiosis II** in the oocyte

   The formation of a 2 cell-stage embryo, known as **zygote**
   - Restoration of **diploid** number of chromosomes
   - Formation of a new individual with **unique genetic content**

   **Determination of the sex of the embryo** _(XX o XY, at the 2 cell stage)_

**FIRST WEEK OF DEVELOPMENT**

During the first week of development, the zygote goes from the two-cell stage to the
blastocyst stage through **cleavage**, and begins the process of **implantation** .

**Cleavage** consists in a series of mitotic divisions which gradually reduce the size of
embryonic cells _(blastomeres)_ until the **normal size** seen in somatic cells is **recovered** . The
**zona pellucida compresses** the dividing cells into a reduced space, forcing them to regain

their normal size.

FORMATION OF THE BLASTOCYST

   First 30h: 2-cell stage. The **zygote** travels along the
tubes to the uterus for its implantation. During this
process, it undergoes **cleavage** and segmentation.

   - The zygote divides _(4 cell stage, 8 cell stage,...)_ until
the **Morula** is formed on **day 4.**

   - **Early blastocyst:** around **days 4-5,** fluid enters
the morula and forms a cavity **(blastocystic**
**cavity),** which compresses the cells and forms the
early blastocyst. At this point, we find an **external**
**layer** of cells surrounding the cavity, and a
compressed **inner mass.**

*[PDF p. 94]*

- **Late blastocyst:** the early blastocyst **releases the zona pellucida,** in order to
implant into the endometrium. In this way, the late blastocyst is formed. **Only a**
**blastocyst released from the zona pellucida can achieve implantation.**

At this point, the inner cell mass is known as the **embryoblast** _(which will give place_
_to the embryo)_ and the outer layer of cells is known as the **trophoblast** _(will be in_
_charge of forming structures which nurture the embryo)._

BEGINNING OF IMPLANTATION

**Implantation** begins during **day 5-6** after fertilization. In other words, between **days 20-23**
**of the menstrual cycle.** The monolayer of flat epithelial cells in the outer cell mass
(trophoblast) will differentiate upon implantation, into:

   **Cytotrophoblast** _(epithelium, seen in light green)_

   **Syncytiotrophoblast** _(syncytium, seen in dark green. Formed by many dividing cells_
_which do not achieve complete cytokinesis; therefore, they form a mass with many_
_nuclei floating in one big cytoplasm)_ . This structure has a **highly invasive power**
and starts eroding the endometrium, **enabling implantation.**

*[PDF p. 95]*

**Ectopic pregnancies**

An ectopic pregnancy occurs when a fertilised egg implants
itself **outside of the womb** . For example, in the fallopian
tubes, abdominal cavity, cervix,...

**●** **Tubal pregnancy:** the embryo begins to develop in
the ampullary region (uterine tube). It normally
ruptures during the 2nd month of development and

has to be removed.

  - **Rectouterine pouch pregnancy:** rarely, the oocyte can
escape fimbriae in the tube during its release and the
fertilized embryo implants and develops in the
rectouterine pouch, the lowest point of the abdominal
cavity _**(Douglas pouch).**_
It’s a pregnancy of risk, but it can come to term.

*[PDF p. 96]*

#### 2nd week of development

The main event of the 2nd week is **completion of implantation.**

The 2nd week of life corresponds to days
21-28 of the endometrial cycle. This is why
each cycle has a duration of around 28
days: it allows time for implantation to

occur.

If there were no sign of implantation,
menstruation would happen.
Implantation takes place by attaching to the
endometrium at the **embryonic pole:** the
blastocyst leaves the uterine cavity and
**penetrates into the endometrium.**

**8th DAY OF DEVELOPMENT. BILAMINAR DISC STAGE**

In the late blastocyst which begins implantation, we can
differentiate an inner cell mass _(embryoblast)_ and an
outer cell mass _(trophoblast, which is divided into_
_cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast)._ On the **8th day**
of embryonic development, the embryo enters the
**bilaminar disk stage.** This bilaminar structure is formed
in the embryoblast, and is composed of the **Hypoblast**
