layer and the **Epiblast** . The **amniotic cavity** also arises
between the epiblast and the cytotrophoblast along with
**amnioblasts,** flat cells which are found surrounding the
amniotic cavity **.** Many more changes continue to take
place in the trophoblastic tissue.

The corpus luteum knows implantation has begun because
the embryo releases a hormone by day 9-10, called **human**
**chorionic gonadotropin (HCG)**, the same hormone tested
for the ascertainment of **pregnancy** ( _by the end of the 2nd_
_week, this hormone is found in high enough quantities as to_
_be detected)_ . This signal reaches the corpus luteum and
makes it remain active, releasing progesterone. Luteolysis
does not occur and gestation may proceed.
The corpus luteum **works until the 4th month of**
**gestation.** From there, the placenta takes over.

*[PDF p. 97]*

**9th DAY OF DEVELOPMENT**

On the 9th day, a **fibrin coagulum** forms to prevent the embryo from leaving the

endometrium.

Within the Syncytiotrophoblast, spaces form
and fuse together forming larger cavities,
known as **trophoblastic lacunae.**
In the embryoblast, cells coming from the
hypoblast begin to migrate, forming a thin

membrane which covers the inner surface of

the cytotrophoblast. This membrane is known
as the **exocoelomic membrane,** which lines
the blastocyst cavity. This lined cavity becomes
known as the **exocoelomic cavity.**

The exocoelomic membrane, together with cells of the hypoblast, form
the **primitive yolk sac** _(which is the site of PGC formation)._ By day 9,
the blastocyst is completely embedded in the uterus wall.

**12th DAY OF DEVELOPMENT**

In relation to the trophoblast: lacunae in the
syncytiotrophoblast continue to expand and
fuse forming **lacunar networks.** At that
time, capillaries in the endometrium dilate,
forming maternal sinusoids.
The syncytiotrophoblast finally erodes the
wall of the endometrium, reaching
**maternal sinusoids** and **uterine glands**
and allowing **maternal blood** and uterine
secretions (glycogen and proteins) to flow
into the lacunar network. Through
**diffusion,** they will **exchange gases and**
**metabolites** with the embryo _(these_
_lacunae are more prominent near the_
_embryonic pole)._
This is the beginning of **uteroplacental circulation** or maternal circulation.

Around the same time, a new population of cells appear between the
inner surface of the cytotrophoblast and the primitive yolk sac
_(exocoelomic membrane),_ known as the **extraembryonic mesoderm** .
It’s a membrane formed by loose connective tissue, found intervening
**between the trophoblast and embryoblast.**
As it forms, spaces/vacuoles also begin to appear within this
extraembryonic mesoderm. Spaces known as **extraembryonic cavities.**

*[PDF p. 98]*

**13th DAY OF DEVELOPMENT**

On the 13th day of development, extraembryonic cavities found
within the extraembryonic mesoderm gradually fuse to form one
large cavity known as the **chorionic cavity** or extraembryonic
cavity. Therefore, the original blastocyst cavity **(blastocele)** now
**becomes known as coelom,** surrounded by the chorionic cavity.
It divides the extraembryonic mesoderm into 2 regions: one
surrounding the trophoblast **(parietal or** _**somatic)**_ and another
lining the yolk sac **(visceral or** _**splanchnic**_ **)** _**.**_

Around day 13, a large portion of the exocoelomic cavity is pinched,
forming a smaller remaining cavity, now known as **secondary yolk**
**sac** _(smaller than the primary)._

The part of the membrane found binding the
embryoblast to the cytotrophoblast is known as the
**connective salk** _(the future umbilical cord)._

Additionally, at this stage the cytotrophoblast
penetrates into the syncytiotrophoblast and forms
villi-like structures, known as **primary villi.** The
extraembryonic mesoderm also begins to
penetrate into the cytotrophoblast forming
finger-like projections.

**DECIDUALIZATION REACTION**

When the blastocyst is found borrowed into
the endometrium, the endometrial wall
becomes known as the **decidua** _(decidua_
_basalis, capsularis and parietalis)._
This occurs because implantation of the
blastocyst induces decidualization of the
endometrial stroma, through the **decidual**

**reaction.**

This decidual reaction **(decidualization)**

consists in the transformation of endometrial

cells into specialized cell types called
**decidual cells,** to provide **nutrition and**
**immunological protection** to the
conceptus _(accumulate lipids and glycogen)._

*[PDF p. 99]*

- **Why isn’t the embryo rejected and perceived as a foreign structure?**

Because the syncytiotrophoblast _(found surrounding and protecting the embryo)_ expresses
specific antigens known as **human leukocyte antigen-G (HLA-G),** which act as a ligand to
leukocytes and inhibit them. They downregulate the maternal immune response.

Implantation window: limited time span when blastocyst competence is at its priming point.

**●** **Dating of pregnancy**

        **Fertilization age:** date pregnancy from the time of conception/fertilization

        **Menstrual age:** dates pregnancy from the woman’s last menstrual period

A normal pregnancy would be **38 weeks** after fertilization and **40 weeks** after the onset of
the last normal menstrual period.

**ABNORMAL SITES OF IMPLANTATION**

The **most frequent** abnormal site of implantation is the **uterine tube.** Tubal pregnancies
Tubal pregnancies aren’t seen to term, and the uterine tube ruptures by the 2nd month of
gestation.

Other abnormal pregnancies can occur due to an
**abnormal placenta.** One example of this is
**“Placenta previa”,** where the placenta develops in
the lowest part of the endometrium, **covering the**
**cervix.** Bleeding often happens as the lower part of
the uterus thins during the third trimester of
pregnancy. This causes the area of the placenta over
the cervix to bleed. The **risk of bleeding** is **higher** if
a lot of the placenta covers the cervix, as is the case
of placenta previa. There is much more bleeding than
there would normally be and this **leads to**
**hemorrhage.**

**●** **Hydatidiform moles**

Hydatidiform moles are **abnormal blastocysts**
with **no embryonic tissue.** However, the
**trophoblast develops** and forms a placenta.
Most often, a hydatidiform mole comes from an
abnormal fertilized egg that develops into a
hydatidiform mole rather than a fetus _(a_
_condition called molar pregnancy)._ However, a
hydatidiform mole can develop from cells that
remain in the uterus after a miscarriage, a
full-term pregnancy, or a mislocated pregnancy
_(ectopic pregnancy)._

*[PDF p. 100]*

_Rarely, a hydatidiform mole develops when there is a living fetus. In such cases, the fetus_
_typically dies, and a miscarriage often occurs._

Women who have a hydatidiform mole feel as if they are pregnant. But because hydatidiform
moles grow much faster than a fetus, the abdomen becomes larger much faster than it does
in a normal pregnancy. Severe nausea and vomiting and vaginal bleeding are common. As
parts of the mole deteriorate, small amounts of tissue, which resemble a bunch of grapes,
may pass through the vagina.
The moles secrete **high amounts of hCG** _(as this hormone comes from the_
_syncytiotrophoblast),_ which can be detected for diagnosis.
About 80% of hydatidiform moles are not cancerous, but they **may become invasive** and
develop into **malignant tumors (choriocarcinomas)** which rapidly spread into the
lymphatic system and the bloodstream.

*[PDF p. 101]*

#### 3rd week of development

MAIN EVENTS

   **Gastrulation,** which involves the formation of the 3 germ cell layers which will give
rise to all of the tissues and organs in the body.

   **Neurulation,** which consists in the beginning of the formation of CNS (but won’t be
completed until the end of the 4th week).

**GASTRULATION**

In summary, **gastrulation** is the process by which the three **primary germ layers of the**

**trilaminar disc are formed.**

The first cells to invaginate through the primitive groove form the **definitive endoderm.** The
remaining cells of the epiblast are called the **ectoderm.** Cells that remain in the space
between ectoderm and definitive endoderm form a layer known as the **mesoderm.**

At the end of the 3rd week, the embryo will be known as **trilaminar disc.**

_**How does gastrulation occur?**_

**1.** Firstly, we have the **formation of the primitive streak**

   - On **day 15,** this new structure can be observed by
looking at the epiblast from above. It’s a groove that
initiates at the **caudal end** of the embryo. In the
bilaminar disc, the epiblast belongs to the dorsal
part of the embryo. As soon as the primitive streak
appears, the **cranial-caudal axis is defined,**
marking where the **tail and head** of the embryo will
be. In addition to this, we imminently know what is
**left** and what is **right** .
The **embryo is now totally oriented.** Thus, regions of the embryo **(all 3 axes)**
become **identifiable by day 15.**

*[PDF p. 102]*

- The streak protuberates outwards and produces an **elevation** known as **primitive**
**node** _(“the organizer'', it releases factors that will induce differentiation),_ which
possesses a **hole** known as the **primitive pit,** through which prenotochordal cells will
migrate to form the notochord.

   During gastrulation, **epiblast cells migrate toward the midline/primitive streak,**
detach from the epiblast and **invaginate.** They start to
pass underneath, giving rise to a new layer formed
between the epiblast and hypoblast.
**Epiblast cells are epithelial cells,** interconnected by
adhesion molecules. As they migrate, they **lose their**
**adhesion** molecules, change their shape and acquire
the ability to migrate, and thus **become mesenchymal**
**cells,** undifferentiated cells with proliferative capacity.

   From here, epiblast cells **expand as mesenchymal**
**cells** in this new layer between epiblast and hypoblast
and **form the mesoderm layer,** giving place to the

**trilaminar disc.**

The **mesenchyme** corresponds to embryonic loose connective tissue.

**2. Fate maps:** cells in specific **regions of the epiblast** and entering the primitive streak will
**form specific structures in the mesoderm.**

   - **Epiblastic cells** which migrate and invaginate **via the**
**primitive pit** will form the **axial mesoderm.** This axial

mesoderm will form the **notochord.**

The notochord serves as the **basis for the axial**

**skeleton.**

The formation of the notochord is very complex: cells
enter via primitive pit and extend cranially. The migrated
cells **fuse with the hypoblast,** forming the **notochordal**
**plate,** which eventually **detaches** from the hypoblast

and forms the **notochord.**

   - The **mesoderm expands** through the space between epiblast and hypoblast **except**
**for 2 regions:**

        - **Oropharyngeal membrane**, where there is **no**
**mesoderm.** Epiblast and hypoblast are directly
in contact, tightly together. This is the region

from which the **mouth** is formed.

        **Cloacal plate/membrane,** Once again, there is
**no mesoderm.** It gives place to the **anus,**
**urethra, urinary bladder and rectum.**

   At this point, we can also observe the **Allantois,** a region of protuberant **hypoblasts**
which will lead to the formation of part of the **bladder.**

*[PDF p. 103]*

The remaining mesoderm is the **paraxial and lateral mesoderm.**

        The cells that extend at both sides of the notochord, close to
the midline, will form the **paraxial mesoderm.**

        Cells closer to the caudal region form the **lateral plate**

**mesoderm**

        The epiblasts that enter at the caudal-most part become
**continuous with the extraembryonic mesoderm,** and

**contribute to its formation.**

**3. Formation of the endoderm: mesenchymal cells** also begin to **replace the hypoblast**
**cells**, forming a totally new layer known as **endoderm** _(definitive endoderm)._
As the mesoderm mesenchymal cells
migrate to replace those of the hypoblast,
they **differentiate** and once again become
**epithelial cells.**
Therefore, the initial epiblast cells will give
place to the endoderm and the mesoderm.
The **epiblast that remains** is known as the

**ectoderm.**

_**Cross section:**_

   - **Ectoderm** : skin, CNS, PNS, eyes, internal
ear, neural crest cells _(in addition, bones and_
_connective tissue of the face and part of the_
_skull)_
   - **Mesoderm** _(+ notochord, which is part of the_
_mesoderm):_ bones, connective tissue,
urogenital system and cardiovascular
system.
   - **Endoderm** : gives rise to the gut and mucosa
of the gut + gut derivatives (liver, pancreas, lungs,...)

PATHOLOGIES

**SIRENOMELIA:** caudal dysgenesis. Occurs due to a **lack of mesoderm in the**
**lumbosacral region,** which results in the **fusion of the limb buds,** accompanied
by other defects in the organs of the body. They generally die after birth.

**Tumors** associated with gastrulation include **SACROCOCCYGEAL**
**TERATOMA.** After gastrulation, the primitive streak usually regresseses,
but if it doesn’t totally disappear, **remnant pluripotent cells** can give rise to

these **teratomas.**

*[PDF p. 104]*

GENETIC REGULATION OF LATERALITY

There is a laterality in the body that comes about due to the expression of signalling
molecules. The **expression of Nodal** on the **left side** of the body regulates left-sidedness
and leads to a cascade of events that **positions the organs.**

**SITUS INVERSUS**

There is an inversion in the positioning of organs.

It can be a **partial situs inversus**, seen in only **one or a**
**few organs** positioned in the wrong side of the body
_(heart = dextrocardia, liver,..),_ or it can be a **complete**

**situs inversus.**

**Complete situs inversus** is more serious and involves
**left-right asymmetry** _(all the blood vessels and_
_organs)._ “20% of people with situs inversus also present
**KARTAGENER’S SYNDROME** _(respiratory system_
_problems + infertility, due to cilia malfunction in PCD)_

**NEURULATION**

Neurulation consists in the **formation of the neural tube.**

During the 3rd week of development, we can observe the

**initiation of neurulation.**

**Formation of the neural plate**

   - The **notochord** forms and starts secreting molecules

that stimulate the **conversion of surface ectoderm**

**into neural tissue** .

   These molecules act on the overlying ectoderm and induce the transformation of its
epithelial cells into **polygonal cells.** The cells **change their form.** As the notochord
grows, the area of influence is larger.

   - By the **end of the 3rd week,** as the embryo grows mostly cranially, the **surface**
**ectoderm is converted into neural tissue,** forming the **neural plate.**
_Why does only the surface ectoderm undergo neurulation? Due to back inhibition. An_
_inhibitor is being produced laterally, so the conversion is topped and restricted to a given_
_area. Combination of induction and inhibition patterns._

**Formation of the neural tube**

   Once the neural plate is formed, there is an increase in the height of cells,
forming a **thickened neural plate.**
   - The plate forms a **central neural groove** and **two lateral neural folds**

   The 2 folds elevate along the groove
   - The **two folds fuse.** Once this occurs, they **detach** from the overlying ectoderm
surface, becoming **invaginated.** The ectoderm is found surrounding the neural
tube, which will give rise to the skin

*[PDF p. 105]*

#### 4th week of development

_At the end of the 4th week, the embryo looks like:_

During the 4th week of embryonic development, **neurulation**
**is complete** and the embryo passes from being a fat
trilaminar disc to adopting a **3D structure.**

NEURULATION AT MID 3rd WEEK

At mid 3rd week _(18 days)_, the neural plate invaginates and the
folds fuse. However, this is a **gradual process:** the tube doesn’t
fuse and invaginate all at once. It **first fuses** and then
**progressively invaginates** during the 4th week.

NEURULATION AT THE END OF 3rd WEEK

_(Day 20)_

Many factors contribute to invagination, such as

somites.

Neurulation takes place while the **paraxial**
**mesoderm** becomes thick, differentiates and forms

compact segments known as **somites.**
Somites are independent, differentiated paraxial
mesoderm which **protrude and**
**press against the neural tube**
to favour **invagination.**

The formation of these structures

progresses along with
neurulation. The **protruding**
**paraxial structures grow in**

**number and increase** .

CLOSURE OF NEURAL TUBE DURING 4th WEEK

_(days 22 - 28)_
By the end of **day 28,** the **neural tube** is **totally**
**invaginated.**

*[PDF p. 106]*

FOLDINGS OF THE EMBRYO

At the same time, both **lateral and cranial-caudal**
**foldings of the embryo** take place. The embryo
undergoes rapid development in size and shape.

**●**
**Lateral body foldings**

The **lateral folding** is produced by the **rapidly growing spinal cord and somites.**

   - The **ectoderm** on the sides **extends ventrally.** The paraxial mesoderm begins to
form the **somites** while the neural tube continues to invaginate. The **yolk sac**
becomes thinner at the sides, and begins to **narrow**, forming what will be the gut

tube.

   - As the **amniotic cavity** and the **ectoderm** continue to **extend ventrally,** the
mesoderm differentiates into **somatic mesoderm** (in contact with the ectoderm) and
**splanchnic mesoderm** (surrounding remaining endoderm, which will form the gut).

   As the amniotic cavity continues to surround and approaches the yolk sac, a new
cavity arises: the **intra-embryonic body cavity.** The connection between gut and
yolk sac continues to get narrower and narrower. They will remain connected by a

canal known as the **vitelline duct.**

   - Eventually, the **amniotic cavity will surround the whole body of the embryo.** The
body will be immersed in that cavity, floating in **amniotic fluid.** Within the body of the
embryo, we find the intra-embryonic body cavity / intraembryonic coelom surrounding
the gut and yolk sac. The **gut** remains attached to the rest of the body by a stalk
known as the **dorsal mesentery** _(mesoderm)._
_Note that although not represented in all images, the ectoderm deriving from amnioblasts_
_still lines the inner face of the amniotic cavity, forming an epithelial layer (see picture at top of_
_the page)_

*[PDF p. 107]*

- **Cranial-caudal folds**

In the cranial-caudal folds, **head and tail move ventrally,** and the embryo will adopt a **“C”**
**shape.** As a result:

   - The **primitive heart** _(heart mesoderm)_ bends ventrally and moves towards the
midline, **compressing the yolk sac.** The **pericardial cavity** and **heart tube** also
begin to arise within the heart mesoderm.

   - **Neural tube** also bends **ventrally** _(anterior and posterior neuroscope)_

   - **Oropharyngeal membrane** _(the future_ _**mouth**_ _)_ bends and ends up being located in
the head; **cloacal membrane** is also invaginated at the caudal end

   - The **allantoid** is also **compressed anteriorly/ventrally** until it **presses against the**
**yolk sac.** The upper-most part will contribute to the formation of the urinary bladder,
and the lower part will insert into the connecting stalk/umbilical cord, known as the
urachus _(thick hollow tube which connects the primitive urinary bladder to the_
_umbilical cord)_

   - ‐
As the body folds progress, **part of the endoderm** **lined yolk sac** is incorporated to
the embryo as **gastrointestinal tract (gut)**

The **endoderm** will give rise to the **gastrointestinal tract.** As embryonic folding continues,
the endoderm moves towards the midline and fuses, incorporating the **dorsal part of the**
**yolk sac** to create the **primitive gut tube** . This tube differentiates into 3 main parts:

  - **Foregut** _(cranial end)._ Temporarily closed by the **oropharyngeal**
**membrane,** which at the end of the 4th week will rupture and give rise to

the **mouth** .

*[PDF p. 108]*

- **Midgut**, which lies in between and remains connected to the yolk sac until
the 5th week of development. As embryonic development continues, the
connection to the yolk sac will narrow into a stalk known as the **vitelline**
**duct.** _Yolk sac remains connected through the midgut, which is why in some_
_cross sections (fore or hindgut) we only see gut tube._

- **Hindgut** _(caudal end)._ Temporarily closed by the **cloacal plate/membrane,**
until the 7th, when it ruptures to form the **urogenital and anal openings**
**(anus).**

➢ **Folding at the rostral end**

     The cardiogenic area is found anterior to the oropharyngeal membrane. Two
tubes in this bulge fuse to form the **primitive heart.** As soon as they fuse, the
**primitive heart starts beating. Originally anterior** to the oropharyngeal
membrane, but as craniocaudal folding proceeds, the heart is **displaced**
**ventrally.**
     - The **oropharyngeal membrane** will therefore be **anterior to the heart** as a
result of the folding.
     - The **yolk sac** _(umbilical vesicle)_ is incorporated into the embryo as the
**anterior wall of the primitive gut.**

➢ **Folding at the caudal end**

     - As craniocaudal folding proceeds, the **connecting stalk and allantois** are
displaced and compressed ventrally. They come together and press onto the
yolk sac, eventually forming the **definitive umbilical cord.**

*[PDF p. 109]*

FORMATION OF THE INTRAEMBRYONIC COELOM

**Spaces** begin to form within the **lateral mesoderm,** as the paraxial mesoderm gives rise to
the somites. These spaces become bigger and **fuse**, forming the **intraembryonic coelom**, a
flattened, horseshoe‐shaped cavity which will originate the definitive intraembryonic cavity.

   Upper view: the cavity extends in a **“U” shape** around the lateral sides of the embryo
_(seen from above)._ The upper coelom will become known as the **pericardial coelom**
and the centre-most, **peritoneal coelom** _(peritoneum = abdominal cavity lining)._

   - Seen in the cross-section: the intraembryonic cavity initially becomes continuous with
the extraembryonic cavity. As the folding progresses, the **lateral plate mesoderm**
splits into 2 layers:

        **Parietal (somatic) layer:** will be found lining the inside of
the ectoderm as the embryo closes. It will form underlying
tissue below the ectoderm, in other words, the dermis +

other structures such as muscles.

        - **Visceral (splanchnic) layer:** It’s continuous with the
somatic layer and is found surrounding the primitive gut
tube. Will give rise to the smooth muscle of the gut.

The **intraembryonic cavity** will correspond with the **abdominal and pericardial cavities.**
As the closing continues, the embryo closes on itself and the **continuity between intra and**
**extraembryonic cavity is lost.**
The **amniotic cavity** is now found **surrounding the whole body** of the embryo, which is at
the same time found within the chorionic / extraembryonic cavity.
The thin connection with the remnant of the yolk sac will become thinner and thinner and
eventually form a **ligament within the umbilical cord.**

EMBRYO AT THE END OF THE 4th WEEK

Lateral view of a 27-somite embryo,
approximately 26 days. The embryo is
curved, especially its **tail-like caudal**

**eminence.** We can observe the **lens**

**placode** and the **otic pit,** indicating **early**
**development of the internal ear.**

*[PDF p. 110]*

We can also see that the **rostral neuropore is closed** and that **three pairs of pharyngeal**
**arches** are present. They are transient structures which will give rise to others.

The **main features** of the embryo at this stage _(26-28 days dev)_ are
the three pairs of **pharyngeal arches** and the **somites.**

This 28-day embryo is **surrounded by the**
**amniotic cavity,** found floating in the
**chorionic/extraembryonic cavity** and
suspended by a ventrally located **umbilical**

**cord.**

OVERVIEW OF THE EMBRYO-FOLDING OUTCOMES

  - The **neural tube** becomes **fully**
**invaginated.**

        - rostral brain vesicles will give rise

to the brain

        - caudal neural tube will give rise to
the spinal cord

  - The **primordial heart** is found in a
**ventral** position in relation to the mouth

  - The **intraembryonic coelom** is formed

  - Connective stalk, yolk sac and allantois

form the **umbilical cord**

  - The **primitive gut** is fully formed

VENTRAL BODY WALL DEFECTS

They are caused due to **abnormal closure of**
**one or more of the body folds.**

**ECTOPIA CORDIS** **GASTROSCHISIS** **BLADDER EXSTROPHY**

*[PDF p. 111]*

#### Placentation

The Placenta is a highly specialized organ. It **nurtures**
**the fetus** when diffusion is no longer sufficient,
**protects it** and **secretes hormones,** as it takes over
the corpus luteum‘s function of progesterone secretion
to maintain the endometrium from degenerating.

   Fetal component: bushy chorion
   - Maternal component: decidua basalis

**VASCULAR SYSTEM**

_Going back to 2nd week embryo:_

By day 6, cells are already determined to form cells of
the body of the embryo or of the trophoblast.
The **extraembryonic mesoderm** and the **2 trophoblast layers** will form the **chorion.**
At the same time, at the end of the 2nd week **primary villi appear** _(prolongations of the_
_cytotrophoblast that penetrate into the syncytiotrophoblast)._

During the 3rd week of development, a first **vascular network is formed** in the **embryonic**
**and extraembryonic mesoderm.** It begins to form during the 3rd week to **connect**
**maternal vascularization with the embryo.**

**How does it form? Through vasculogenesis**

A set of mesenchymal condensations **(blood islands)** in the
**extraembryonic mesoderm, surrounding the yolk sac**,
differentiate into **hemangioblasts** under the effect of **FGF2**

and differentiate into:

   - **Epithelial cells**, that will give rise to **blood vessels.**

**-**
**Embryonic blood cells**

*[PDF p. 112]*

Blood vessels will grow through **angiogenesis** to form higher
networks of blood vessels. They appear in other regions such
as the **connecting stalk and the embryonic mesoderm.**
Within the **embryonic mesoderm,** they start to form at both
sides of the embryo. Initially form a **dorsal aorta**, from which
**primitive cardiac tubes arise and fuse** to form the **primitive**
**heart** by the 4th week. It starts to beat as soon as tubes fuse.

**Formation of Chorionic vessels**

_Changes in the chorion during the 3rd week:_

**●** **Primary villus:** Cytotrophoblastic **prolongations** towards the endometrium

  - **Formation of secondary villus:** during the 3rd week, **extraembryonic mesoderm**
**penetrates** within the **villus,** forming a **core of mesenchyme**, along with
**cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast layers.**

  - **Formation of tertiary villus:** days after, this **mesenchyme will differentiate** into
hemangioblasts to eventually form blood cells and vessels. These will become known
as **chorionic blood vessels,** as they arise from the chorion.

_Cross section:_

*[PDF p. 113]*

- We can also observe **other changes**, such as **separations between villi.**
Syncytiotrophoblast continues to erode and include **maternal blood vessels,** which
**surround** the villi and **increase the separation** between them.

  - **Formation of the cytotrophoblastic shell:**
cytotrophoblast cells continue to prolong and
**contact decidua basalis.** From here, they
extend to form the **cytotrophoblastic shell,**
which **anchors** the whole future **placenta into**

**the endometrium.**

Close to this shell, we find anchored villis
known as **anchoring villis.** On the other hand,
**free and unanchored villi** will expand and
branch a lot, and will constitute the site where

**exchange** takes place.

Within the **chorionic vessels,** we find:

        - **Chorionic arteries** = carrying out baby’s **deoxygenated blood**, which they
receive from the umbilical artery
        - **Chorionic veins** = bringing in **oxygenated blood,** draining it into the
umbilical vein _(note: the colour doesn’t signify the type of blood they carry,_
_just that they are arteries or veins)_

**Vascularization at the end of the 4th week**

A complex cardiovascular system is already formed and
working by the **end of the 4th week. Embryonic, umbilical**

**and chorionic vessels** are formed and the **cardiac tube is**

**beating by day 21** ‐ **22** of embryonic development.
It’s the **1st system to be functional.**

**PLACENTA**

As the embryo grows, the placenta becomes larger and
more efficient through a complex maturation.

Primary and secondary villi form all around the embryo,
but are more prominent and develop more at the
embryonic pole _(site where implantation started)._
By the **end of the 2nd month, chorionic villi**
concentrate in the **embryonic pole.**
The rest of the villi don’t develop much and finally
disappear; only the mentioned ones **fully develop into**
**the placenta.**

At the end of the 2nd month they concentrate mainly at
one point, and we can distinguish a **villish/bushy**
**chorion** and a **smooth chorion** _(depleted of villi)._

*[PDF p. 114]*

**Embryo-Endometrium relation**

During pregnancy, the **endometrium becomes decidualized.**

   - End of 2nd month: we can differentiate between **decidua basalis, capsularis and**
**parietalis,** separated by **uterine cavity.** The embryo is found inside the **amniotic**
**cavity,** which is at the same time within the **chorionic cavity.**

   - End of 3rd month: amniotic cavity/membrane becomes **fused** with the chorionic
membrane, and they join to form the **amniochorionic membrane**, which is now the
**only membrane which is found surrounding the embryo.**
This membrane **compresses against the decidua capsularis,** which degenerates,
and **expands towards the uterine cavity.** The embryo and amniotic cavity uptake
the uterine cavity and will continue to push up against other organs.

In this process, the **yolk sac and allantois**
become **totally lined by the amnion.** As
the amniotic cavity expands and obliterates
most of the extraembryonic coelom, the
**amnion forms the epithelial covering of**

**the umbilical cord.**

At the same time, **decidua basalis** ends up **penetrating** within groups of villi and
forms **decidual septa.** This maternal stroma penetrates and divides the villi into
several groups. These **rounded, isolated areas** are known as **cotyledons**, and give
place to the **mature placenta.**

*[PDF p. 115]*

Therefore, by the end of the 3rd month, the **chorion frondosum and**
**decidua basalis** are tightly associated, forming the **mature placenta.**

**Blood circulation in hemochorial placenta**

**Hemochorial placenta** _(human)_ = the **blood is in contact with the chorion**

   Anchoring villi = no exchange

   Free villi, expanding = exchange

The chorionic structure is found deep between **lacunae of maternal blood,** where maternal
blood vessels are draining blood. The maternal blood is **released at high pressures** into
these lacunae. It goes all the way down _(due to high pressure)_ and **returns slowly.** Slower
rate **allows the exchange of nutrients, excretion materials, CO2,..** so it slowly returns
deoxygenated.
Decidual septa doesn’t fully separate cotyledons. **Blood therefore also flows laterally,**
**between cotyledons.**

**Placenta-functions**

The placenta is the primary site of **nutrient and gas exchange** between the **mother and**

**the fetus.**

   Exchanges gas and nutrients

   - Protection

   - Metabolism

   Hormone secretion (hCG)

   - Excretion

**Maturation of placental villi**

Initially, separating maternal and fetal blood, we find **4 layers** forming tertiary villi.
**Syncytium, cytotrophoblast, connective tissue and endothelium.**

However, as the **placenta matures,** villi ramify, and these
**barriers become thinner and thinner** with time. Only a
few residual trophoblastic cells will remain: blood vessels
of the fetus end up being embedded in **2-layered mature**
**placental villi.** But the **2 bloods are never in direct**
**contact.** They are always well separated by the **placental**

**membrane.**

*[PDF p. 116]*

➢ _Complications. Placental membrane_

**Oxygen and other nutrients** can easily flow through this membrane. **IgG antibodies**
( _small)_ can also pass, and provide **some immunity** to the fetus.

However, **harmful substances** such as **drugs, poisons,... and microorganisms like**
**parasites and viruses,** can also cross this membrane.

   - If the mother has a **viral infection** _(ex: rubella or cytomegalovirus, parasites such as_
_toxoplasma gondii (toxoplasmosis),...),_ it can be **transmitted** to the embryo.

   - **Bacteria** generally can’t pass, but some break the placenta membrane and can

cross.

   - **Fetal RBC** generally don’t pass through the membrane; although some might pass at
the very end of gestation. If RBC from the fetus are **Rh+** and the **mother, Rh-,** this
RBC membrane-crossing induces a reaction and the **production of maternal**
**antibodies against fetal RBC.**
If, in the next pregnancy, she has another Rh+
baby, there will be a problem as the primary
exposure during the 1st pregnancy means the
mother has already developed antibodies. These

attack the RBC of the fetus. This condition is

known as **HEMOLYTIC DISEASE OF THE**

**NEWBORN** .

**MULTIPLE PREGNANCIES**

*[PDF p. 117]*

**BIRTH DEFECTS**

**Types of birth defects**

   - **Malformations** : occur during **organ formation** _(organogenesis, 3rd-8th weeks)_

   **Disruptions:** alterations in already formed structures _(vascular accidents, amniotic_
_bands)_
   - **Deformations:** mechanical forces which alter a structure ( _clubfeet)_

**Teratology** is the science that studies birth defects.
**Teratogens** = factors that cause birth defects. They can be:

   - Genetic factors _**(15%)**_
   - Environmental factors _**(10%)**_

**Causes of birth defects** : 15% are Genetic, 10% environmental, 20% multifactorial and

50-60% remain unknown.

**Genetic factors**

**1.** **Numerical anomalies** (aneuploidies correspond to
50% of spontaneous abortions. They are mainly
caused by nondisjunction during meiosis, wither in
meiosis I or II)

        - Turner 's syndrome _(X0, 45. It’s the only_
_monosomy compatible with life)_

’
        - Klinefelter s syndrome _(47, XXY)_

’
        - Down s syndrome _(trisomy 21)_

’
        - Edward s syndrome _(trisomy 18)_

**2.** **Structural anomalies** _(ex: microdeletions)_

’

        Angelman s syndrome _(microdeletion in the mother’s chromosome)_
        - Prader-Willis syndrome _(microdeletion in paternal chromosome)_

**Environmental factors**

Alcohol, Antiepileptic drugs, antibiotics _(streptomycin, tetracycline)_, Endocrine disruptors,
Radiation, Infections _(viruses: Rubella, Zyka, CMV; Toxoplasma gondii, Treponema pallidum_
_(syphilis)),_ Maternal diabetes, Obesity and Nutritional deficiencies.

**Most structural birth defects** are caused **early in gestation** (3 to 8 weeks), when organ
systems are forming and when most women don’t know they’re pregnant.

*[PDF p. 118]*

#### Development of the nervous system

**INTRODUCTION**

The nervous system is in charge of all the other systems, making it
possible for them to **work in unison towards a common goal.**
_How is this achieved?_ By being aware of the external environment and
internal environment. The nervous system is able to **gather information**
and **transmit/convey it via the nerves (PNS) to the main centre of**
**control (CNS, brain and spinal cord)** which **integrates** the info and
**elaborates the most appropriate response.**
There are different types of responses, but we will mainly focus on **motor**
**responses.** The response is sent to **effector organs.**

**Main parts of the Nervous System**

  - **Central Nervous System** (brain and spinal cord)

  - **Peripheral Nervous System** (mainly nerves, although we also have neurons).

Subdivided into:

        - Somatic: It’s related with the muscles and voluntary movement
        - Autonomous: visceral, non-voluntary movements

**Functional unit of NS**

Functional unit = the **nerve cell or neuron.**

**Santiago Ramón y Cajal** was the Spanish physician and early
neuroscientist who first discovered that the nervous system was **not**
**continuous** but was composed of **interconnected individual cells.** He

was awarded the **Nobel Prize in 1906.**

Nerve cells are the main signaling unit of the NS. **Made up of:**

**-** **Dendrites** (processes around cell body)

**-**
**Cell body**

**-** **Axon**

Incoming information reaches these processes
(dendrites), it’s integrated in the cell body and
the response is conveyed via the axon to the
next neuron or to a muscle cell (effector). This
is done through a **chemical synapse** _(release_
_of chemical signals)._

*[PDF p. 119]*

**Other parts of the NS:**

   - **Glial cells:** they outnumber the
nerve cells. These are **supporting**
**cells,** found around neurons within

nervous tissue.

_How do we define the incoming and exciting_

_information?_

   Incoming information = **perception**

   - Efferent information = **motor**

**FORMATION OF THE NERVOUS SYSTEM**

**Induction of the neural plate. Neurulation**

   - At the beginning of 3rd week, **notochord** forms from cells
migrating from the primitive streak through the primitive pit.
   - Ectoderm cells become **polygonal**, **thicken and extend**
**rostrally,** forming the **neural plate.**
   - A **neural groove** forms, the plate elevates forming **two**
**neural folds** which approach one another and end up
**fusning and invaginating, detaching from the overlying**
**ectoderm,** during the 4th week.

   The ectoderm becomes continuous again. Therefore, the
**origin of the neural tube is the ectoderm.**

It’s thought that the tube invaginates first at the rostral end, and
then caudally. The structures that remain open in the final stages
are the pores, at the rostral end and caudal end (known as
**anterior and posterior neuroscope** ). The caudal end finally
closes by the **end of the 1st month, when the neural tube**
**becomes fully invaginated.**

Others think that the closure takes place at **different places at the same time.**

*[PDF p. 120]*

**Neural tube defects at this stage**

  - **SPINA BIFIDA:** defect caused by neural tube not closing properly, hence the
**vertebral arch doesn’t correctly form.** This leads to different types of pathologies.
Defect in the vertebral arch (unfused): it's **not continuous,** has **two tips = bifida.**

        - **Spina bifida occulta:** In the **mildest** case, as it’s **asymptomatic** and doesn’t
really affect. There is incomplete closure but the tips are very proximate. The
site of defect is usually marked by a **tuft of hair.**

**Problems** come when the formation comes with lack of proper closure. The **content**
**of the central canal** _(spinal cord within the meninges)_ protrudes, since there is no
boundary.

        **Spina bifida cystica: cyst** formed by **protruding meningeal sac,** containing
**cerebrospinal fluid.** The meningeal sac is protruding towards the skin and
forms a **mass.** This situation, in which only the sac is protruding, is known as
**meningocele.**

        - **Meningomyelocele:** both the **meningeal sac and CNS structures** are found
displaced and disorganized, protruding outwards. Patients present more
**complications in walking,** etc, but it’s a situation **compatible with life.**

        - **Rachischisis:** the **whole neural tube is open** and **exposed to amniotic**
**fluid** during formation _(open spinal cord)_ . This situation is **incompatible with**

**life.**

_Neural tube defects at separate sites:_
_further evidence in support of_

_multi_ ‐ _site closure of the neural tube_

_in humans. Triple neural tube defects._

  - **ANENCEPHALY** : **cranial bones aren’t formed properly** . The **cerebral cortex**
**doesn’t form,** only the brain stem. The baby is born but dies hours later, as it is **not**
**compatible with life.**

To avoid these defects (Defects in the neural
tube closure, NTDs), women are given **folic**
**acid,** which has been seen to prevent to a
great extent neural developmental defects.

*[PDF p. 121]*

**1. CENTRAL NERVOUS SYSTEM (CNS)**

**The CNS derives from the neural tube.**

**Brain**

The anterior part is enlarged and expands, forming **brain vesicles** which will give rise to the

brain. **5 vesicles:**

   **Telencephalon**, will give rise to the **cerebral hemispheres (cerebrum)**
**-** **Diencephalon** = **thalamic** areas + **optic vesicle**
   - **Mesencephalon**, will give rise to the **midbrain**
   - **Metencephalon**, that in the adult will give rise to the **cerebellum**

**-**
**Myelencephalon,** which will give rise to the **medulla oblongata**

**Spinal cord**

**Neuroepithelial cells** form the **wall of the neural tube.** They derive from ectoderm cells
that invaginate and end up forming **pseudostratified epithelium, known as**
**neuroepithelium.**
From this point, cells start dividing and proliferating.
**3 stages in the formation of the spinal cord:**

**1.** **Proliferation**

   - Undifferentiated neural stem cells start dividing **close to the**
**lumen of the tube**, giving rise to **immature neurons**
**(neuroblasts)** that will eventually differentiate.

   The closely packed dividing cells form the **ventricular layer**
_(neuroepithelial layer),_ which is **pseudostratified** . Will be
found lining the cavities in the brain.

*[PDF p. 122]*

**2.** **Migration**

Cells in the ventricular zone stop dividing and begin to
**migrate** and leave the ventricular zone, forming a second
layer known as the **marginal layer.**

- As the **wall thickens,** an intermediate zone forms, known
as the **mantle layer.**

- The marginal zone is in contact with the somites _(forming the meninges),_ which limit
the amount of space available. Intermediate zone and marginal zone will quickly fill
the neural canal and start **differentiating into neurons and glial cells.**

This neural canal, which becomes known as the central canal, is lined by **ependymal**

**cells derived from the neural tube.**

- Once the mantle layer becomes more and more thick, **axons are formed** through

maturation.

- Cells **concentrate and mature in the mantle layer,** and once matured their **axons**
position themselves and flow towards the marginal layers _(cranially and caudally)._

Therefore:

     - **mantle layer** = we mainly find **cell bodies**
     - **marginal layer** = we mainly find **axons** . The marginal layer becomes **thicker**
at midgestation due to the **myelination** of the axons.

Neuroepithelial cells in the mantle layer will take on different roles depending on their
position relative to the **sulcus limitans:**

     cells posterior to the sulcus = **alar plate** → will become sensory neurons
     - cells anterior to the sulcus = **basal plate** → will become motor neurons

- During specialization and differentiation, alar plate will become known as the **dorsal**
**sensory horn** and the basal plate, as the **ventral motor horn**

- The cells in the **ventricular layer** which remain lining the ventricle will give rise to
**ependymal cells**

*[PDF p. 123]*

**3.** **Regional specialization-differentiation**

   The spinal cord has a **specific organization.**
**Notochord** secretes molecules (ex: SHH) and
**induces differentiation** in the lower part of the
spinal cord. Cells begin to express particular genes
and differentiate. It allows the cells to acquire the
particular shape and functions of neurons from that
particular region.

   - The **alar plate** of the mantle layer will give rise to
neurons with a **sensory function** _**(afferent).**_ The
**basal plate** will originate the ventral layer, which
will have a **motor function** _**(efferent).**_

   - **Motor neurons** from the motor horn will extend their axons (PNS) towards **muscles.**
**Sensory neurons** in the dorsal horn (CNS) will receive info from sensory neurons
that arise from neural crest cells _(see below, explained)._ These belong to the PNS
and will extend their processes out to the **skin** and other portions.
These neurons are **spinal nerves** and make up the **spinal system (PNS).**
We will also find **interneurons** that connect sensory to motor neurons, which will

allow us to have **coordinated reflexes.**

Many of the formed neurons enter **apoptosis**
and die, as to **make space for the**
**supporting cells** in the nervous tissue (glial
cells), which also arise from the
neuroepithelium

   - The **mantle layer** will become **grey matter** in the spinal cord
The **marginal laye** r will give rise to **white matter**

**2. PERIPHERAL NERVOUS SYSTEM (PNS)**

**Neural crest cells** will give rise to **dorsal root ganglia and sensory**

**neurons.**

They are a specific population of cells located **above the neural tube**
of the embryo. As the tube invaginates, they are found at the **tips** of
the neural folds. When the folds fuse, these **tips join and detach,**
**forming the neural crest.** The origin of these cells is therefore the
**ectoderm** (ectodermal cells).
These cells **become mesenchymal** _(ectoderm to mesoderm_
_transition)._ As they become mesenchymal, they **will give rise to many**
**cell types.** To such an extent that they are sometimes referred to as
the **4th germ layer.**

*[PDF p. 124]*

The most important type of cell type they give rise to are all the **sensory neurons** in the
body, those that receive information from the periphery.

All the soma of those sensory neurons are located within sensory ganglia **(dorsal root**
**ganglia, containing the soma).**
Sensory neurons are a special type of neuron: they have **two processes,** are
**pseudounipolar** _(Why pseudounipolar? because exiting the body we see only one process,_
_which then splits into two)._ They have:

   - A **central process** _(synapse region with sensory neurons in dorsal horn)_

   **Peripheral process** _(going towards the periphery, join motor fibers to form spinal_
_nerves)_

**Derivatives of neural crest cells**

Many other cell types are derived from neural crest cells:

   Melanocytes

**-**
**Cells from autonomous nervous system**

   **Sensory neurons** _(already mentioned)_
   - **Schwann cells** (form the myelin sheath in the PNS).
Oligodendrocytes myelinate axons in the CNS (1
oligodendrocyte can give rise to many internodes)
and Schwann cells myelinate axons in the PNS (1
SC per internode).

*[PDF p. 125]*

**Spinal nerves**

There are **2 ganglia,** one on each side at every segment of the spinal cord _(segments = sets_
_of neurons at each level of the spinal cord)._

The **spinal nerve** comprises:

   **The peripheral processes of sensory neurons** coming from many parts of the
body. Incoming neuron (sensory) = **dorsal or posterior root**
   - The **axons of motor neurons.** Exting (motor) = **ventral or anterior root**

Spinal nerves are **mixed**, containing **sensory fibers** _**(axons)**_ **and motor fibers.**

As they approach the spinal cord they **split,** as sensory neurons have
to reach the dorsal horn and motor neurons are exiting from the

ventral horn.

The spinal cord ends at the level of L3. But we also have the sacral
cord. They have to travel long distances to reach their corresponding
foramina and exit to innervate many parts of the body. This is why
**myelination** will be vital. Myelination starts around the **4th month of**
**gestation.**

*[PDF p. 126]*

#### Development of axial skeleton and muscles of the trunk

_All these structures are forming at the same time_

**INTRODUCTION**

The skeletal system is composed of **bones and joints.** It’s dense
connective tissue (bone tissue). Associated to tissues, cartilage and
ligaments.

   - **Axial skeleton** = vertebral column, ribs and cranium. The trunk

is ribs + spinal cord + sternum
   - **Appendicular skeleton** = the rest

**EMBRYONIC ORIGIN OF THE AXIAL SKELETAL SYSTEM**

_Metamerism or metamery: repetition of alike elements (segments)_

In the embryo, the **somites** form structures that **resemble the axial skeleton.**
They look like vertebrae. The **vertebrae will come from somites,** which are
derived from **paraxial mesoderm** in the embryo.
Therefore, mesenchyme giving rise to the axial skeleton of the trunk arises mainly
from the paraxial mesoderm _(somites)._
**Somites** are **metameric structures**, repeated elements that are similar along the
body of the embryo. Many structures have this metameric disposition, and many
maintain this metameric, segmentary organization, such as the vertebrae and the
ribs. This is so because they originate from somites.

The **number of somites increases from 3rd to 5th week,** when they start to
**disappear** . They will give rise to:

   - **Axial skeleton** (vertebral bodies and ribs)

**-** **Portion of striated muscle**

**-** **Portion of skin**

**Spinal nerves** will be **innervating** these **somitic derivatives** _(somatic = derivative of_
_somitic)._

   Every somite will give rise to a **portion of skin**, which will be **innervated by the**
**corresponding spinal nerve.**
   - Correspondingly, a **portion of muscle** will be **innervated by the spinal nerve of the**
**corresponding segment.**
_(neurologists know where to explore nerve innervation, which spinal nerve they are_
_activating. Person cannot flex their thumb = lesion in C6)_

*[PDF p. 127]*

**Transition from ectoderm to mesenchyme**

Somites are mesoderm which acquires a roundish structure. During the **4th week** of
development, the ventral portion of the somites **decondensate** and **become mesenchymal,**
forming the many regions, one of them being the **sclerotome**
We can also find the **dermatome** (→skin) and the **dorsal and medial muscle cells.**

**Sclerotome** : _sclero- =_ _hard, hardened; -_ _tome = segment._ Sclerotome is the portion of
somite-derived mesenchymal cells which **will give rise to bone structures.**

The somite cells are mesenchymal and can therefore **migrate.**

   - They migrate and come together **surrounding the notochord.** They **condensate**
around the notochord and will form the **body of the vertebrae.**
   - These cells will also migrate and **surround the neural tube,** forming the **vertebral**
**arch** which will enclose the neural tube, giving rise to the vertebral canal.

Vertebral body formation enables the **passage of spinal nerves** between every two

vertebrae to innervate the **somitic derivatives.**

*[PDF p. 128]*

**Ossification**

**Long & short bones** undergo **endochondral ossification** at diaphysis. **Flat bones**
undergo **intramembranous ossification*** . Ossification **begins by the 2nd month:**

   - At the **7th week,** the whole vertebral column is
cartilaginous and begins to ossify at **primary**

**ossification centers.**

   - **At birth,** the whole vertebral column is **ossified**
_(except for some_ _**portions of cartilage**_ _at the_
_terminal ends)_
   - **After birth** new ossification centers appear,
**secondary ossification centers,** enabling growth of
the vertebral body during childhood and puberty.
   - Ossification **ends** around **25 years of age.**

Irregular bones like **vertebra** have **one or more primary ossification centers:**

_What determines the shape/type of vertebrae (cervical, thoracic,_
_lumbar,...)?_

The **patterning** of the rostrocaudal axis of the body is **regulated by**
**Homeobox genes.**
Homeobox genes control the differentiation of each vertebra.
The **specific combination of these genes** that every vertebra
expresses is what **causes them to be different,** as they confer them
with **fundamental regional characteristics.**

**Anomalies in the axial skeleton**

  - **Vertebral column** : there are vertebral anomalies associated with **neural tube**

**defects (NTDs)**, such as **incomplete closure** of the vertebral arch that leads to
**spina bifida.**

Other anomalies of the axial skeleton include:

  - **Ribs:** they also originate from the **somites and**

**sclerotome**

        - **Cervical rib:** An additional rib is found at the

cervical level, which can cause problems in
breathing.
        - **Hemivertebra:** the vertebral column is bent,

giving rise to **scoliosis**

**-** **Forked rib**

*[PDF p. 129]*

- **Sternum:** the sternum arises from the **parietal mesoderm** located at the
**ventral-most part.** 2 mesenchymal condensations fuse and join the costal
formations, where 2 sternal bars are formed and ossification centers arise.
Malformation: **Pectus excavatum** : there is strong evidence of autosomal recessive
genetic control.

**DEVELOPMENT OF THE MUSCLES IN THE TRUNK**

**Striated muscle of the trunk and limbs** originate from the myotome that derives from
somites. Therefore they ultimately derive from the **somites.**

Muscles in the trunk, at least initially, have a **segmentary organization.** Some
maintain that segmentary organization in adult age: for instance, **intercostal**
**muscles,** as they are found next to the ribs, which during development make it
difficult for muscles to fuse and mesh together, maintaining them separated in
their initial segmentary disposition.

**Skeletal muscle and dermis originate from the dorsolateral portion of the**
**somites,** which differentiates into myotome and dermatome.
This is why this dorsolateral portion is known as **dermo-myotome,** as
one part will give rise to a **portion of dermis (dermatome)** and
another, to a **portion of muscle (myotome)**

The part of the somite that will give rise to **muscle** are the
**dorsomedial and ventrolateral regions** of that embryonic
dorsolateral portion.

**Dorsomedial and ventrolateral portions**

   We have a dorsomedial region

   A ventrolateral region
Each portion expresses **different genes** that will give rise to different muscles:

   **In the dorsomedial region:** myoblasts **won’t migrate** very

far and stay close to the vertebral column. This portion of
muscle will give rise to the **intrinsic muscles of the back.**
They arise from this **epaxial portion / dorsomedial**
**portion** of the myotome. Will allow to extend the back.

   - **In the ventrolateral portion:** cells will **migrate** and form the

**muscles of the anterolateral walls** of the body **(hypaxial**
**muscle).** At this level, we find muscles such as the

**intercostal muscles and rectum muscles.**

*[PDF p. 130]*

Each portion of the somite is innervated by 1 spinal nerve, very soon in
development. A **functional unit** is formed very early on. As **myoblasts**
**migrate,** those from the same myotome **carry with them their**
**corresponding segmentary nerve** .

To know where a muscle originated from, we can know by paying
attention to which nerve innervates it. In this way we can know from
which somite it arose/which portion of myotome.

**Spinal nerves**

Spinal nerves contain both **motor and sensory**

**neurons.**

   - **Motor neurons** originate from the **spinal**
**cord (neural tube)** and extend their

axons to innervate effector **muscle cells**

   - **Sensory neurons** arise from **neural**
**crest cells** which migrate and
concentrate in ganglia. They transmit
signals coming from the corresponding
portion of **skin**

The **muscles** will have the **motor fibers,** and the **corresponding portion of skin** which
originated from the same somite will be innervated by the **sensory neuron** of said spinal

nerve.

**Epaxial and hypaxial muscle innervation**

2 sets of muscle: Epaxial (back) Hypaxial (anterolateral).
The **spinal nerve branches into 2 rami,** a **dorsal** one and a **ventral** one.

   - **Epaxial muscles** (intrinsic muscles of the back) are innervated by **dorsal rami of**
**spinal nerves**

**-**
**Hypaxial muscles (** muscles of the anterolateral walls and muscles of the limbs) are
innervated by **ventral rami of spinal nerves**

The **dorsal ramus** is **thinner** as it innervates many less structures than the ventral one.
**Both ramus contain sensory and motor neurons.**

*[PDF p. 131]*

**Congenital malformations regarding the muscles of the trunk**

Children with _**prune-belly syndrome:**_ **hypoplasia or absence (aplasia) of**

**abdominal musculature.**

The belly is distended as the organs are not enclosed by the muscles of the

trunk.

It’s a rare disorder characterized by the following triad of anomalies:

   - partial or complete absence of the abdominal muscles _(hypoplasia or_
_aplasia)_
   - failure of both testes to descend into the scrotum (bilateral
cryptorchidism)
   - and/or urinary tract malformations

_* What is the difference between intramembranous and endochondral ossification?_

_-_
_Intramembranous ossification is the direct deposition of bone on thin layers of_
_connective tissue and is characteristic of the bones on the top of the skull (flat bones)_

_-_
_In endochondral ossification, cartilage is present: endochondral ossification involves_
_the replacement of hyaline cartilage with bony tissue. Most of the bones of the_
_skeleton are formed in this manner. Involves primary and secondary ossification_
_centers, and allows bone growth through epiphyseal plates where cartilage remains_

_un-ossified._

*[PDF p. 132]*

#### Development of the appendicular skeleton and limb formation

**LIMB FORMATION**

**Limbs** are formed **between the 5th and 8th week of development.**
Almost totally formed by the end of the 2nd month.
**Limb buds** however appear by the **end of the 4th week (28days),** at
this stage they can already be appreciated on the embryo.

_**What are they made of?**_
They are an **evagination of the lateral**
**aspect of the embryo:** therefore, they are
made up of a **chore of mesenchyme**
(coming from the parietal layer of lateral
mesoderm) lined by a **layer of ectoderm.**

Very early on, a thickening of the ectoderm forms at the tip of the
limb bud. This tip becomes known as the **apical ectodermal ridge**
**(AER),** which is the structure that **induces the outgrowth** of the
limb (outward, distal pull).

**Molecular regulation of patterning and growth in the limb**

The growth is **molecularly regulated.** 3 axis:

**Proximodistal axis**

The **apical ectodermal ridge (AER)** promotes limb growth in the proximodistal direction.

   - **FGF10** secreted by lateral plate mesoderm _(underneath the ectoderm layer)_ in the
limb forming regions leads to the **formation of the AER**
   - The ridge secretes molecules **(FGF4 and FGF8)** that induce
**proliferation of the mesenchyme** in areas proximal to the ridge **,**
such that these cells proliferate and remain **undifferentiated** .
   - As the limb grows, the **most proximal part,** not under the action
of the ridge, **starts differentiating.**

**It follows a proximal-distal differentiation.**

*[PDF p. 133]*

**Dorsoventral limb axis**

There is also a **dorsal-ventral axis.** It is directed by **WNT-7,** which is
expressed in the **dorsal ectoderm**, specifying these cells as dorsal.

**Anteroposterior axis**
_(what is the thumb, etc...),_ determined by **SHH**, with a **mediolateral**
**differentiation** _(from the medial towards the lateral side, from pinky to thumb)_

However it's the **HOX genes** that regulate the **regional patterning of the**
**limb.** In other words, the shapes and types of bones in the limb.

**Development of the limb buds**

  - **4th week:** around day 28, **limb buds begin to appear** at the end of the 4th
week. The upper limb is slightly ahead to the lower _(3 days ahead)._

  - **5 week embryo** : all limb buds are clearly visible at this point

  - **6th week:** the limb develops and forms a flat end, the **hand plate** in the upper
limbs and **foot plate** in the lower limb. We see thickenings within the hand
plate, elevations known as the **digital rays (future fingers).** We also see a
**distal constriction.** _(externally we first see differentiation in the distal, growing_
_apical area)_

  - **7th week:** digital rays appear in the footplate. The fingers in hands and feet
are slightly separated and we can begin to see the **elbow and the knee.**

Also during this 7th week, the upper and lower limbs **rotate in opposite**

**directions.**

Initially, thumb and toe are pointing upwards.

➢ The **upper limb** will **rotate laterally 90 degrees** _(supine anatomical view)_,

implying:

        - The **elbow will be facing dorsally**
        - The **extensor muscles** will be located **posteriorly**

➢ The **lower limb rotates medially almost 90 degrees.**

        - The **knee** is **facing anteriorly**
        - **Extensor muscle** s lie **anteriorly**

**○** **8th week:** in the 8-week embryo, we see the **rotated limbs,** and **separate**
**digits** in hands and feet

_How do the fingers separate?_

Due to induced **apoptosis** . Programmed

cell death causes the **destruction of**

**the interdigital spaces,** and fingers
become fully separated.

*[PDF p. 134]*

_Maximum point of vulnerability in relation to limb formation:_

Between the **3rd and 8th week,** during their period of formation. The defect will be **more**
**severe during the 4th-5th week** as damage here will cause the limb to maybe not form at
all. Defects further along in development will have more mild consequences.

**Congenital defects**

  - **MEROMELIA:** congenital defect due to exposure to teratogens
like **thalidomide.** Characterized by a **partial absence of a limb.**

_Children born with limb abnormalities between 1957 a 1962 in_

_Europe after thalidomide (antiemetic) intake by pregnant women_
_between weeks 3-5 of gestation_

  - **AMELIA** : **full absence** of the limb. Most are due to genetic factors _(limb defect)_

**●** **CLEFT HANDS/FEET:** autosomal dominant abnormality with incomplete penetrance
_(limb defect)_

  - **POLYDACTYLY** : extra finger. Polydactyly is inherited as a dominant trait _(digital birth_
_defect)_

  - **SYNDACTYLY** : interdigital membrane which has not been fully removed _(digital birth_
_defect)_ . Can be resolved with surgical intervention

  - **CLUBFEET**, the **most common musculoskeletal defect.** Appears
to be caused by multifactorial inheritance _(genetic and environmental_
_factors acting together)._ It’s postnatally reversible.

*[PDF p. 135]*

**DEVELOPMENT OF THE APPENDICULAR SKELETON**

The appendicular skeleton originates from mesenchymal cells arising
from the **parietal layer of the lateral mesoderm**

  - During the **5th week, mesenchymal bones** arise in the limbs

  - During the **6th week,** mesenchymal bone models in the limbs
undergo **chondrification** to form **hyaline cartilage bone**
**models.** Bones will become cartilaginous.

**○** During the **7th week,** bones continue to develop as
cartilaginous structures

  - At the **8th week, ossification** begins in the long bones in **primary ossification**
**centers,** and initially occurs in the diaphyses of the bones.

In a **12-week fetus, primary ossification centers** are present in **nearly all**

**bones** of the limbs.

**Secondary ossification centers appear after birth.**

**Skeletal malformations**

In a 3 month fetus, there are primary ossification centers (black) in all bones
of the body. Secondary ossification centers appear afterwards, in the
epiphysis, from where bones will grow.
Disruptions during these ossification processes lead to malformations:

**●** **ACHONDROPLASIA** is caused by a dominant autosomal mutation in the
receptor fibroblast growth factor 3 (FGFR3).
Consists in a **disturbance of endochondral ossification** during **fetal**
**life** at the **epiphyseal cartilage plates,** particularly of long bones.

**●** **OSTEOGENESIS IMPERFECTA:** autosomal mutation,
dominant or recessive, for the gen of type I collagen. It
results in **extreme bone fragility** with **spontaneous**

**fractures.**

*[PDF p. 136]*

**SKELETAL MUSCLES IN THE LIMB**

**Skeletal muscles in the limbs** originate from the **somites**,
specifically from the **ventrolateral myotome.**
These cells, which migrate for the original dermo-myotome,
also **expand into the growing limb buds** and extend with
them _(hypaxial muscle)_ . Give rise to **flexor and extensor**

**muscles in the limb.**

However, as they enter into the limb, the muscles fuse together
forming muscle bands and **lose their segmentary**
**organization.**

As this is hypaxial muscle, the muscles in the limbs will be innervated by ventral rami. The
**nerves also extend with the migrating cells** and **combine**, forming **plexuses** .
We will find networks / plexuses of **ventral rami that have fused** to finally form terminal

nerves in the limbs.

   - **Brachial plexus:** made up of **ventral rami** of spinal nerves **C5-T1**
**-** **Lumbosacral plexus:** made up of **ventral rami** of spinal nerves **L2-S2**

**Dermatome in the limbs**

In contrast to the skeletal muscle in the limbs, the **dermatome cells in the limb maintain**

**their segmentary pattern.** Although regions are slightly overlapped, they are well

differentiated.

Therefore, **sensory innervation** to the limb maintains a **segmental pattern,** reflecting the
**embryological origin of each dermatome** and its **innervation.**
_Forelimbs with their sensory innervation to the_
_dermatomes represented:_

_There is also a cutaneous distribution of the_

_dorsal rami (towards the intrinsic muscles in the_
_back+dermatome) of the spinal nerves._

**VASCULARIZATION**

**Blood vessels follow the same trajectory** and
**accompany the spinal nerves in extension**
during muscle cell migration.

Blood vessels receive sympathetic innervation:

                               - Skeletal muscle bv = Ach

                               - Rest of bv = adrenaline and noradrenaline

*[PDF p. 137]*

#### Autonomous nervous system

Aside from the somatic nervous system, found innervating structures derived from somites,
we will also have an **autonomous nervous system** (visceral plexuses) **.** It **innervates**
**smooth muscle in glands and viscera** . It's not a voluntary muscle. _(Gray's anatomy book._
_Final part)_
Somatic plexuses = striated muscle; visceral plexuses = muscles in viscera and glands
The Autonomous nervous system is further divided into **Sympathetic (S) and**
**parasympathetic (PS) nervous systems.**

**Where does smooth muscle come from?**

It originates from the **splanchnic layer of the lateral**
**mesoderm.** The parietal/somatic mesoderm lines the
body walls and this other layer surrounds the
endoderm of the gut, the forming endocardium, and

other viscera.

   - The **endoderm** will give rise to the **mucosa**
**of the GI tract,** whereas
   - The **smooth muscle** that forms the walls of the gut originates from the **splanchnic**
**layer of the lateral mesoderm.**

**Di�erences between somatic and autonomous system**

  - **Somatic or spinal nerves** innervate **striated muscle** via **one**

**motor neuron.**

  - **Autonomic nerves** innervate **smooth muscle** via **two**

**neurons.**

Autonomic nerves have second neurons:

   - The **2nd neuron** of the SNA is always located in **ganglia**, and
is therefore found originating **outside the CNS.** We therefore
call it **postganglionic neuron.** The nerve _(accumulation of_
_fibers)_ will be referred to as postganglionic fiber. It makes
reference to the trajectory followed by the axons.
   - The **1st neuron** is located in the **CNS** _(spinal cord or brain_
_stem)_ and activates the 2nd neuron, located in ganglion. It’s
known as **preganglionic neuron,** and its fiber, preganglionic
fiber, as its synapse occurs in the ganglion.

**Where do those ganglionic neurons come from?**

They come from the **neural crest cells.** NCCs give rise
to the **ganglion of sympathetic trunk** where the
somas of these neurons will be found, and from which
their processes will extend. **Axons of the CNS** finally
**target and penetrate them** to be in connection.
Neural crest cells will give rise to both **Sympathetic**
**and Parasympathetic ganglionic neurons.**

*[PDF p. 138]*

The autonomic nervous system is made up of **afferent branches (sensory) and efferent**
**branches (motor).**

**VISCEROMOTOR FIBERS**

These efferent visceromotor branches belong either to the **Sympathetic**
**or Parasympathetic SNA.** They are both characterized by having
autonomic nerves, made up of **2 neurons** (pre and post ganglionic). It is a
two neuron motor system:

   preganglionic neuron in the brainstem or spinal cord

   postganglionic neuron in a ganglion. The postganglionic axon
synapses with its target.

**Sympathetic and parasympathetic systems**

   - **Sympathetic system** : **fight and flight response.** It prepares
the body for this situation, by activating organs in order to
respond to a danger/challenge.
Involves the activation of muscles, change to far sight rather
than near site (dilates pupils), increased heart rate, inhibits
digestion....

   **Parasympathetic: “rest and digest” response**
It involves stimulation of digestion, slow contraction, reduces
blood flow to skeletal muscle,... Near sight is also facilitated
by the activation of the parasympathetic system (pupil
constriction).

Each of these two systems are complementary, but have different effects.

**Location of preganglionic neurons**

Preganglionic axons **exit the spinal cord through the ventral root** with other
motor axons. Where are they found? In the **CNS** .

   **Parasympathetic system:** preganglionic cells originating from the
**brainstem** which innervate the visceral wall (through the vagus nerve
mainly), and preganglionic cells located at the **sacral level (S2-S4).**

We will see more about them later.

   - **Sympathetic system:** whole **column** of preganglionic cells along the
**spinal cord** (originate from **T1 to L2** ).

a) SYMPATHETIC SYSTEM

**Sympathetic ganglia**

We find 2 sets of sympathetic ganglia:
**1- Sympathetic trunk or chain:** a series of **paired ganglia at each side/level of the**
**spinal cord,** found forming a chain. Also known as paravertebral.

**2 - Preaortic single ganglia:** located ventral to the aorta _(= preaortic)._ They are located in
the **abdominal and pelvic cavities.** Also known as prevertebral. These ganglia are located
beneath the diaphragm, in the mentioned cavities.

*[PDF p. 139]*

Preganglionic sympathetic axons will synapse either in sympathetic chain ganglia
(paravertebral ganglia) or preaortic/prevertebral ganglia.
The Postganglionic sympathetic axons will then go and synapse with blood vessels, glands
and other tissues throughout the body.

**Preganglionic sympathetic neurons**

Preganglionic sympathetic neurons originate in the intermediolateral cell
column **(lateral horn)** in thoracic and upper lumbar spinal
cord (spinal segments **T1 to L2** ). **Only these portions have**
**said intermediate horn.** Therefore, in order for upper and
lower postganglionic neurons to be innervated, preganglionic
neurons will have to originate in the intermediate horns at
T1-L2 levels, and travel through the sympathetic trunk to
reach their corresponding ganglion.

Therefore, we will see that preganglionic axons can **ascend**
**or descend in the sympathetic chain** on the sides of the
vertebrae, depending on which ganglion they are targeting.

**What viscera will they innervate?**

   - **Dermal smooth muscle:** there are other viscera that are **only innervated by the**
**sympathetic nervous system:** the dermis, such as the dermis in **sweat glands,**
**arrector pili muscle (hair follicles) and blood vessels (endothelium).** The
postganglionic neuron **originates at the sympathetic trunk ganglia** _**.**_
   - **Thoracic viscera:** the synapse between pre and postganglionic neurons **won’t**
**occur in preaortic single ganglia.** Therefore their 2nd effector neurons will be
**located in the sympathetic chain.** The postganglionic fibers form **cardiac nerves.**
   - **Abdominal viscera** : They will have their motor neurons **located in preaortic single**
**ganglia.** The long preganglionic fibers will form **splanchnic nerves** (thoracic and
lumbar)

**Dermis, sweat glands,**
**arrector pili** → sympathetic
trunk

**Thoracic viscera** →
sympathetic trunk. Cardiac
nerves = postganglionic

**Abdominal and pelvic viscera**
→ preaortic single ganglia.
Splanchnic nerves =
preganglionic

*[PDF p. 140]*

As the distance between the trunk and preaortic ganglia is small in the SNS (both are very
close to the spinal cord):

**-** **preganglionic fibers** are quite **short**

   **postganglionic fibers** will be very **long and extended.**

The **preganglionic** fiber is **myelinated**, so it has a **whitish color.**
These myelinated preganglionic fibers join together to form a cord,
known as the **white communicating ramus** = all the nervous cords
that are connecting the sympathetic ganglia to the spinal cord.
The second, visceromotor neurons, the **postganglionic neurons**
whose soma originates in the ganglion are **unmyelinated.** When
they exit the ganglion they form another cord known as the **gray**
**communicating ramus** = unmyelinated, so they have a Gray-ish

color.

**Sympathetic fibers from T1-L2. Dermal smooth muscle**
Innervation of **dermal smooth muscle** . It’s innervated **exclusively by Sympathetic fibers.**
Bodies of the preganglionic neurons originate in a special horn in the spinal cord: the
**intermediate horn / lateral horn.** This horn is found only **from T1 to L2.** It contains
**sympathetic preganglionic visceromotor neurons.**
As it is a motor, it will take the ventral root to exit and extend towards the neuron it has to
connect with, which is in the ganglion. Therefore it **exits the nerve** and **enters the**
**ganglionic chain** through the white communicating ramus.
Here, the **postganglionic fiber** exits and overlaps:

   - If it goes **towards the back,** it will take the **dorsal ramus.**
These glands in the dorsal portion (back) also have a
segmentary distribution as they are in every portion of
skin. Therefore, these postganglionic nerves **follow the**
**same route as spinal nerves**, taking the easiest route, in
order to reach sweat glands, hair follicles and blood
vessels of that part of the dermis.
   - If it goes **towards the front,** it will take the **ventral ramus.**
These visceromotor sympathetic neurons exit along with
many other motor neurons, going to innervate other bodily

structures.

**Dermal sympathetic fibers beyond T1-L2**
_**What happens above T1 or below L2?**_ We don't have
preganglionic fibers entering the corresponding ganglion of the
sympathetic chain, as at these levels of the spinal cord, we
**don’t find an intermediate horn.** Therefore, **no**
**preganglionic fiber.**

_Who innervates the postganglionic fiber then?_ For this
purpose, preganglionic neurons originating at T1 go into their
corresponding ganglion; but instead of innervating the
postganglionic neurons at that level, they **go up to innervate**

these others that are at levels that lack intermediate horn. If it

were at a lumbar level, it would **go down** from L2 level to
connect with the lower sacral ganglia.

*[PDF p. 141]*

They enter the ganglionic chain and either **go up or down to innervate postganglionic**

**nerves which are at a level in which there is no intermediate horn.**

**Sympathetic innervation of thoracic viscera**

The **thoracic viscera** are innervated through both the sympathetic and the parasympathetic
divisions of the autonomic nervous system. The sympathetic nerves in question arise from
the **cervical and thoracic sympathetic trunk ganglia.**
At the thoracic level we **have an intermediate horn.** The postganglionic
fiber/axon **directly exits the ganglion.** It won’t follow the route taken by
spinal nerves ***** as it is not going to innervate muscles of the back,...etc, but
rather **thoracic viscera** _(such as the heart, esophagus,..._ **).** All together, the
**postganglionic nerves** which arise from the sympathetic trunk are called
**cardiac sympathetic nerves,** which join to form the **cardiac sympathetic**
**plexus.**
***** _Postganglionic fibers which go to innervate smooth muscle in peripheral dermis and body_
_walls do follow the same route as spinal nerves = exit through dorsal and ventral rami_

**Sympathetic innervation of abdominal and pelvic viscera**

They do have **single preaortic ganglia.** The **preganglionic nerve** will enter the
sympathetic trunk but **exit the ganglion** to directly **enter the preaortic single ganglion,**
where the **postganglionic neuron is found.** There, they will synapse.
These preganglionic neurons which exit the sympathetic trunk and meet up in preaortic
ganglia are known as **splanchnic nerves,** _(greater splanchnic, lesser splanchnic and least_
_splanchnic plexuses)._ We find both **thoracic splanchnic** _(innervating abdomen)_ and **lumbar**
**splanchnic.**
All these are **splanchnic nerves = preganglionic fibers.**
Sympathetic nerves generally **enter the viscera together with the blood vessels.** This is
so because all the arteries that also go to the kidneys, to the gut,... are exclusively
innervated by sympathetic nerves. These are visceromotor neurons.

*[PDF p. 142]*

b) PARASYMPATHETIC SYSTEM

**Parasympathetic ganglia**
Parasympathetic ganglia are generally only seen **microscopically** . They’re **located very**
**close to the viscera** or even in the wall of the viscera itself. Therefore, in the PS:

   - Axons of **preganglionic neurons** are very **long**
   - **Postganglionic fibers** will be quite **short**

**Parasympathetic preganglionic neurons**
The **preganglionic neurons** will be located in:

   - The **brainstem**

   - In the **spinal cord from S2 to S4.**
The **postganglionic neurons,** as they’re very close to the viscera, have **very short axons.**

**Innervation from the brainstem**

We have one nerve, the **vagus nerve**, which exits via the
jugular foramen, passess through the neck and provides
**parasympathetic innervation for abdominal viscera and**

**thoracic viscera.**

It slows down the heart rate, contracts the bronchi,... and

stimulates the GI tract.

It innervates all the way down to the distal third of the colon,
and is therefore “in charge” of **⅔ of the transverse colon**
_(AC, TC, DC)._
Therefore, it **innervates thoracic and abdominal viscera all**
**the way to the Transverse Colon (TC).**
_(There are also other clusters of neurons innervating facial_
_structures, which as well as the vagus, also arise in the brain_
_stem. But we will focus on the vagus nerve)._

**Innervation from S2-24**

Preganglionic fibers arising from S2-S4 are known as pelvic nerves. They **innervate the**
**rest of the GI tract** _(remaining abdominal viscera: AC and DC + ⅓ of TC)_ **and pelvic**
**viscera,** the remaining structures.
We call these nerves **pelvic splanchnic nerves**
_(don’t mix with thoracic and lumbar splanchnic,_
_from the sympathetic)._ These pelvic splanchnic
nerves are **preganglionic** fibers which reach the
bladder, rectum, prostate in males, clitoris in
females,.... they are involved in erection,
therefore also known as **erogenous nerves.**
These nerves form the **inf. hypogastric plexus.**

                         - PS innervation of the abdominal viscera: There is a

whole network of tiny ganglia containing postganglionic
neurons and fibers which stimulate **peristaltism** .

*[PDF p. 143]*

PS innervation of pelvic viscera: there is no intermediolateral horn in S2-S4, but we
