do have motor neurons. They exit from the motor horn, go through the ventral root
and exit it. They follow the route towards the midline and innervate for example, the
bladder, to trigger and regulate **micturition.**

**VISCEROSENSORY FIBERS**

Sympathetic and parasympathetic systems stimulate smooth muscle. But viscera also have
sensory innervation _(stomach pain, menstrual pain,...)._ We will also find **sensory fibers**
**conveying information about pain or how full the viscera are** _(distention in the stomach,_
_rectum,..)_

2 modalities of sensory innervation:

**-** **Pain**

**-** **Distention**

*[PDF p. 144]*

**Trajectory of these viscerosensory fibers**

   - **Pain:** as a general rule, pain information **travels**
**with motor sympathetic fibers** (although in the
**opposite direction** ). They generally accompany
the sympathetic system in the opposite direction,
**except for pelvic pain** which will accompany the

PS.

Therefore, they will convey pain information and
reach the CNS, first through **white communicating**
**rami** and then through the **dorsal root.**

   - **Distention:** distention information **accompanies**
**Parasympathetic fibers** ( _vagus nerve and other_
_cranial nerves)_ in the **opposite direction.** They

enter the CNS at the **brainstem** and **sacral** level.

**Distension information**

Therefore, **distension sensory info from thoracic**
**viscera, abdominal viscera, till the distal third of TC**
will travel **following the vagus nerve.**

**Distension information of the pelvic viscera** travels with the **pelvic splanchnic nerves.**
Pain in pelvic viscera will also follow this trajectory, accompanying the pelvic splanchnic

nerve.

**Pain information**

Pain information is conveyed by **sensory neurons accompanying**
**Sympathetic** nervous system. It will accompany either **cardiac**
**nerves**, **thoracic splanchnic** or **lumbar splanchnic nerves**, in the
opposite direction. They reach the spinal nerve via the **white**
**communicating ramus.**

As said, this is so **except for pelvic pain**, which follows the same route as **pelvic**
**splanchnic nerves in the PS.**

_Where are their sensory neurons located?_
**Sensory neurons are ALWAYS located in sensory ganglia,** in this case, in the ganglia
prior to entering the jugular nerve which contains all the bodies of the sensory neurons. In
the brain stem, it makes synapse in an area similar to the dorsal horn.

**Chroma�n cells**

Some **neural crest cells** enter adrenal structure and stay there (chromaffin
cells). These cells **release norepinephrine and epinephrine** to the
bloodstream upon stimulation, **as if it were a sympathetic ganglion**
located in the **adrenal gland.** When activated, the adrenal medulla
releases epinephrine and norepinephrine into the blood, which potentiates
sympathetic activity throughout the body. Needed to coordinate the bodily
**"fight-or-flight"** response to fear, stress, exercise, or conflict.

*[PDF p. 145]*

**NEUROTRANSMITTERS**

**All preganglionic fibers release Acetylcholine (Ach) neurotransmitter (cholinergic).**
The neurotransmitters vary between the postganglionic fibers of the Sympathetic and
Parasympathetic nervous systems:

**Sympathetic neurotransmitters**

As said, the **preganglionic fiber** will release **Ach**

**-** **The second neurotransmitter** will generally be **Norepinephrine and Epinephrine**
**(adrenergic)**, as is the case of arrector pili muscles, blood vessels of the skin,...
   - However there are exceptions: postganglionic sympathetic fibers innervating **sweat**
**glands and blood vessels in skeletal muscle** will release **Ach**

**Adrenal medulla**

Adrenal glands are innervated by preganglionic sympathetic neurons. They receive
stimulation through the release of Ach, and they themselves release **Norepinephrine and**
**Epinephrine into the bloodstream.** They are not postganglionic neurons, but are
considered as such as they fulfill the same function, with the exception that instead of
innervating smooth muscle, they release their neurotransmitters into the blood.
Adrenal glands are **adrenergic** .

**Parasympathetic neurotransmitters**

The **preganglionic fiber** will release **Ach.**
Here in the PS, the **postganglionic fiber will also**
**release Ach** to innervate smooth muscle **(cholinergic)**

*[PDF p. 146]*

#### TBL. Autonomous Nervous System

   - Soma of sensory neurons are located in **ganglia of spinal nerves,** which then enter
the dorsal horn to convey sensory information

   **Sympathetic and parasympathetic neurons are visceromotor** : they convey motor

information to smooth muscle of the viscera

   - **Pain information** is conveyed through **sensory fibers** which **travel along with**
**sympathetic nerves** (cardiac, thoracic splanchnic and lumbar splanchnic). This is so
**except for pelvic pain,** which travels along with nerves from the parasympathetic
system (S2-S4).
**Distention information** travels along with the **PS** (pelvic splanchnic nerves and
vagus nerves)

        Cardiac nerves = mainly innervate heart + esophagus

        Thoracic splanchnic = abdominal viscera

        Lumbar splanchnic = lower abdominal and pelvic viscera
_Intercostal nerves are somatic, and innervate intercostal striated muscle_

   **All preganglionic fibers release Ach.** Preganglionic are all **cholinergic** (Ach)

        Sympathetic system = postganglionic fibers release **norepinephrine and**
**epinephrine** neurotransmitters. They are **adrenergic.**
**Chromaffin cells** act as postganglionic sympathetic fibers, as they release
norepinephrine and epinephrine.

        **Parasympathetic neurotransmitters** = postganglionic fibers release
**acetylcholine** . Ach = both for the pre and post ganglionic PS fibers. They are
**cholinergic.**

   - The **vascular system** is innervated exclusively by the **sympathetic system.** Heart
and lungs both receive parasympathetic stimulation in addition to the sympathetic.
   - An effect of the **vagus nerve stimulation** = triggers **rest and digest response.** Will
induce **acid release** in the stomach to carry out **digestion (gastrocolic reflex)**
   - The substance majorly released by the **adrenal medulla** is **Epinephrine** . Adrenal
medulla secretes **80% of epinephrine, 20% of Norepinephrine.** Majorly epinephrine
   - **Sweat glands** are **only innervated by the sympathetic system.** They are special,
an exception: they use **Ach** as **second neurotransmitter,** for postganglionic

innervation

CLINICAL CASE

A 27-year-old pregnant woman is delivering her first child. In the last stage of labor the
anesthetist carries out a lumbar puncture and administers a local anesthetic through a
catheter located in the epidural space. The woman experiences immediate relief and thanks

the anesthetist for the intervention.

   Before the delivery of the anesthetic the arterial pressure was 130/85 mmHg
Heart rate, 98 beats per minute.

   After 20 min the arterial pressure is 80/45 mmHg and the heart rate 120 bpm
and the patient feels dizzy.
_epidural = space between the vertebral arch. Anesthesia is administered there to_
_block sensory information._

*[PDF p. 147]*

_What complication has appeared as a result of the anesthesia?_
Arterial hypotension as a direct consequence. Anesthesia has the intention of blocking
sensory information, but has had other effects.
The consequence that the anesthesia has brought this patient is:

   Arterial hypotension (low bp)

   Tachycardia: heart rate increases to compensate the hypotension, to maintain blood
supply to all the organs

_Why is the woman suffering from hypotension?_
Because the anesthesia has reached the sympathetic system, which is found innervating

smooth muscle in the walls of the vessels.

The **vascular smooth muscle is innervated only by the sympathetic system** which is
normally activated at a basal level. This small muscle contraction is needed to maintain a
constant pressure in the vessels, by keeping them constricted. Inhibition of the sympathetic
nerves after the **epidural anesthesia decreases the stimulation of alpha1 receptors** in
the arterioles, **increasing the diameter** of arterioles and leading to **arterial hypotension.**

By trying to block the afferent sensory system, we somehow block the motor sympathetic
nerves. The vascular smooth muscle is normally activated, to induce a basal sympathetic
tone. If inhibited, it induces **↓ in neurotransmitter release (alpha1 receptors) = smooth**
**muscle in the vessels relaxes = increase in arterial diameter = ↓ bp = hypotension**

_Anatomically, how has this happened?_

1. Epidural space
2. Dorsal root (goes towards the dorsal horn) + dorsal
root ganglia
3. Ventral root (towards ventral horn)
4. Ventral ramus of the spinal nerve spinal nerve
5. Dorsal ramus of the spinal nerve

6. Lateral/intermediate horn

7. White communicating ramus _(preganglionic,_
_myelinated)_
8. Grey communicating ramus _(postganglionic,_
_unmyelinated)_
9. Sympathetic trunk ganglia

_What neurons have been affected by the anesthetic, what neurotransmitter do they use and_
_where do they make synapse?_

**-**
**Anesthesia is blocking the synapse between the preganglionic and**
**postganglionic sympathetic fibers.**

   acetylcholine _(all preganglionic fibers release Ach)_

   Ganglionic neurons of the sympathetic chain

_Where else may ganglionic neurons of the sympathetic system be located?_

   At preaortic ganglia, next to the main branches of the abdominal aorta

   - As chromaffin cells in the adrenal medulla

_What drug would you administer to revert the arterial hypotension?_
In the **blood vessels,** the **adrenergic receptors** expressed are **alpha-1.** By administering
an **agonist of alpha 1, we can reverse the hypotension.**

*[PDF p. 148]*

Firstly, physicians provided a saline solution to increase blood volume (fluid therapy,
to increase vascular and interstitial extracellular fluid)

   They then proceeded to provide **agonists of alpha-1 adrenergic** such as ephedrine
or phenylephrine.

   As the sympathetic tone is restored, arterial pressure goes back to normal.

_If bradycardia appears (45 bpm), how would you explain it?_
Because **phenylephrine has no beta-adrenergic activity**, it does not increase contractility
force and output of the cardiac muscle. It may increase blood pressure resulting in a slow
heart rate through stimulation of vascular baroreceptors. In the presence of high mean
arterial pressure, the **baroreceptor reflex produces a reflex bradycardia** as a method of
decreasing blood pressure by decreasing the cardiac output, to counteract the unbalance.
This takes place in the brain stem.

**Alpha-1 receptor agonist = increased receptor stimulation =**
**↑ bp = detected by vascular baroreceptors = baroreceptor**
**reflex = decreased heart rate (bradycardia)**

Baroreceptor reflex
The changes in distension of the carotids is detected by the
**glossopharyngeal nerve** (9th spinal nerve). The sensory
neuron’s somas of this nerve are located in the inferior ganglion

of the IX cranial nerve.

Picks up distension vessel information in the **bifurcation of the**
**carotid artery (carotid sinus).**
Its peripheral process goes towards the brain stem, where it reaches its some, located in
ganglion prior to the jugular foramen. The central process enters through the jugular foramen
for synapse

   Arterial pressure is high = glossopharyngeal nerve detects it and goes from carotid
sinus to solitary nucleus _(similar to dorsal horn)_ in the brainstem = excite
**vasodepressor neurons** _(green)_ = synapse with PS motor neurons in the dorsal
vagal nucleus = they run down through the vagus nerve, which stimulates
postganglionic PS neurons = release Ach which binds M2 muscarinic receptors on
cardiac muscle cells = bradycardia

   Arterial pressure is low = induce tachycardia through another pathway
glossopharyngeal nerve transmits distension information = d excite vasopressor
neurons that project to the intermediolateral column (T1-L2) = has to go down the
intermediolateral cell column and synapse with the corresponding sympathetic motor
neuron, to induce tachycardia

_How would you then treat the patient?_
As a response, if arterial pressure goes up,
the vagus is stimulated. **Some patients**
**experience bradycardia after agonist**
**alpha-1 administration**
To block this effect (PS response inducing
bradycardia), we would administer
**antagonists for muscarinic receptors,** as
they use Ach as a neurotransmitter.

*[PDF p. 149]*

**Key ideas:**

   - Organs and systems such as the **vascular system** are **exclusively innervated by**
**the sympathetic nerves.** Other examples would be the kidney and the system
regulating glucemia.
   - There is a **basal sympathetic tone** in the sympathetic system and the regulation of
function under that control depends on the increase or decrease of that basal tone.
That is why our patient responded to the treatment with alpha-1 receptor _(like_
_ephedrine or phenylephrine)_

   **Other organs** have both sympathetic and parasympathetic innervation and their
**regulation depends on the basal sympathetic and the parasympathetic tones** .
An example of this is the **heart.** That is why if our patient experiences bradycardia
she would respond to a treatment with antagonists of muscarinic receptors _(like_
_atropine)._

*[PDF p. 150]*

#### HISTOLOGY AND PHYSIOLOGY OF NORMAL TISSUES

*[PDF p. 151]*

#### Cartilage

_Connective tissue: cartilage_

**Cartilage** is a **specialized** type of **connective tissue.** It consists of cells **(chondroblasts**
**and chondrocytes)** and ECM made of **fibers** and **ground substance** _(proteoglycans,_
_glycoproteins,..)_

The whiter areas, with lots of ground substance, represent **loose**
**connective tissue** which is found surrounding cartilaginous tissue.
Within the cartilage, we can appreciate **chondrocytes embedded in**
**lacunae** which are differentiated and **secreting matrix.** This matrix which
surrounds the chondrocytes and lacunae is formed by **fibers and ground**
**substance** _(it’s not fibrous ECM though)._

As part of the **components of the ECM** we can find collagen type II, which is seen only in
cartilage. Other components include:

   **Type II collagen,** already mentioned

   Hyaluranan _(hyaluronic acid)_

   Proteoglycans

**-**
**Aggrecan**

**-** **Chondroitin sulfate**

   - Keratin sulfate

   Glycosaminoglycans

➢ _**Different types of collagen. What’s their importance?**_

_Knowing the type of collagen is useful to aid in identifying the structures._

_-_
_Connective tissue = type I collagen_

_-_
_Cartilage = type II collagen_

_-_
_Reticular connective tissue = type III collagen_

_-_
_Basal membrane = type IV collagen_

*[PDF p. 152]*

Separating loose connective tissue and cartilage, there is a kind of
capsule _(a little denser and more compact than cartilage)_ found
surrounding cartilage. This layer is known as **perichondrium.** This layer
of **dense irregular connective tissu** e acts as the **reservoir for**
**chondrocytes.** Signalling molecules can activate the cells in the
perichondrium, causing them to differentiate into chondrocytes through
gene activation and inactivation.
Fibroblasts have the potential to differentiate into other tissue cells, such
as those of cartilage: the fibroblasts in loose CT start to move towards
cartilage and begin to change their morphology (becoming round) and
secreting matrix.

We can differentiate **2 layers** of the perichondrium: **outer**
**fibrous layer** _(fibroblasts)_ and **inner chondrogenic layer**
_(contains chondroblasts, which secrete matrix)_

CHARACTERISTICS

   - It’s an **avascular** tissue; in general connective tissue is highly vascularized, except in
the case of cartilage. This is why this tissue is **compact and forms thin layers,** as to
easily obtain **nutrients through diffusion** from surrounding tissues, drawn in
through osmosis.
They **proliferate very slowly** as they have a scarce supply of nutrients which they
have to store. As an adaptation to this, chondrocytes have developed a way to store
energy, in the form of glycogen. Therefore, cartilage is **very rich in glycogen.**
_(chondrocytes replicate very slowly that’s why it is difficult to recover from lesions_
_affecting cartilage)._

   - It serves as a **precursor/model** for **embryonic development** and subsequent
**growth of many long bones.** Cartilage is replaced by bone tissue in adult life,
except for the surfaces that **articulate** with other bones.

FUNCTIONS

   - The cartilaginous ECM allows the surrounding tissues to **bear mechanical stresses**
without being permanently distorted ( _it’s very resilient)._

   - Serves as a framework to give **support** to other tissues

   - It’s **shock-absorbing** and acts as a **gliding area for joints**

   - It facilitates **bone movements**

   - It’s responsible for **development** and **formation of long bones**
through **ossification**

*[PDF p. 153]*

TYPES OF CARTILAGE

1. **Hyaline cartilage** _(type II collagen)_   - It’s the most abundant cartilage in the body. It’s

found on **articular surfaces** _(articular cartilage = specialized form_
_of hyaline cartilage, has NO perichondrium)_ **,** forming the **nasal**
**septum** and the **tracheal rings, sternal end of ribs,** ... It also
forms the template for developing long bones **(epiphyseal plate).**
Hyaline cartilage forms the temporary skeleton in the fetus, until
it’s replaced by bone through ossification.

2. **Elastic cartilage** _(type II collagen)_   - found in **pinna of the ear,**

**epiglottis, eustachian tube** _(connects your throat to your middle_
_ear)._ Abundant elastic fibres.

3. **Fibrocartilage** _(large amount of type I and type II collagen)_   - found

in **intervertebral discs, pubic symphysis** and the insertion sites
of **tendons and ligaments.** Chondrocytes are found in parallel in
between collagen fibres. Due to this parallel alignment, they’re
present in structures which bear high stresses, as it is more

resilient.

CARTILAGE ECM

Some areas of the matrix stain more blue than other areas; these darker areas represent
regions with more fibers, specifically type II collagen. In the lighter areas of the matrix, we
find more proteoglycans and therefore, water.
The matrix is **not homogeneous** _(not the same amount of substances in each_
_of the regions)_ because we need areas which draw in water and therefore,
nutrients and oxygen through diffusion, and others which provide protection.

   - The **darker** matrix _(more basophilic = seen in blue/dark red in this case)_
which is adjacent to the chondrocytes is known as the **territorial matrix** . Richer in
proteoglycans, stains darker.

   - The **lighter** matrix ( _eosinophilic areas = clearer)_, found between isogenous
groups is the **interterritorial matrix** . Less proteoglycans, stains darker. All
the **O2 and nutrients can easily flow and diffuse** into the different cells.

CARTILAGE FORMATION / GROWTH

Cartilage begins to form from **embryonic mesenchymal**
**cells.** It can either originate **from fibroblasts and**
**chondroblasts** found in the **perichondrium** layer, or
from **interstitial growth** through the **mitotic division of**
**chondrocytes,** which results in the formation of
isogenous groups that accumulate in lacunae.

*[PDF p. 154]*

**Appositional growth:** consists in the deposition of new cartilage on the surface of
existing cartilage: it grows from the outside, originating from the perichondrium. This
occurs through the differentiation of fibroblasts, a layer of cells with proliferative
capacity. Each layer contains more and more differentiated cells until they finally
derive to chondrocytes _(fibroblasts-chondroblasts-chondrocytes)._ This
**differentiation by layers** is known as **appositional growth.**

   **Interstitial growth:** formation of new cartilage within existing cartilage: this is
another way in which cartilage can grow _(which is really slow)_ and is known as
**interstitial growth** _(through the middle)._ It occurs through the **division of**
**chondrocytes.** All the daughter cells will be a **clone** of the mother cells, which will
give place to clonal groups found within the lacunae, known as **isogenous groups.**

Interstitial growth is not an efficient kind of growth, as it is a **slow**
**process** that takes a long time to happen. This is why it takes lots
of time to recover from a cartilage injury; the cells will divide very
slowly. These cells can be **stimulated to grow through**
**physiotherapy:** throughout movement and exercising, we are
inducing these cells to proliferate.

CLINICAL CASE

_AGE: 38, GENDER: female. She presents a right middle finger distal_
_phalangeal swelling._
_The mass is being caused by aberrant proliferation of tissue; but not_
_bone tissue. The nature of the tissue isn’t bone, but cartilage._
_This is detected on the MRI: we can see a black line surrounding the_
_phalanges, which is found enlarged in the lesion._

_We do a biopsy of the lesion: the yellow area represents bone tissue,_

_with lines that are bone travicle_

_(spongy). Growing outside the_
_bone we can see a large mass of_
_cartilage._

_Under the microscope:_
_The lesion of the patient is just an outgrowth of cartilage, which can be resected._

*[PDF p. 155]*

#### Bone tissue

_Connective tissue: bone_

Bone tissue is a **specialized type of connective tissue** with a **calcified extracellular**

**matrix** in which characteristic cells are embedded.

BONE TISSUE FUNCTIONS

   - To **support and protect** vital organs, and fleshy structures such as hematopoietic

organs.
   - Provides a **storage site for phosphate and calcium** _(bone contains about 99% of_
_the body's calcium)_

TYPES OF BONES

   Long bones _(humerus)_
   - Flat bones _(sternum)_
   - Short bones _(talus)_

   Irregular bones _(vertebrae)_

↳
**Structure of long bones:** _epiphysis, metaphysis_

_and diaphysis._

**BONE STRUCTURE**

Bone can be divided into **compact/cortical** (dense) **bone** or **spongy** (cancellous) **bone.**

   - On the outside: **compact or cortical bone.** More dense.
Formed just by bone tissue.
   - On the inside: **cancellous bone.** We find bone, medulla,

blood vessels,...In between the cancellous bone, we find

**hematopoietic tissue: bone marrow.**

Within the bone, we find:

   - Cells: osteoblasts and osteocytes
   - Fibers: collagen fibers, specifically type I collagen. They serve as a
scaffold for mineralization of calcium and phosphate
   - Ground substance: we find water in between, but very little as it’s highly
dense tissue. Therefore, bone needs a way for the 02 and nutrients to
diffuse. This is why it is highly vascularized.

*[PDF p. 156]*

1. BONE MATRIX

**Organic matrix / Unmineralized** : More **immature**, mostly made up of **type I**
**collagen** (95%) and type V, as well as ground-substance (low water content) which
contains chondroitin sulfate, keratan sulfate and hyaluronic acid. This immature
matrix is secreted by osteocytes and will serve as a **scaffold** for calcium and
phosphate.It’s known as **osteoid.** Its function is to allow mineralization.

**Inorganic portion / Mineralized** : it’s the **calcified** part of bone, more **mature** .
Formed after calcium and phosphate are deposited on the collagen matrix. Made up
of calcium, citrate, phosphate, bicarbonate,....

**Cartilage**

- Water content: aprox 70%

- Collagen: type II, 40% of organic content

- Grows interstitially and by apposition

- Avascular tissue

**Bone**

**-** Water content: 25%

- Collagen: type I, 90% of organic content

- Grows only by appositional growth

- Highly vascularized

Other Ground substance components in bone _(bone proteins, located in between collagen_
_fibers, which also aid in scaffolding for calcification)_ :

   - **Osteonectin:** it anchors collagen to bone mineral
   - **Osteocalcin:** calcium-binding protein, involved in bone calcification

   **Osteopontin:** carries out binding of osteoblasts and osteoclasts to bone

2. PERIOSTEUM

**Periosteum** is a layer of **noncalcified connective tissue,** found covering bone on its
**external surfaces** _(except at synovial articulations and muscle attachments)._
It presents an **outer dense, fibrous collagenous layer** and an **inner cellular**
**osteoprogenitor (** _**osteogenic**_ **) layer.**

**Sharpey fibers (type I collagen) attach** the periosteum to the bone surface. It serves as
the place through which blood vessels enter and reach the bone.
Its function is to provide cells, **fibroblasts**, that will **differentiate** into **osteoblasts** which will
later on differentiate into **osteocytes** . This type of growth _(one layer on top of the other)_ is
known as **appositional growth.**

3. ENDOSTEUM

Endosteum consists of **thin, specialized connective**
**tissue** that **lines the marrow cavities (inside).** It
contains undifferentiated cells that give rise to
osteoblasts, which begin to secrete collagen type I.
Therefore, its function is to supply osteoprogenitor cells
_(fibroblasts)_ and osteoblasts for **bone growth and repair.**

*[PDF p. 157]*

4. BONE TISSUE CELLS

   - **Osteocytes** : **mature** bone cells, secreting **type I**
**collagen.**

**-** **Osteoprogenitor cells:** cells derived from
**mesenchymal stem cells**, which will originate

osteoblasts.

   - **Osteoblasts:** once they start to secrete **osteoid**
**matrix,** they become **surrounded** by it and
**differentiate** into osteocytes.

**-** **Bone lining cells:** found on **bone surface** when
there is no active growth. They are derived from
**osteoblasts that did not originate osteocytes.**

   - **Osteoclasts:** they carry out a process of
**remodeling**, by destroying bone while osteoblasts
secrete new bone tissue. They are present where
bone is being **removed/remodeled** or where
bone has been **damaged.**

**MATURE BONE ORGANIZATION**

  - On one hand, we find the **periosteum,** a **dense fibrous**
**connective tissue** layer made up of 2 sublayers:

        - An **outer fibrous layer** _(fibrous tissue)_
        - An **inner cellular layer** _(more cell content)._ It
contains the osteoprogenitor cells which will give

rise to osteoblasts.

It contains many **collagen fibers,** commonly known as **Sharpey’s fibers.** In
**ligaments and tendons,** these fibers extend directly into the bone tissue, where they
are **continuous** with the collagen fibers of the extracellular matrix of the bone tissue.
It connects bone to surrounding tissues _(ex: muscles)_

Bone is a highly vascularized tissue. It’s **vascularized**
by blood vessels that **penetrate** the matrix **from the**
**periosteum** . We can differentiate:

               **Perforating (Volkmann’s) canals**

**-**
**Central (Haversian) canals**

_Collagen has a concentric deposition surrounding_
_these concentric canals, forming layers known as_

_lamellae._

*[PDF p. 158]*

- The **endosteum** is a part of bone tissue found both in **compact bone,** facing the
marrow cavity, and in the **trabeculae of spongy bone.** It is often formed by only one
cell layer _**(endosteal cells)**_ of **osteoprogenitor cells** that will differentiate into
osteoblasts, for growth and repair.

- **Osteons:** Mature bone is largely composed of
cylindrical structures known as **osteons or**
**Haversian systems,** inside which we find the
**Harvesian canal** containing blood vessels and

nerves.

By definition, **osteons** are **concentric lamellae**
**of bone matrix** surrounding a central canal, the
**osteonal (Haversian) canal,** which contains
the **vascular and nerve supply of the osteon**
_(long axis of an osteon is usually parallel to the_
_long axis of the bone)._

**Collagen fibers** are laid down:

     - **parallel to one another in any given lamella,** but
     - in **different directions in adjacent lamellae**

This is done to make the structure even **stronger** and confer **more**
**resistance,** by creating a meshwork of collagen type I. The deposition
within the **lamellae** is parallel, but each layer is oriented in different directions.

- **Lacunae and canaliculi:** lacunae are “holes” / lagunes located within the bone
tissue, where we will find **osteocytes.** These osteocytes are **secreting bone matrix.**

Once the osteocyte is totally surrounded by organic
and inorganic matrix, it produces **prolongations** of the
cytoplasm to coordinate the process of degrading and
regenerating bone. **Inorganic matrix** makes it **difficult**
**for communication** to occur. Therefore, these

**prolongations contact each other** and **exchange**

**molecules for communication.**

These prolongations are known as **canaliculi** and are found in
**between lacunae,** connecting cells found inside said lacunae.

*[PDF p. 159]*

**IMMATURE & MATURE BONE TISSUE**

CHARACTERISTICS:

IMMATURE BONE

- Non-lamellar arrangement of collagen

- Matrix = osteoid, also known as woven bone
(unmineralized, prior to maturation)

- Matrix can also be found calcified in IMM-B. It
presents different levels of calcification

- ++ cells than mature bone, arranged randomly

- Likely to be found in the trabeculae/bone marrow

_We can observe some darker pink lines,_
_which correspond to collagen fibers. The_
_matrix is rich in type I collagen, which is_
_found in this organization: fibers laid one on_
_top of the other, becoming mineralized._
_Mineralization can be appreciated as a more_

_blue area._

_-_ _Blue = mineralized_

_-_ _Pink-ish = unmineralized._

MATURE BONE

- Organized arrangement of collagen, found
surrounding a central canal + osteocyte-containing
lacunae

- Contains cylindrical units = osteons / Harvesian
systems

- les cells, arranged concentrically

- Can be found in compact bone - also known as
lamellar bone

_Image 1: no Haversian organization. Collagen presents different colors, corresponding to_
_different stacks of calcification. Lighter pink corresponds to the osteoid, unmineralized_
_matrix, and the blueish areas are calcified. Likely to be found in the bone marrow/trabeculae_
_Image 2: several cylinders, osteons with haversian canals + lamellae where we find lacunae,_
_containing osteocytes. Can be found in the compact bone. Lamellar bone._

*[PDF p. 160]*

➢ **What is the main difference between trabeculae and cortex bone?**

The **organization** .

   - In **trabecular bone** _(immature bone),_ we **don't find osteons**, just bone tissue and
osteocytes. Collagen isn’t being deposited in concentric lamellae, but **in lines.** There
are **no central vessels** _(no Harvesian canal)_ **,** so they are therefore not concentric.
In addition to this, in immature bone, we can find **different ranges of calcification.**
Woven bone/osteoid will start to become calcified in certain areas, and remain

unmineralized in others.

   - In **cortical bone** _(mature bone),_ we **can observe these concentric structures**
known as **osteons.** In this mature bone, the **matrix is all calcified.**

_CLINICAL CASE LESION. In the image, we_
_can distinguish cartilage within the bone._
_Inside, bone marrow cells. We can find the_

_cartilage next to trabecular bone. which_
_contains osteocytes and osteoblasts lining_
_the surface, synthesizing collagen which is_
_being deposited in a linear manner._

_Calcified cartilage is gradually demolished by osteoclasts while_
_new lamellar bone is laid down by osteoblasts. After a period of_
_remodelling, the bone is restored to its previous anatomy._

➢ **What do we mean by lamellar and non-lamellar bone?**

   - **Lamellar bone = calcified bone.** It can be **concentric on non-concentric.**

Cortical bone _(concentric calcified)_ and trabecular bone _(non-concentric calcified)_
   - **Non-lamellar = unmineralized** _(non-concentric decalcified)_ . Also
known as **“woven” bone,** unmineralized spongy bone.

_In trabecular bone, the linear deposition of collagen becomes calcified and_
_therefore matured (woven → lamellar). Therefore, it becomes lamellar bone._
_It’s still, however, a non-concentric structure. Spongy bone tissue does not_
_contain the osteons that constitute compact bone tissue. Instead, it consists of_
_trabeculae, which are lamellae that are arranged as rods or plates._

**BONE MICROENVIRONMENT**

This bone microenvironment is comprised of a
**mineralized extracellular matrix and specific cell**
**types** that are under the **control** of **local and**
**systemic factors.** Bone is constantly being built and
broken down through the process of bone remodeling

                   - Once the **osteoblast** is totally
surrounded by a calcified matrix, it’s known as
osteocyte. They originate from **mesenchymal and**
**osteoprogenitor cells.**

                   - **Osteoclasts** come from

**Hematopoietic stem cells.**

*[PDF p. 161]*

**REMODELING PROCESS**

Bone mass is maintained by a **balance between formation ad destruction,** controlled by
the activity of **osteoblasts**, which form bone, and **osteoclasts**, which break it down. Bone
remodeling has to go through **several phases**

OSTEOCLASTS

**Osteoclasts** are in charge of **bone degradation,** and secrete acid, collagenases and
proteases that demineralize the bone matrix and degrade matricellular proteins such as type
I collagen. _Hydroxyacetate = calcium phosphate. Main component of bone, which is liberated_
_upon demineralization._
They are derived from **myeloid lineage cells (monocytes).** Signalling molecules _(growth_
_factors such as_ _**M-CSF**_ _and_ _**RANKL**_ _, mainly released by osteoblasts)_, which support
**osteoclastogenesis**, cause the transformation of **monocytes into osteoclasts**,
characteristic due to their **polynucleated features.**
**Osteoprotegerin** is an endogenous receptor of RANKL that **inhibits**
**osteoclastogenesis.**

Parts and characteristics

Osteoclasts are **highly polarized** _(EM image)_ . Invaginations of the
membrane on the basal face, found forming the **ruffled border,**
increase the area of the membrane, and therefore **increase the**
**surface area for bone reabsorption.** This zone is known as the
**sealing zone,** where reabsorption process takes place. **Enzymes** are
released from this zone onto mineralized bone, and as the osteoclast

advances, the bone becomes **demineralized** and all the **minerals**
**are freed,** becoming **readily available for the body** through

circulation.

_* Excessive bone loss = osteoporosis_

OSTEOBLASTS

**Osteoblasts** are in charge of **bone formation,** which originate from **mesenchymal stem**
**cells** coming from within the **bone marrow** _**.**_
Osteoclasts resorb bone, thus allowing the **release of factors** usually stored in the bone
matrix that **recruit osteoblasts** in the resorbed area. Once recruited, **osteoblasts produce**
**the new bone matrix** and **promote its mineralization,** thus completing the bone
remodeling process.

**CLINICAL CASE**

_We order an X-ray and CT scan._
_On the CT we can appreciate an_
_outgrowth of the fractured rib. The_
_outgrowth is white, corresponding_
_to compact bone._

*[PDF p. 162]*

_As this is a fracture, we see a mass of cartilage. A process of_
_bone formation is occurring._
_White big circles = Harvesian canal, in which we find blood_
_vessels and nerves. On the periphery we find periosteum. We_
_can also appreciate the way in which collagen is being_
_distributed in a concentric way, forming units known as osteon._

_Osteoclasts = multinucleated._

*[PDF p. 163]*

#### Nerve tissue

It enables the body to **respond to internal and external stimuli,** by integrating the

information.

Divided anatomically into:

**-**
**Central nervous system (CNS)**

        brain (in the skull)

        spinal cord (vertebral column)

**-**
**Peripheral nervous system (PNS)**

        peripheral nerves

        associated ganglia

        - connections to the CNS

**CELL TYPES**

   - **Neurons** : most characteristic cell/structural functional unit, responsible for
conducting electrical impulse

   **Supporting cells (neuroglia):** support, nurture and protect the neurons. Both central
and peripheral neuroglia.

**NEURONS**

Neurons are cells with lots of **cytoplasmic processes** (multiple dendrites and usually, a
single axon) **.** Regarding the body/soma of the cell: it has a **central nucleus** and a **very**
**developed nucleolus.** In the cytoplasm we can also find **dark, basophilic granules**
_(chromatophilic substances)._

**Identified structures/components:**

   Cell body (soma)

   Centered nucleus + developed nucleolus

   - Dendrites + dendritic branches

   - Axon hillock

   - Axon

   Synaptic terminals

Some neurons are really large, but they are of **variable size.** Range from the smallest to the
biggest cells in the body (ranging from 5 to 150 μm in diameter)

**Morphofunctional regions:**

   - **Receptor region:** formed by the **dendrites and soma of the neuron. Receives the**
**signal** from another neuron or cell.
   - **Generation and conduction of the electrical impulse:** formed in the **axon hillock**
and conducted through the **axon.**
   - **Synapse region:** the **impulse will be transmitted** to another neuron or effector cell.

*[PDF p. 164]*

Depending on how these regions are distributed, we can differentiate different regions in
CNS and PNS. For example, in the spinal cord:

**Spinal cord. Neuronal cell distribution**

Darker inner region and paler outer region.

The distribution of neuronal cell bodies and axons

allows us to differentiate between **white and**

**gray matter in CNS** and **ganglia and nerves in**

**the PNS.**

   **Gray matter and ganglia : Neuronal cell**
**bodie** s (CNS and PNS, respectively)

   - **White matter and nerves: No neuronal**

**cell bodies,** just neuronal processes (and
supporting cells)

**Functional classification of neurons**

Neurons are spatially organized in chains and networks. We have 3 different
chains/components:

   - **Sensory neurons: receive** information from the internal and external environment.
They **conduct impulses to the CNS** for processing and analysis.

   - **Interneurons:** conduct information and **connect** different neurons in a chain of

information. They generally connect sensory and motor neurons. In charge of
regulating signals transmitted to neurons. **Most abundant** type of neurons we have.

   - **Motor neurons: transmit** the information **from the CNS** to the motor cell/effector

cell in order to exert the response (muscles and glands), or to other neurons.

**Morphological classification** _(focusing on the processes):_

   - **Pseudounipolar neurons:** a **single process** that extends from the cell
body and subsequently branches into a **peripheral process and central**
**process.** Not very abundant, found mainly in **spinal and ganglia.**

   - **Bipolar neuron:** a **single axon and a single dendrite.** Found in some
**sensory organs.** Also not very abundant.

   - **Multipolar neurons:** a **single axon and**
**more than one dendrite**, with different

branching degrees. These are the **most**
**abundant** type in vertebrates.

*[PDF p. 165]*

**Neuronal cell body. Soma**

The region of a neuron containing the **nucleus, various cytoplasmic**
**organelles and inclusions, and cytoskeletal elements.**

**●** **The nucleus**

        - It's a **large, spherical nucleus** (euchromatin, uncondensed
= **pale staining** ).
        - **Centrally** located within the soma
        - **Large, well developed nucleolus** within **abundant**

**euchromatin.** We see a dark nucleolus in the center of a

very euchromatic **(pale)** nucleus _(owl-eye)._

  - **Cytoplasmic organelles**

        - **Nissl bodies** : granular structures containing
**polyribosomes and RER,** which stain **basophilic** .
Very abundant in the cytoplasm, they appear as
**clumps** under light microscopy. **Abundant in large,**

**motor neurons.**

        - **Lysosomes:** We can also find **abundant** lysosomes

        - **Mitochondria:** scattered through the cytoplasm

        - **Golgi apparatus:** located near the nucleus.

  - **Inclusions**

        - **Lipofuscin-containing granules:** formed by **residual bodies** of lysosomes.
The number increases with age

        **Lipid droplets** (occasionally present)

**-**
**Melanin-containing droplets**

**●** **Cytoskeletal components**

We will also find a **very well developed cytoskeleton,** for the **transport of synaptic**
**vesicles** from the region of production through the axon all the way to the region of
synapse. They also **maintain the structure** of the neurons.

        **Neurofilaments (10nm):** abundant, throughout

the soma

**-** **Microtubules (24 nm):** in the soma

**-**
**Microfilaments (actin filaments, 6 nm):**
associated with the plasma membrane.

*[PDF p. 166]*

**Dendrites**

**Receive stimuli** (signals) from sensory cells, axons, or other neurons and convert these
signals into small **electrical impulses** _(NOT action potentials yet)_ transmitted toward the

soma.

Generally branched **(arborized structures)** _(except in bipolar neurons)_ **,** allowing neurons to
**obtain stimuli from very different neurons** .
Besides this, within the branches they contain very different **spines** which play a very
**important role in transmission** as they increase the area available for synapse formation.
These spines **decrease with age and poor nutrition.**

   - Its cytoplasm is **similar** to that of the soma
   - With **reduced number of organelles** as they are not necessary,
with the exception of mitochondria
   - **Abundant mitochondria** (necessary)

**Axons**

Axons **conduct impulses** away from the soma to the axon terminals without any diminution
in their strength.

**Axon hillock**

Axons **originate from the axon hillock,** which is continuous with the soma
_(can be considered as a specialized region of the soma)_ ; however, in this

axon hillock:

   - There are **no Nissl bodies,** which are characteristic of the soma.
   - They also have a very **abundant network of microtubules and**
**neurofilaments** which will allow the passage of mitochondria and

vesicles into the axons.

   It is the region where the **electrical impulse is generated.**

**Axon**

   - The proper axon has a surrounding plasma membrane, known as **axolemma**

   Cytoplasm known as **axoplasm:**

        **No RER or Golgi** as it won’t be in charge of any synthesis

        However, it contains **abundant cytoskeleton**
        - Also contains **SER and elongated mitochondria.**

   It derives into many **small branches = axon terminals,** regions
where **synapsis** will take place. Impulses are passed to another
neuron or other types of cells.

**-**
**Protected by glial cells**, in general because the axon is a very
thin, long structure and if it gets **damaged**, we **risk losing many**
**connections.** They can be **up to 1m long,** so they have to be
protected, and this is done by glial cells.

*[PDF p. 167]*

**Axonal transport systems**

In the axons, mostly in the membrane, we have to transport molecules. This is a bidirectional

mechanism:

   **Anterograde transport:** from the cell body to axon terminals _(periphery)_, where
there is **regeneration** of proteins and vesicles. **MAPs,** such as **kinesin**, plays an
important role in anterograde transport.

   **Retrograde transport:** from axon terminal to body. It’s the way of **recycling**
material. **Dynein** plays important role in this retrograde transport.

**Recognizing neurons under microscope:**

   Clear, pale nucleus, clearly well-centered

   Dark, condensed and developed nucleolus

   Basophilic clumps, Nissl bodies, found
within the cytoplasm of the soma

   Large size with a characteristic shape

**NEUROGLIAL CELLS**

Found both in **CNS and PNS.** The neuroglia is a web of tissue composed of modified

ectodermal elements.

Main function of neuroglial cells is to **support and protect** neurons **.** But they’re also
involved in synapse.
They **don’t conduct neurological impulse,** but are involved in synapsis by providing some
regulation of neurons in the process of neural transmissions through:

   Actively **monitoring synapses**

   **Removing neurotransmitters** from synapses

   Delivering molecules known as **gliotransmitter substances** into the synaptic space

From an imaging point of view, we **can’t really differentiate** the different neuroglial cells
under conventional staining. We have to use specific staining, **gold and silver stains.**

_-_ **IN THE CNS:**

**Astrocytes**

They are very branched, with **long extended processes.** Astrocytes are the largest of
neuroglial cells, characterized by having many processes, with different **functions:**

   **Surrounding blood vessels** through expanded **pedicles (vascular feet).** They
participate in reinforcing the **blood-brain barrier:** it tightly **regulates exchange and**
**passage** of substances from blood to brain stroma.
   - **Surrounding neurons,** scavenging for **ions** involved in **synapses** after they have
completed their function, and **supplying energy** for metabolism

   In contact with meninges (pia mater), also creating a protective barrier. They form a
**protective sealed barrier** between the **pia mater and the nervous tissue.**
   - In the brain, astrocytes form **scar tissue (glial scar)** after **injury** to the CNS.

*[PDF p. 168]*

**Different types of astrocytes** :

  - **Protoplasmic astrocytes:** .

        Mainly found in **gray matter**
        - Have **short processes but very arborized**, which have the mentioned
functions _(enveloping blood vessels, neurons, and synaptic areas)_ .
        - In these cells, we can find some **intermediate filaments** composed of glial
fibrillar acidic protein **(GFAP).**
        - Mainly involved in the **formation of blood brain barrier** and its maintenance

  - **Fibrous astrocytes:**

        - Mainly found in **white matter**
        - **long, slender processes** with **little arborization,**
carrying out characteristic functions.
        - Many **intermediate filaments** composed of **GFAP**

**Blood-brain barrier**

In CNS there is very tight regulation of the diffusion of molecules from blood into nerve
tissue. This is mainly controlled by the **blood-brain barrier,** composed of **tight junctions**
formed between **adjacent endothelial cells** in the capillary walls surrounding nerve tissue.
Tight junctions exert a **very restricted regulation:**

   - 02, H20, C02 and certain liposoluble molecules can pass via **diffusion**

   Some Ions can pass through **active transport**

   Macromolecules and drugs have to go through **receptor**
**mediated transport.**

This barrier is **supported by the feet of astrocytes.** These
astrocytes **don’t form occluding junctions,** they **reinforce those**
**formed** between endothelial cells **by connecting through**
**anchoring junctions.** They themselves don’t create the tight
junctions but reinforce them.

**Oligodendrocytes**

Oligodendrocytes live **symbiotically** with neurons. They are essential in the **survival** of
neurons in the CNS. They **protect the axons of neurons** in the CNS. Can be found both in
**grey and white matter.**

**Characteristics**

   They have a **small, condensed centered nucleus**

   They contain an **electron dense cytoplasm.**

        - Ribosomes

        - Numerous microtubules

        - Mitochondria

        - RER

        Large Golgi complex

*[PDF p. 169]*

- Have a **few short processes.**
The short processes **surround the axon and create concentric layers of myelin,**
myelin sheaths, which will play an important role in the **electrical transmission.**
Oligodendrocytes form **several processes**, and each process produces myelin for a
single internode. Therefore, **each oligodendrocyte can myelinate several axons.**

**Microglial cells**

Very similar to astrocytes under the microscope.
They are small, **phagocytic** neuroglial cells derived from the **mononuclear phagocytic cell**
**population in the bone marrow.**

**Characteristics**

   Present a condensed, elongated nucleus

   - Many short, branching processes. Very **arborized**

   - They are sort of the **immune cells of the CNS.**
When **activated**, they **change their shape,** loose branching and
become more similar to **macrophages.** They start to **secrete**
**cytokines** which recruit other immune cells (T cells); they act as

**APCs.**

**Ependymal cells**

   - Derived from the **neuroepithelium**

   Located in very particular places: they are the **epithelial cells**

that **line the neural tube and ventricles of the brain**

   - In certain regions of the brain, they contain **cilia** _(responsible_
_for moving the_ _**cerebrospinal fluid**_ _(CSF))_

   - Modified ependymal cells contribute to the **formation** of the
**choroids plexus** _(It is a highly organized tissue that lines all_
_the ventricles of the brain. Resides in the innermost layer of the_
_meninges (pia mater) which is in close contact with the cerebral_
_cortex and spinal cord)._

*[PDF p. 170]*

_-_ **IN THE PNS:**

**Schwann cells**

Their function is **similar** to that of **oligodendrocytes** . They **protect and isolate the**
**neurons,** same as oligodendrocytes in the CNS. But they **exert their functions in cells of**

**the PNS.**

Oligodendrocytes are **flat cells (** with **basement membrane)** with only a **few mitochondria**
and a **small Golgi region.**
They can form:

   **myelinated covers**, surrounding the axons

   **unmyelinated covers** : in this situation, Schwann cells are just found surrounding the

axon.

A **single Schwann cell** can only insulate a **single axon** in a myelinated fiber, whereas a
single oligodendrocyte may insulate several axons.

_**Neuroglial cells. Origin and functions**_

**SYNAPSES**

Synapse consists in the **signal transmission from one neuron to another** or **to another**
**cell type** _(e.g., muscle cell)_ . Classified according to the site of synaptic contact and the
method of signal transmission:

**Classification according to site**

  - **Axodendritic synapses:** most common type,

between **axon and dendrite**

  - **Axosomatic synapses:** between **axon and soma**

  - **Axoaxonic synapses:** between **2 axons,** much less

common

*[PDF p. 171]*

**Classification according to signal transmission**

  - **Chemical synapses. Unidirectional**

        - **Most common** type of **neuron-neuron synapse,** and the **only** type of
**muscle-neuron synapse** _(neuromuscular junction)._
        - Occurs by the release of **neurotransmitters** from the
presynaptic cell which reach and act on the
postsynaptic cell.

        - The **transmission will be slower** than the electrical

synapse as it is a longer process. **Delayed** by about **0.5**
**ms,** time required for secretion and diffusion of
neurotransmitter from the presynaptic membrane into
the synaptic cleft and then to the postsynaptic

membrane.

        - Also important: **neurotransmitters themselves don’t**
**effect the change;** they only **activate a response** in
the receptor cell.

  - **Electrical synapses. Bidirectional**

        - Much **less numerous** than chemical synapses

        - Consists in the **movement of ions** from one neuron to

another **via gap junctions.**

        They **transmit the action potential** of the presynaptic cell
**directly** to the postsynaptic cell
        - It’s **much faster** than the chemical synapse. Signal
transmission across these synapses is **nearly**

**instantaneous**

**Synaptic morphology (chemical synapse)**

**Axon terminal:**

   - Axon terminals can have **different shapes.** They vary
morphologically, depending on the site of contact.

   They contain **synaptic vesicles** _**(distinguishable**_
_**morphological features)**_
   - Contain **voltage-gated calcium channels** which will play
important role in the process of synapse _(regulate the entry_
_of Ca2+ into the axon terminal)_
   - The region of the **presynaptic membrane** in “contact” with
the postsynaptic has a **thicker membrane** in the area of

synapses.

   **Synaptic vesicles** fuse with vesicles and release the containing neurotransmitters
into the synaptic space.

**The postsynaptic membrane:**

The membrane of the neuron or other target cell that is receiving the impulse is **also**

**thickened.**

*[PDF p. 172]*

**Synaptic cleft:**

It’s the narrow space _(20-30 nm wide)_ **between presynaptic - postsynaptic** membranes.
Space **neurotransmitters** have to diffuse across to reach the next neuron.

**Astrocytes:**

Astrocytes protect the synaptic region. They **aren’t actively involved** in
synapsis, but **participate** by:

   **removing neurotransmitters** from the synaptic space **(glutamate)**
   - **secrete** molecules that will play a role in **regulating synapse**
**(gliotransmitters)**

**Synaptic vesicles and neurotransmitters:**

   **Synaptic vesicles:** small, membrane-bound structures
in the **axoplasm** of the transmitting neuron. Synaptic
vesicles **discharge neurotransmitters** into the
**synaptic cleft** by **exocytosis.** Can be seen through EM
like small vesicles with a membrane, found in the
cytoplasm of the transmitting neuron.

   - **Neurotransmitters** : Produced, stored (vesicles),
and released by **presynaptic neurons.**
Released by **exocytosis** : they **diffuse** through
the synaptic cleft and bind different **receptors in**
**the postsynaptic neuron,** facilitating the
transmission of the electrical impulse. They
generate an **action potential:**

_-_
_Transmitter-gated channels_

_-_
_G-protein–coupled receptors_

_EM image:_

**Neuromuscular junction**

Particular type of synapses: **Neuromuscular junction / Motor end plate** (muscle
innervation). It’s a type of **chemical synapse.**

**1 Axon can innervate several muscle cells,** and therefore:

   - **Motor unit** refers to the group of muscle cells **innervated**
**by the same motor neurons**
   - **Motor end plate** is the large area where the **different**
**synapses take place.** We can see that the axons branch

into **several axon terminals.**

*[PDF p. 173]*

_Focusing on one motor end plate, we focus on a neuromuscular junction:_

The general structure is the one already seen in chemical synapses. The special feature is
that **in the postsynaptic membrane,** there are **junctional folds.** These are deep
**invaginations** of the muscle cell plasma membrane ( **sarcolemma** ) which **increase the**
**surface area** for the neurotransmitter **(ACh)** to interact.

**NERVE FIBERS**

**1 axon = 1 nerve fiber.**

**Motor neurons:**

The **axon** leaves **CNS**, and in the **PNS** the nerve fibers (individual axons)
group, forming bundles which give place to **nerves.**

   - In the **CNS**, enveloped by **Oligodendrocytes**, with **myelin sheath**
   - In **PNS**, enveloped by **Schwann cells, myelinated or**
**unmyelinated sheaths**

**Myelin sheath**

   Axon recovered by concentric myelin layers
   - Produced by **oligodendrocytes** in the **CNS** and by **Schwann cells** in the **PNS**

   Deposited in **concentric layers** forming an **insulating sheet.** Seen as several spiral
layers of the **plasma membrane** of an oligodendrocyte or Schwann cell **wrapping**

**around the axon**

   - However this layer is **not continuous.** It has the length that a single Schwann
cell/oligodendrocyte can cover. These **gaps** left between adjacent glial cells are

known as **nodes of Ranvier.**

   - The **thickness** of these layers is usually **constant along the length of an axon;**
however, thickness usually **increases** as the **axonal diameter increases.**

**Process of formation:**

   Schwann cell/oligodendrocyte starts to wrap around a portion of an axon;
their **cytoplasm and plasma membrane** begin to form **consecutive spiral layers**

around the axon

   Overlapping inner layers of the Schwann
cell/oligodendrocyte plasma membrane form the **myelin**

**sheath.**

   - The **nucleus** gets **pushed to the periphery** of the cell.

*[PDF p. 174]*

_Image 1: nucleus of the Schwann cell (SC) + axon (A) embedded in its cytoplasm and_
_recovered by myelin sheath (M); inside said axon, we see microtubules (MT) and_
_neurofilaments (NF); on the top right, we see another SC surrounding an axon and a forming_
_myelin sheath (FM); on the bottom right, another axon-surrounding SC, but in this case the_
_recovering is unmyelinated (UM)._

_Image 2: axon, with microtubules (MT) and NF. We can find organelles (mitochondria, Mit) +_
_a surrounding myelin sheath (M). The surrounding cytoplasm corresponds to that of a_
_schwann cell. Extracellular granules (C) correspond to collagen fibers, transversal cut._

**Schmidt-Lanterman clefts**

The Schwann cell or oligodendrocyte’s cytoplasm is found **surrounding**
**the myelin sheath,** but also **in between** the myelin sheath and the

axon. There has to be **communication** between the inner and outer

cytoplasm of the recovering cells. This is done through **incisions** in
myelin, known as **Schmidt-Lanterman clefts.**
They are **oblique discontinuities of the myelin sheath** due to the
presence of Schwann cell (or oligodendrocyte) cytoplasm within the
myelin.

**Nodes of Ranvier**

Nodes of Ranvier are **periodic gaps** in the insulating sheath **between Schwann cells or**
**oligodendrocytes.** It **facilitates** the **rapid conduction** of nerve impulses.

Differences between PNS and CNS:

   - In **PNS**, the small unprotected area (node) is covered
by **interdigitated cytoplasmic processes** of the
adjacent Schwann cells, which **protect** the axon.
   - In **CNS**, the myelin free surface at the node is
covered by a **foot plate of an astrocyte, protecting**
those unmyelinated regions.

In these regions there are **abundant Na+ voltage gated**
**channels** to **facilitate “saltatory” conduction.**

*[PDF p. 175]*

**Internodes**

**Length between adjacent Nodes of Ranvier** (extension of the recovering cells which will
give place to the myelin sheets).
Vary in length **from 0.08 to 1 mm,** depending on the size of the Schwann cells or
oligodendrocytes associated with the fiber.

**Myelinated nerve fibers**

_Cross section and longitudinal view of a peripheral nerve._ We can see the axons and
epineurium on the left image. _We can also distinguish Schmidt-Lanterman clefts and Nodes_

_and Ranvier._

**Unmyelinated nerve fibers**

Surrounded by a **large number of Schwann cells.**
In the case of **SC unmyelinated fibers,** a **single SC is seen to recover**
**many axons** at a time. Many axons embedded within its cytoplasm.
Remember: only SC give rise to unmyelinated fibers.

*[PDF p. 176]*

**Myelinated vs Unmyelinated**

   - In **CNS**, **oligodendrocytes** only produce
**myelinated fibers.** In **PNS** they can be **myelinated**
**or unmyelinated (SCs)**

   Since impulses in myelinated axons jump from one
node of Ranvier to another, **propagation** of the
impulse is much **faster in myelinated axons.** For
unmyelinated fibers, it has to be continuously
regenerated so as not to lose action potential.

**-**
**Myelinated SC → 1 axon; Unmyelinated SC → several axons**

**Myelinated-related diseases**

**Multiple sclerosis, (MS),** the typical disease related to unmyelinated fibers which should be
myelinated.

**NERVES**

Nerves are **cordlike bundles** made up of **nerve fibers,**
surrounded by **connective tissue sheaths.** Within each
nerve bundle it is difficult to distinguish any other cells.
Whitish surrounding structure (light microscopy): **myelin**

Connective tissue components: we can differentiate three
layers:

   - **Epineurium:** the **outermost** . It consists of **dense fibrous connective tissue**
(fascia), found **surrounding nerve bundles.** Often embedded in adipose tissue.

   - **Perineurium: surrounds each bundle of nerve fibers** (fascicle) **.**
Made up of **specialized connective tissue** cells, which **block and regulate** the
transmission of specific molecules by binding through **tight junctions.** Form the
**blood-nerve barrier,** preventing the transmission of toxic components.

   - **Endoneurium:** connective tissue layer that **surrounds each individual nerve fiber.**
Made up of a thin layer of **reticular fibers and collagen,** containing some

**fibroblasts.**

*[PDF p. 177]*

**GANGLIA**

Encapsulated aggregations of **neuronal cell bodies (soma)** outside the CNS + abundant
small glial cells **(satellite cells).** Found containing the neuronal bodies.
_Neurons surrounded by satellite cells (glial cells marked by blue arrow heads)_

**WHITE MATTER & GRAY MATTER**

Both present in the CNS. We will differentiate them by identifying the different parts of the

neuron.

**Gray matter**

   - Neuronal cell bodies **(soma)**

   - Dendrites

   Some axons: mostly unmyelinated fibers +
some myelinated fibers

   Neuroglial cells

**White matter**

   - **Neural axons:** mostly myelinated nerve fibers
+ some unmyelinated

   Neuroglial cells

There are 2 very clear regions in the **CNS:**
**In the spinal cord:**

   - **Grey matter** located in the **center** of the spinal

cord

   - **Outer white matter**

**In the cerebellum:**

   - **Grey matter** in the **outer layer**
   - **White matter** in the **inner layer**

**MENINGES**

**Membranous coverings of brain and spinal cord,** formed by **connective**
**tissue.** Found protecting the inner nerve tissue. 3 layers:

**-** **Dura Mater:** Outermost, lining the bony skull and close to vertebrae.
**-** **Arachnoid Mater:** Intermediate, abutting the dura
**-** **Pia Mater:** Innermost, highly vascular. Lying directly on the surface
of the brain and spinal cord.

*[PDF p. 178]*



*[PDF p. 179]*

#### Muscle histology I. Skeletal muscle

Muscle cells are specialized elongated cells with the **primary roles of contraction.**

**OVERVIEW**

Muscle can be classified into **2 major groups:**

  - **Striated muscle** _(cells present striations)_

**-** **skeletal muscle**

**-** **cardiac muscle**

**●** **Smooth muscle** _(no striations)_

Muscle cells are **contractile filaments,** whose major components are **actin and myosin**

**filaments.**

Regarding contraction, we find:

**-**
**Voluntary contraction** _(skeletal muscle)_

   **Involuntary contraction** _(in cardiac and smooth muscle)_

**Skeletal muscle** is found attached to **bones and / or skin;**
**cardiac muscle** is located in the **walls of the heart; smooth**
**muscle** located in almost all the **visceral organs** of the body:
arteries, airways,... The appearance of the different types varies
greatly.

**CONNECTIVE TISSUE IN MUSCLE**

Found surrounding and giving structure to the muscles. Connective tissue **conveys neural**
**and vascular elements** to muscle cells and provides a vehicle that **harnesses the forces**

**of muscle contraction.**

Organization:

  - **Epimysium** : surrounds the **entire muscle** and forms a connection between skeletal
muscle to **other muscles and tendons**, which at the same time connect to **bone** . It

will be important in **conveying the contraction** to tendons = pull on bones.

  - **Perimysium** : layer of connective tissue surrounding **each muscle bundle.** Also
mostly found in **skeletal muscle.**

  - **Endomysium** : surrounding **each individual muscle cell.** It’s composed of collagen
fibers, reticular fibers and some fibroblasts. In skeletal, smooth and cardiac muscle.

*[PDF p. 180]*

**SKELETAL MUSCLE**

_Skeletal muscle in longitudinal view:_

   - **Multinucleated** cells (fibers)
   - Nuclei are located at the **periphery** of the cell
   - **Long, large** cells **, cylindrical** in shape
   - The cytoplasm presents **striations** which
corresponds to the myofibrils and thin filaments

_Skeletal muscle in cross-section:_

   In the cross sectional views, we can also see these **cylindrical**

structures

**-** **Multinucleated**

   - **Peripheral** location
   - Surrounded by **connective tissue layers** and many **blood**
**vessels** (BV), as it is highly vascularized.

Types of skeletal muscle

We can distinguish 3 types:

**-**
**Red fibers (type I)**

**-**
**White fibers (type IIb)**

**-**
**Intermediate fibers (type IIa)**

Different coloring patterns. Specific staining for metabolic components also reveals that the
**metabolic components** for each of these cells are **different** .

**These muscle types differ in:**

   Contractile speed

   Metabolic profile

   - Size

**RED FIBERS** _= slow fibers (type I)_

   **Slow contraction, but don’t fatigue**
**easily** (due to high ox. activity)
   - Abundant **myoglobin:** O2 storage point,
contains iron → gives red fibers their color

   - Abundant mitochondria

   Strong oxidative capacity

   Smaller, thinner fibers

   - More vascularized

   In muscles that contract frequently
_(diaphragm, lower extremities,..)_

**WHITE FIBERS** _= fast fibers (type IIb)_

**-**
**Fast contraction but fatigue easily**

   Low myoglobin
   - Few mitochondria, as they have a **fast**
**anaerobic glycolysis metabolism**

   Larger, more myofibrils

   - Less vascularized

   - Found in muscles that contract less

frequently _(eyeball muscles)_

*[PDF p. 181]*

Skeletal muscle cell characteristics

   - 1 cell = 1 fiber.

   - **Multinucleated** and **peripheral** location

   Long, cylindrical nuclei

   Enveloped by an **external lamina (basement membrane) and reticular fibers**

   Has a plasma membrane known as **sarcolemma;** The sarcolemma forms deep
invagination known as **T tubules →** together with terminal cisternae, form **triads**

   The cytoplasm is known as **sarcoplasm**, where the most abundant component are
cylindrical collections: **myofibrils**

   In the cytoplasm we will also find the **sarcoplasmic**
**reticulum,** surrounding the myofibrils. It’s **specialized**

**SER** which **stores Ca2+**

   - Small abundant **Golgi**, surrounding the nucleus

**-** **Mitochondria**

**-**
**Glycogen**

**Myofibrils**

The abundant myofibrils are **1-2 micrometers in diameter** and
extend the whole length of the cell.
They’re formed by bundles of **thick and thin fibers,** aligned in
a specific **pattern that gives place to striations** _(Banding_
_pattern visible in light microscopy: dark bands = A bands, light_
_bands = I bands, which are bisected by Z discs)._
This repeated pattern forms the contractile unit of the muscle

cell: the **sarcomere** .

➢ **Sarcomere: formed by portion between two Z discs.**

_Green arrows: mitochondria_

_Yellow: sort of vesicles, surrounding the myofibrils =_
_sarcoplasmic reticulum + T tubules_

_Try to identify the banding pattern and the different lines_

Structure of the sarcomere

**Contractile unit of striated muscle.**

Basic components:

   - In the middle, darker **A band**
   - Lighter **I band,** cut through the middle by **Z discs** _(each I band_
_on the sides of the sarcomere is bisected by the Z discs = we_
_have two halves of different I bands)_

   In the middle of the A band, paler region = **H zone,**

   - In the middle of the H zone we can find darker lines

corresponding to **M lines**

**Each sarcomere = A band + 2 halves of 2 different I bands**

*[PDF p. 182]*

Structural components:
Myofilaments:

   Thick filaments = mostly **myosin**
   - Thin filaments = mostly **actin**
In between, many proteins stabilize them and maintain

the structure:

   - **Titin** _(myosin associated),_ an elastic protein that

**binds thick filaments to Z disc**

   - **alpha-Actinin** _(at Z disc),_ bound to actin
   - **Desmin** _(at Z disc),_ which binds Z discs to the
plasma membrane to transmit the force of the
contraction to the whole cell)

**-**
**Dystrophin** _(binding actin to cell membrane)_

Myofilaments. Thick and thin
They lie **parallel to the long axis** of the myofibril in a precise arrangement that is
responsible for the sarcomere banding pattern.

**Thick filaments**

   Diameter and length: 15nm diameter, 1.5 micrometers long
   - Each thick filament consists of **many myosin molecules,** whose **heads** protrude at
**opposite ends of the filament**
   - Each myosin molecule _(= myosin II):_ composed of **2 heads** _(at each end)_ **and 2 tails**
The **heavy chains** which form the **tail** constitute the **central bare zone.** Central part
of myosin molecules lack myosin heads
   - **Light chains** form the myosin **heads**, with **bipolar** location _(heads at the two_
_terminal regions of the thick filament)_ Located at the **interaction sites** with actin.

Accessory proteins:
   - **Titin** : bound to titin, **elastic** protein which **acts as a spring** and anchors thick
filaments (myosin) to the **Z disc.**
   - **Myomesin** : located at the **central region (M line),** to maintain thick filament
cross-linking

**Thin filaments**

   - 7 nm in diameter _(half the thickness)_ and 1.0 μm long
   - Thin filament (F-actin) consists of **2 strands of actin**
**subunits** (G-monomers) twisted into a **helix**

   In addition, 2 regulatory proteins are bound: **troponin and**
**tropomyosin,** covering the actin active sites
_-_ _**F-actin:**_ _a polymer of G-actin monomers arranged in a double_
_helix, which posses an active site that will interact with myosin_

*[PDF p. 183]*

Accessory proteins:

   **Tropomyosin:** two-stranded alpha-helical coiled protein, located in the grooves of
the F-actin helix _(bound to its_ _**active sites**_ _)_
   - **Troponin** _:_ binds to calcium and reveals active sites

        **Troponin T (TnT):** binds troponin complex to tropomyosin

        **Troponin I (TnI):** binds to actin and troponin T
        - **Troponin C (TnC):** binds **Ca2+**

**-**
**Alpha-actinin and CapZ**

   **Tropomodulin and nebulin:** other associated proteins that help in stabilization

They are organized in a parallel disposition which gives muscle its characteristic **striations.**

Sarcomere bands and striations

How structure corresponds to the images:

  - **A bands:** **overlapping between thick and thin filaments.** 6 thin

filaments surround each thick filament. It stains and is seen as a **dark**

**band.**

When muscle contracts, the A band **doesn’t change size.**

  - **I bands:** The band corresponds to a region where there are **only thin filaments.**
Appears as a **lightly stained band.** Bisected by the Z discs.

  - **H bands: light zone** in the **middle of the A band.**
Corresponds to the **bare region** of thick filaments, where there
are no myosin heads, **only myosin tails.**

  - **M lines:** narrow, **dark lines** in the **middle of the H zone.**
Correspond to **cross-connections** _**(M bridges)**_ between
adjacent thick filaments. They are crosslinked by **myomesin**
_(accessory protein of myosin)_ located on the M line.

  - **Z discs: sarcomere stabilization**

Z discs are found **bisecting the I bands.** Their function is to
stabilize the sarcomere. They have many different proteins,
highlighting:

        - Titin, for the stabilization of **thick filaments**
        - alpha-actinin and Cap Z, which bind **thin filaments to the Z**

**discs** to stabilize them with the assistance of **nebulin**

Also important to have the different sarcomeres bound among
them + to other components of the cell:

        - Desmin: In this regard desmin plays a major role:

       - Connecting sarcomeres between them by
**anchoring Z discs**

       - **Binds myofibrils to other components** of the cell,
like cytoskeleton and cytoplasm = **distributes**
**contraction force** throughout the cell

*[PDF p. 184]*

- Costameres: Peripherally located Z disks are **anchored to the sarcolemma**
through costameres. They are important to **transmit contraction** to the

whole cell and external environment.

Connection **to adjacent cells** or fibres also occurs through costameres.
        - Dystrophin: binds the **cytoskeleton of the cell to the ECM** through the
sarcolemma. Important to **transmit contraction force to the extracellular**
**region** .
Alterations in dystrophin cause effects on muscular movement that lead to
**muscular dystrophies.**

Longitudinal view:
_Image: N= nuclei; A band, I band, Z_
_bands and H zone; a darker nuclei,_

_more heterochromatic and flattened_

_= fibroblast, corresponding to a_
_connective tissue nuclei (CT)._

Transverse view: ( _B&W)_

T-Tubule system

**Deep invaginations of the sarcolemma,** very important for regulating the contraction of the
muscle cell. Responsible for **conducting the depolarization deep into the muscle fiber,**
thus propagating the signal and triggering release of Ca2+
T (transverse) tubules:

   - Run **perpendicular (transverse)** to the myofibrils

   **Conduct membrane depolarization** deep into fibers

Sarcoplasmic reticulum

Found **surrounding the myofibrils.**

   - It's **specialized SER,** found forming a **meshwork** surrounding each of the myofibrils.

   Very important in contraction as it **regulates calcium content inside the cells:**

    - Sequestering Ca2+ ions – **Relaxation**

    - Releasing Ca2+ ions – **Contraction**
   - There are dilations in the SER in the areas **proximate to**
**the T tubules,** known as **terminal cisternae.**
   - This structure formed by the **T tubule + the terminal**

**cisternae** at both sides are known as **triads.** In

mammals, these triads are in the **intersection between I**

**and A bands.** Important structures as they help to
provide **uniform contraction** throughout the muscle cell.

*[PDF p. 185]*

_Longitudinal cross section:_ _we can observe the triads; but there_
_is something abnormal, their location, as this section_
_corresponds to an amphibian and not mammalian cell. The_
_triads are located on the Z discs, not between the A-I bands._

Muscle motor innervation. Neuromuscular junction

**Bunch of muscle fibers innervated by the same neuron =**
**motor end plate** _(several neuromuscular junctions)._ However, each fiber is innervated by 1
and only 1 neuron.

**Synapse** :

   - **Action potential** in the motor neuron stimulates release
of **acetylcholine** from axon terminal into synaptic cleft
   - **Junctional folds** → grooves of the sarcolemma to
increase **surface area,** where Ach-receptors are located.

   Acetylcholine in synaptic cleft binds **Na+ ligand-gated**
**channel receptors** and initiates **sarcolemma AP**

**Signal propagation:**

   - In the process of transmitting action potential, **T**
**tubules** play a very important role.
They **conduct membrane depolarization**
**deep into the cell**

   - The terminal cisternae of the **SER**

(sarcoplasmic ret.), closely located to T tubules,
detects this depolarization through
**voltage-gated channels,** which **releases**
**calcium in response.**

   After contraction, **Ca2+ pumps** put calcium
back into the SER to stop the process

**Contraction. Sliding filament theory**
**Changes in the amount of overlap** between thick and thin filaments within the sarcomeres

allows for **contraction and relaxation** of muscle fibers.

   - Sliding theory → Sliding of the **thick filaments over the thin filaments** = contraction

   Makes the sarcomere become shorter, and the cell contracts.

   Relaxes when sarcomere goes back to its original position.
   - **Many sarcomeres contracting at the same time** results in muscle **contraction** .
   - It’s a **calcium and ATP (energy) dependent** process
During contraction:

   - **Z discs** move **closer** together

   - **I band** becomes **smaller** as the actin

filaments slide towards the M line,

over myosin.

   - **H band** also becomes **smaller** : as

the actin slides onto myosin, this area
is no longer a bare region of only tails
   - **A band** (myosin) remains
**unchanged**

*[PDF p. 186]*

Muscle sensory innervation

**Muscle spindle** (stretch receptor): muscle spindles are proprioceptors _(somatic sensory_
_system),_ therefore **detect the degree of stretching and tension** in a muscle.

Forming this muscle spindle, we find intrafusal muscle fibers and other components:

   **Spindle cells** are modified skeletal muscle cells. These cells which make up the
muscle spindle are also known as **intrafusal fibers.** Extrafusal fibers are only
involved in contraction, not proprioception.

   - **Nerve fibers:** spindle cells receive both sensory and autonomic motor innervation:

    - Sensory: convey stretch information. Afferent fibers I and II

    - Motor: intrafusal fibers are innervated by **gamma motor neurons** _(allow_
_contraction of the intrafusal fibers)_, and extrafusal, by alpha motor neurons.
Difference? Alpha motor neurons control muscle contraction involved in
voluntary movement, whereas gamma motor neurons control muscle
contraction in response to external forces acting on the muscle. Gamma
motor neurons **induce the involuntary, reflexive movement called the**

**stretch reflex.**

   **Capsules:**

        - Internal: surrounds group of spindle cells and nerve fibers
        - External connective tissue sheath around the whole spindle

Skeletal muscle regeneration. Satellite cells

Skeletal muscle can **hypertrophy** by building up protein.
Skeletal muscles cannot divide, but there are **progenitor cells: satellite cells** _(probably_
_residual cells coming from embryonic development)._

   - They are wrapped within the **same basement membrane** _(within the external_
_lamina)_ **as skeletal muscle cells**
   - Connected to them through **M-cadherin molecules**

   Progenitor cells = already committed to the **myogenic lineage.**
   - Function: to **repair muscle** cells in the case of muscle degeneration: Satellite cells
become activated under the influence of **myogenic regulatory factors** and become
**myoblasts**, finally **differentiating into mature muscle cells.**
_Difficult to differentiate them from fibroblasts, but we can see they are within the same_

_basement membrane as the skeletal muscle cells_

*[PDF p. 187]*

#### Muscle histology II. Cardiac & smooth

**CARDIAC MUSCLE**

   - Cardiac muscle is **striated:** it has the same contractile machinery as skeletal muscle,
although with some differences in innervation and contraction
   - It’s **self excitatory**
   - It’s **electrically coupled**
   - The rate of contraction is **regulated by the SNA**
   - We **don't have motor end plates.** The innervation is **neuroendocrine**

**Cardiac muscle cells features**

   - nuclei are **centrally** located

   1 or 2 nuclei per cell
   - Accumulation of organelles **surrounding the nucleus** =
**paler region**
   - The shape differs: they are **not cylindrical,** there is
**branching of the fibers**
   - The **fiber** is formed by **many different cells,** connected by

structures seen as darkened lines: **intercalated disks**

   Intercalated disks are found connecting the cells
   - **Striations** are weaker, more difficult to see

**Other features:**

   - More **abundant mitochondria** (up to 40% of cell
volume)
   - Some **Golgi**, mostly found in the pale region
surrounding the nuclei

   Glycogen

   **Sarcoplasmic reticulum** surround the myofibrils, but it

doesn’t form terminal cisternae around the T tubules

   - **T tubules,** which are wider than in skeletal muscle and

less abundant

   As there are no terminal cisternae, we don’t talk about

triads, but diads

   In cardiac muscle, sarcoplasmic reticulum & T-tubules
appear as **diads** at **Z lines**

_To distinguish cardiac and skeletal muscle in cross_

_sectional view:_

_Look at the nuclei: peripheral or centered_

_T tubule/SR diads, on the area_

_corresponding to Z lines._

https://youtu.be/v5KBJuT9QRc
