_Cardiac muscle 2 - cells. Wendy Riggs_

*[PDF p. 188]*

**Intercalated discs**

Interdigitated blunt cytoplasmic expansions which are responsible for **mechanically and**
**electrically coupling heart cells.** They are **aligned with the Z discs.**
They run both transverse and laterally:

   - Transverse portion: contains **fascia adherens** _(aka zonula adherens)_ **and**
**desmosomes.** Forms **mechanical coupling**
   - Lateral portion: contains **gap junctions** which transmit the electrical, contractile
electrical signal through **ion transport.** Forms **electrical coupling.**

**Cardiac muscle contraction**

The heartbeat is regulated by modified cardiac muscle cells called **cardiac conducting**

**cells:**

**-** **Nodes**

   **Purkinje fibers**

Contraction: overall, the process is similar but with some differences:

   As in skeletal muscle, contraction is **Ca2+ dependent**

   Contraction initiates through **contact between Purkinje fibers and**
**cardiac myocytes.**

   Signal from Purkinje fibers is transmitted from cardiac muscle cell to
cardiac muscle cell through **GAP junctions (electrical coupling →**
**ion transport).**
   - Upon signal and depolarization, **Calcium** enters from **extracellular**
**region**, through the **T tubule**
   - **Ca2+** from the T tubule is transported to the **sarcoplasm**
   - Once in the cell triggers the **release of more intracellular calcium**

from the **SER.**

*[PDF p. 189]*

**SMOOTH MUSCLE**

**Smooth muscle characteristics**

   - It’s a **fusiform**, **non-striated muscle.** No striations in the cells.
   - **Each fiber** will be formed by just **a single cell.**
   - We will find **layers of smooth muscle** in many organs _(vessels, GI, urogenital_
_organs,...)_, and **isolated fibers** in other organs (dermis, spleen, eye,..)

**Contraction**

   - Contraction is **non voluntary**
   - Contraction in smooth muscle is **modulated by**

**different mechanisms:**

    - **Innervation** (varicosities)

    - **chemical stimuli** (hormones)

   - Its **contraction is slower** than that of skeletal muscle

**Organization**

**Layers in different orientations** . Muscle layers run at right
angles to each other (90º) **:**

   Longitudinal layer of smooth muscle

   Circular layer of smooth muscle

_Smooth muscle, longitudinal section:_
In the longitudinal view, pay attention to the nuclei, as **not to confuse it with connective**

**tissue.**

   - Muscle nuclei are **elongated but more rounded,** and **more euchromatic.**

   - Fibroblasts are **heterochromatic** and much flatter

_Smooth muscle, transverse section:_

In transverse view: individual cells with nuclei in the center

_In electron microscopy:_ **euchromatic, elongated nuclei**

*[PDF p. 190]*

**Ultrastructure of smooth muscle**

**-** **Fusiform**

   **Single, centrally located nucleus.** As the cell gets contracted,
nuclei take an typical cork-screw shape
   - Muscle cells are **arranged in compact groups**
   - These bundles are **kept together by connective tissue.**

   The cells themselves are surrounded by basal membrane (type
IV collagen) + reticular fibers (type III collagen), which form the
**endomysium** .

   They **don’t have T tubules or sarcoplasmic reticulum;** instead possess many
**pinocytic caveolae** and **SER (** in the periphery)

   Secretory phenotype: have a relatively active nucleus, RER, Golgi, mitochondria
Produce: Collagen (type I, III and IV) laminin, elastin, proteoglycans

   They don’t form fibers, but connect through **GAP junctions**

The **most abundant component** in the cytoplasm will still be **contractile fibers,** although

**’**
they **don** **t form sarcomeres →** **lack of striations** _(distinctive feature)_

Thin filaments: very abundant

**-** **Actin**

   **Tropomyosin** (smooth muscle form)

   **Caldesmon and calponin** _(actin binding proteins)_ . Instead of troponin, there are
other binding proteins.

Thick filaments:

   **Smooth muscle myosin** _(special type of myosin. In unmodified state, it has low_
_affinity for actin = no need for troponin covering the actin binding sites)_
   - **Side polar orientation,** with **no central bare zone** (vs. bipolar in skeletal muscle).
There is no bipolar organization of the heads, but a side-polar orientation (no bare
region = no H zone or central line)

We need some sort of **structure to stabilize and transmit the**

**contraction** to the rest of the cell. For this purpose, we find **dense bodies**
_(similar to Z lines in skeletal and cardiac):_
These dense bodies, like Z lines, are membrane associated. Made up of:

   - **Intermediate filaments** plaques that help to stabilize and transmit

the contraction

**○** **Desmin**

**○** **Vimentin**

**-**
**alpha-actinin**

   Other adhesion proteins _(vinculin, talin)_

**They** bind actin, connecting **filaments** between them and with the **ECM** to

transmit the force.

*[PDF p. 191]*

**Smooth muscle contraction**

   - Much **slower** than in skeletal muscle

   - Also **calcium dependent**
   - **No motor end plate:** the innervation occurs at a **distance** . The neurotransmitters
diffuse from the axon of the neuron to receptors. Therefore, we don’t talk about
synapse regions but **varicosities**

**-**
**2 types of innervation:**

    - Visceral (unitary): very **few nerve endings,** but the cells
are **connected** through gap junctions. Therefore, they are
electrically coupled, which ensures a **rhythmic or tonic**

**contraction**

    - Multiunit contraction: very **few gap junctions,** practically
**every cell** is **individually innervated.** This allows them to
contract rapidly and individually for more precise control.

**MUSCLE REGENERATION AND GROWTH**

Skeletal muscle

   - Skeletal muscle can grow, **increase in size** → **hypertrophy** (depositing collagen)
   - Can **increase in number. Satellite cells** are the ones thought to provide a source of
regeneration, as skeletal muscle cells cannot divide themselves.

Smooth muscle

   - Smooth muscle can increase in size and **hypertrophy**

   - Can **increase in number:**

    - Can **divide and proliferate** _(ex: uterine myometrium)_

    - There are also **mesenchymal cells** which can differentiate and regenerate
smooth muscle. **Vascular pericytes**

Cardiac muscle

   - Cardiac muscle can **hypertrophy** and **increase in size** .

   There is some potential capacity of regeneration, but there are **limited possibilities**
**of increase in number.** Can occur through:

        circulating progenitor cells _(post-infarction tissue remodeling by fibroblasts)._
        - Resident stem cells _(new evidences)_

MUSCLE HISTOLOGY. SUMMARY

*[PDF p. 192]*

#### Integration of basic tissues

In the **cyst** _(abnormal cavity)_, we find areas formed by
epithelial tissue, and others formed by specialized
connective tissue: adipose tissue. We can also
observe hair, something you wouldn’t expect to find in
an ovary, within areas formed by a creamy substance
(probably keratin) and a more solid substance
(adipose tissue) on the left.

**-**
**Stratified squamous keratinized epithelial**

**tissue**

**-** **Hair follicle Exocrine glands** next to hair follicle = **sebaceous glands, acinar**

structure

*[PDF p. 193]*

**White adipose tissue,** formed by adipocytes (big cells with a large lipid droplet
which displaces the nuclei to the side)

**Hyaline cartilage,** surrounded by a layer of **perichondrium**, containing
**chondroblasts and chondrocytes** (within lacunae, totally surrounded by type II
collagen). We can also observe matrix: **territorial** (more fibers) **and interterritorial**
**matrix** (more proteoglycans). **Isogenous groups** within the same areas of territorial
matrix. Made up of **type I collagen. Basal membrane = type IV collagen; reticular**
**fibers = type III collagen.**

- We can observe **bone tissue:** totally mature bone tissue (calcified). We can see
**cortical bone,** in which **concentric structures** are observed, surrounding a
**Harvessian canal.** Containing **osteocytes**, surrounded by a concentric matrix of
**type I collagen.**

*[PDF p. 194]*

- Very **fibrillar matrix** area, supporting a very important and delicate tissue. In
between the lines, small round cells. From time to time, bigger round cells can be
observed. These **round cells are glial cells** _(giving support)_ **.** We are looking at
**nervous tissue** _(very fibrillar)_ **.** The bigger cells that can be found from time to time

are **neurons.**

Nervous tissue **calcifies with age.** This is what we can see within this nervous

tissue. **Concentric calcifications.**

   - We can find **muscle** (redder) surrounded by **dense irregular CT.** Muscles can be
striated (skeletal or cardiac) or smooth. In this muscle we **cannot see striations,** and
**nuclei are located in the center** of the muscle fibers. Therefore, this is **smooth**

**muscle.**

The **surrounding CT is lighter pink** as it contains less proteins, and has more
elongated cells **(fibroblasts).**

Name the lesion: this tumor is known as **teratoma** .

*[PDF p. 195]*

#### Electrical properties of cell membranes

_Basic concepts_

**INTRODUCTION**

**Key ideas about electric properties**

   - **Electric forces** are **conservative forces.** The work that is done by an electric force
only depends on the initial and final point, but is **independent from the trajectory.** If
we do a circular trajectory, W=0.

   - An **electric field** is a **real quantity.** It’s a **vector**, not a scalar quantity: depending on
the direction of the field, we will obtain different results.

   - The electric current in the **human body** is caused due to the **displacement of ions.**
This displacement is the source of electric current. In electric circuits, it’s caused by
the displacement of electrons.

   - As this is a **conservative system, mechanical energy remains constant.** It
consists in the **balance of kinetic and potential energy.** If one increases, the other
decreases, as to maintain a constant Em.

   Electric field lines and isopotential lines are **perpendicular.**

   Electricity comes in 2 flavours: **positive and negative charges,** whose charge
corresponds to **1.6 · 10^** **-19** **C,** either with + or - sign.

   - There are **repulsive forces** (interaction between particles with same electric charge)
**or attractive forces** (particles with different electric charge). Different to gravity,
where we only find attractive forces.

**Charged particle interactions**

Situation: we place a + charge and place a “probe” charge. If the probe is also +, the force
exerted will be a repulsive force.
This can be explained either by the appearance of an interaction between the particles, or
because this charge interacts with another magnitude known as electric field, in such a way
that the force will be equal to the charge of the affected particle _(probe)_ times the electric
field created by our positive particle.

This last version is correct: **the interaction appears**
**between the charge and the electric field.**

*[PDF p. 196]*

- When we have a **positive charge,** we say we have a **source of electric field lines,**
found pointing **outwards.**
   - When we have a **negative charge,** we have a **sink of electric field lines,** found
pointing **inwards.**

_(convention: we establish this supposing that we will be using a + charge. Therefore, when_
_the field is created by a + charge = repels, outwards lines. Source; when it’s created by a -_
_charged = attracts, pointing inwards. Sink)_

In the human body it’s rare to find isolated charges. We usually find **many charges.** To
analyze their behaviour, we must know, _what is the global behaviour of the electric field?_
To analyze it and answer this question, we can apply the **superposition principle** . We will

be able to **recover a total value.**

_Example:_ a sodium ion perceives the existence of an electric field formed by another ion.
There will be an **electric force** created on the system _(formula)_ due to the field _:_ if we
suppose there is **no friction,** the result will be that the ion will begin to move in the direction
of the field lines. There will be an **acceleration,** and the **kinetic energy will increase.**
As previously mentioned, because this is a conservative system, the **kinetic energy has to**
**be in balance with the potential energy. If one increases, the other will decrease.** Here
we can introduce a new magnitude, known as **electric potential voltage (V):**

**POTENTIAL ENERGY**

For a + charge:

   **Potential energy decreases** from point 1 to point 2 when we move along an electric
field line in the **same direction** as the field _(away from a + charge creating the field,_
_or towards the - charge creating it, as Ek increases)_ .

   If we are moving in the **opposite direction,** the potential energy of the system will
**increase** _(towards a + or away from a -, as Ek decreases)_
For a - charge:

   - **Potential energy (Ep) increases** when we move in the **same direction**
   - **Ep decreases** when we move in the **opposite direction**

**ELECTRIC POTENTIAL**

When we move along a field line, the electric potential changes, independent of the charge:

   - If we move in the **opposite direction, potential (V) will increase**
   - If we move in the **same direction** as the field lines, **V will decrease**

_FOR ANY CHARGED PARTICLE, + or -_

*[PDF p. 197]*

**ELECTRIC BEHAVIOUR OF A CELL**

**Impermeable membrane**

We will focus on the membrane: when considering a cell, we have a small volume of fluid

enclosed inside a membrane.

In principle, this membrane is **impermeable** _(closed barrier),_ permeability = 0. Inside this
cavity we find **water** but also other components, **ions or small charged proteins.**

_What is the interaction between these particles and_
_other ions found on the outer part of the cell?_

We introduce a few sodium ions. _What will happen?_ Due to the presence of all the other
particles, on each of them there will be a **repulsive force.** The expected result is that these
ions will try to **separate**, and will finally be placed on the inner side of the membrane. This is
what would be **expected if we only have sodium ions.**
However, in a real system we find **many other charged particles, + and -,** placed randomly
inside this volume. There is a huge amount of positive and negative ions present.
Therefore, one may ask, _what is the total force experienced by one single particular ion?_
This **total force will be 0** if the total number of positive and negative charges is **balanced** .

In other words, **we can reach an**
**equilibrium of that system if the total**
**charge (Q) is 0.**
**In order to have an equilibrium**
**situation, we need to fulfill this**
**condition (Qt = 0)**

This is the situation found in a resting

cell: **balance between + and - .** There

is **charge neutrality.**

**Membrane permeability for single ion**

Now let's consider the real situation, in which the
permeability of the membrane isn’t 0, although selective.
This **selective permeability** of the membrane means that
**only one of the single ions (+ or -) will be able to cross**
**the barrier,** in this situation.

We are in a situation in which **Qt inside = 0 and Qt**

**outside = 0** _(normal situation)_ **.** However, the concentration
of a positive ion A is greater inside than outside; the same
happens with the concentrations of negative ion B.

*[PDF p. 198]*

_Will this situation persist?_

In the human body the temperature is NOT = 0; it’s 37ºC. This is important as **temperature**
**means vibration.** These **ions aren’t stationary,** but slightly vibrating, so there will be a
**small random displacement of all these ions.**
This random displacement is the mechanism that will **create the resting potential** in the
cells of the human body.

If the temp were = 0, there would be no change in the equilibrium of the system. However,
this is not the case. There is a **small amount of kinetic energy,** so each one of these
molecules will follow a **random trajectory** and will eventually arrive at the membrane:

   - If the membrane is impermeable for the specific ion, they will collide and come back if
_(membrane doesn’t allow their trespass)._
   - But if there is permeability _(in this situation, there is, only for ion A)_ : if one of the
incoming moving ions hits one of the pores, it **can cross the membrane.** It’s an
event that may occur for the positive charge **(ion A),** but not for the negative charge
as the membrane is not permeable for it. If this ion B hits one of the holes, the result
will be to change its direction.

Due to the selective permeability, only one of the ions will be able to cross the barrier. This
**ion transports charge.** Therefore, the ion that has crossed causes the Qt outside to be +
and the Qt inside to become negative.

In order to see if this is a stable situation, we have to check if this diffusion
from left to right can continue in time.
_What is the probability to cross from left to right? Can we go back to the_

_initial situation?_

The **probability will depend on the number of ions we**
**have on each side.** The probability of crossing will be
higher when the concentration of ions on the other side is
greater. Therefore, we will create a **flux of ions, all going**
**from left to right.**

The result: the **total charge inside is smaller than 0,**
**and the total charge outside is larger than 0.**

*[PDF p. 199]*

As we said, the system tends to transition to its **stable state, when Qt = 0.** Therefore, the
**inner volume will divide into 2 parts,** as to maximize the distance between the excess of
particles _(due to repulsive forces, they press against the membrane):_

   - The **negative charges** from the inside will be placed on the **inner face of the**

**membrane**

   - The **positive charges** which have exited the cell will place themselves on the **outer**

**face of the membrane**

There will be an **excess of + on the outer**

**face of the membrane** and an **excess of -**

**on the inside** _(talking about A and B)_ . Only if
there is an excess of particles on the inner

and outer side of the membrane will there be

Qt different than 0. When we **move away**
**from the membrane** and separate, having a
more global view, **Qt REMAINS being 0** _(we_
_are talking in relation to proximity of the_
_membrane)_

**Therefore, Qt is greater and smaller than 0 only in the regions that are proximate to**

**the membrane.**

Coming back to our question: _is this going to continue forever, or will there be a mechanism_
_to stop this diffusion process?_

Because of **diffusion**, **positive charges** will continue to try
and **cross the membrane** from **left to right.**
However, now there is the **presence of positive charges** all
around the outer face, exerting a force pointing in the
opposite direction. **Diffusion will have to fight against the**
**repulsive forces created by the + charges located on the**

**outer membrane.**

In other words, due to the generation of an electric field, a
**voltage** is created between the inner and outer part.
**Voltage = difference of Potential energy (E** **p** **) between 2**
**points**

_When is equilibrium reached, putting an end to the pass of molecules?_
**Equilibrium** appears when the **energy due to the electric force** _(created by repulsive_
_forces on the outside of the membrane)_ is **equivalent** to the **energy available because of**

**the vibration.**

*[PDF p. 200]*

_(Ep_ _elect_ _=Energy that you can obtain from the_
_electric field. Potential energy obtained by the_
_electric forces)_
The **voltage potential** on a **membrane in an**
**equilibrium** situation will be:

This concept, in which **Ep** **elect** **= E** **vibr** is known as

**MEMBRANE RESTING POTENTIAL.**

_What happens if the concentration is similar on_
_both sides? the log=0. The nernst potential is 0._

_Nernst constant / Kb = 26.7 mV in the case of Na+,_

_Cl- and K+_

_How would we define the membrane resting potential / Nernst potential for each individual_
_ion?_ It’s the potential at which ion X stops flowing from in → out, as we have reached
equilibrium between the force driving it and the force stopping it. For each particular ion, this
equilibrium is reached at specific concentrations, which will determine the potential at which
the process stops and the situation is sustained.

SUMMARY:

The **concentration of each ions inside and outside are different** _(starting situation),_ but
there is **charge neutrality.** Our membrane becomes **permeable to ion A only.** Some of the
ions will be able to cross the barrier instead of bouncing off. There is a **displacement of**
**ions** because of **diffusion** . As soon as a small amount of charges are placed on the outer
membrane, there is an **electric force** pointing in the other direction.
The process stops when **diffusion is balanced by the electric field** created by the outer
charges. There is a **voltage for which there is equilibrium** between diffusion and electric
potential (membrane resting potential).

KEY POINTS

   - **Inner and outer concentrations remain CONSTANT,** even if there is a small
amount of particles crossing. It’s a very tiny amount compared to the total amount of
ions. Therefore, no modification in concentration.

   **Total charge inside and outside the cell is ALWAYS 0** in a global view, when we

are **far from the membrane.**

*[PDF p. 201]*

**MEMBRANE POTENTIAL IN A REAL CELL**

In our cells, there are 3 ions for which the membrane is **permeable: Na+, K+ and Cl-.**

In resting potential, they have their **respective ion concentrations.** From these values we
can obtain the logarithm by using the formula, and therefore, obtain the **Nernst potential for**

**each ion:**

**Nernst potentials:**

_Expected results when we_
_consider only one of these ions:_

                           - +60 mV

                           - -90 mV

                           - -70 mV

The values are the results obtained **when we consider**

**the membrane is permeable only for each of the**
**ions.** +60 mV is the value we obtain if only Na+ can
cross the membrane, -90mV if only K+ could cros,...etc

However, we **cannot separate them.** They are all found
together, and **Nernst potential is only valid for when**
**considering 1 ion.**

To recover the real value of membrane’s potential, we cannot use Nernst potential only, we
have to **combine all the different factors;** if we combine everything, **introducing in the**
**formula the concentrations of each ion** (in and out) and their respective **permeabilities**,
we get an expression for membrane potential:

**P** **Cl-** is so small, it **can be excluded from the formula.** And **PNa = 0 '002 Pk.** Introducing
this in the formula, we get that the membrane potential on a cell is **-70 mV.** The limit values
are **-90 mV** _(Nernst potential for K+)_ and **+60 mV** _(Nernst potential for Na+)_
The correct, real value for **membrane potential,** when considering all the factors, is that the
voltage _(for example in a neuron)_ is **-70 mV.**

*[PDF p. 202]*

_What controls membrane potential?_

It is controlled by **permeability.**

**We can recover different values by modifying the permeability.**

   - If the permeability for potassium (P k+ ) is really high (closed Na+ gates), we can

recover values of **-90 mV.**

   - If we close K+ gates, we increase permeability for sodium and recover **+60 mV.**

Depending on if we **depolarize or hyperpolarize** the membrane, the potential will increase

or decrease.

If we cross a specific **threshold** for Na+ permeability, we can reach an **activation potential**
**(+60 mV).**

_* Water is not crossing the barrier. And we are talking about very small particles. There is no_
_great change in concentration, so there will be no flow of water across the membrane. It’s_
_not an osmotic process_

*[PDF p. 203]*

#### Electrical properties of membranes

_Dynamics._ _https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iBDXOt_uHTQ_ _(Action Potential in Neurons,_

_Animation, Alila Medical Media)_

**Previous class. Summary**
_Nernst potential only appears because there is competition between diffusion (permeability_
_different than 0) and an electric potential. Transfer of ions will stop when there is equilibrium_
_between the diffusion and electric processes._

_Due to the different concentrations, there is a possibility to modify the membrane potential._
_All this behaviour is controlled by the action of the sodium-potassium pump, which_
_establishes a gradient (pumps Na+ out, K+ in) after each action potential so a new one can_
_be triggered:_
_Sodium = higher concentrations on the outside → will enter by diffusion when Na+ voltage_
_gated channels are opened, at the beginning of a new action potential (depolarization)_
_Potassium = higher concentrations inside the cell, will reinstate resting potential by exiting_
_the cell after the Na+ influx (repolarization)_

_Equation that controls electric potential across the membrane:_

_Permeability for Cl- ion is roughly 0, so we can differentiate 2 situations:_

_-_ _when the permeability of the membrane for Na+ is almost 0. Therefore, the potential_
_of the membrane is close to the Nernst potential for Potassium. Found outside and_
_inside. Corresponds to the resting potential._

_-_
_when the permeability for potassium is nearly 0. The terms on the equation are_
_switched, and now the membrane potential resembles the Nernst potential for_
_sodium. We will find sodium inside as well as outside. Corresponds to the initiation of_
_the action potential._

_To switch membrane potential, we have to change_
_permeability (open canals). When we do this,_

_diffusion will be the mechanism that will drive the_

_change in potential and try to reinstate the_
_equilibrium._

*[PDF p. 204]*

_**How does the cell modify the polarity and electric membrane potential when we**_
_**switch the permeability of the membrane?**_

Our starting point is **55mV (action potential reached).**
Permeability for sodium is different from 0, and P k is
similar to 0, very small compared to that of Na.

**Sodium** is the one which **will drive the behaviour of**

**the system:** somehow _(we will see now),_ there is a
modification of the permeability, making the P Na = 0
(approx 0) and the permeability for potassium different
from 0. There is a transition, back to the resting
potential. _How much time is needed for this transition?_
_Will this be a fast process?_

Let's start from the beginning.
In the 1st configuration, the membrane potential is +55mV.
In the final situation, polarity is reversed and we have a
membrane potential of -90 mV.

   - **1st situation: 55mV** = there are negative charges
on the outer face of the membrane, + on the inner

inside. **+Q1 inside, -Q1 outside**

   - **2nd situation: -90mV** = reverse situation.

Negative charges on the inner side, Positive on the
outer. **-Q2 inside, +Q2 outside**

**V** _**(membrane potential)**_ **= V** **inner** **- V** **out**

_When we are far from the membrane, Qt still = 0._

**To reverse the potential, we need a charge transport.** A total charge needs to be
transported from the inner side to the outer side. For this purpose, we need to **transfer a**
**specific amount of ions** _**(around 10^**_ _**11**_ _**).**_

_**How many ions do we need to transport from one side to the other?**_

This can be answered by answering 2 questions:

_-_
_What is the total charge that needs to be transported?_

   _How fast can these charges cross the barrier? What is the intensity?_
**(charge/second = electric current or Intensity).** The unit for this electric current (I)
is **Ampere (Amp).**

*[PDF p. 205]*

**1.** **What is the total charge that needs to be transported?**

In this system we have a membrane with + and - charges, and a 55mV potential (AP).

When reaching a specific membrane potential, we know **there is a relation**
**between the charges being transported and the potential being applied**
**on the system.** The more charges which are transported, the higher
differential potential (voltage) between both sides of the membrane.

_Can we determine the_ _**value of the constant**_ _which relates charge and voltage relationship?_

We can do an **analogy with a battery:** a battery consists of
**2 metallic plates,** separated a certain **distance** . In between
these 2 plates, electrical field lines cross from the + (source)
to the - (sink). This structure is known as a **capacitor**, used
in many electrical devices as it **can use its electrical field to**
**store charge**, and therefore, **store energy.**
_Take our cell phones for instance: the charges cannot cross_
_this separation. But when we charge a battery, what we do is_
_inject + on one side and - on the other, which allows us to_
_store charge and therefore, energy. The plates are_
_connected and the energy stored on both sides is able to be_

_used._

This analogy is **similar** to what happens in the **cellular membrane.**
The **cell membrane** would be equivalent to the **capacitor** . Thanks to the analogy, we

can derive the formula:

Where the **constant C** corresponds to a value known as **capacitance** _(measured in Farads,_
_F):_ it measures the **ability to store charge** in a specific device.

The point which is relevant: this **capacitance depends on the area of the plates, the**
**separation and the material that is in between the plates** _(known as_ _**permittivity**_ _)._

   - In the cell membrane, the **distance** corresponds to the
**thickness** of the membrane **(roughly 4-** **10** **nm).**
   - The **area** corresponds to the area of the specific cell

   Epsilon (permittivity) corresponds to the material found in
between the membrane. The constant value, **permittivity**,
therefore **depends on the material.**

In a **typical neuron**, this **capacitance** value corresponds to:

**10 ^** **-2** **F/m2**

_https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u-jigaMJT10: Capacitors &_
_Capacitance, Khan academy_

*[PDF p. 206]*

_Situation: we consider a spherical neuron. The diameter is 1 micrometer._
_The area will correspond to the area of a sphere, therefore A = 4pi · 0,25 · 10^-_ _12_ _m_ _2_ _,_
_roughly 3 · 10_ _-12_ _m_ _2_ _._

_Therefore, the capacitance value is 3 · 10^_ _-14_ _F,_
_quite a small value. Applying the formula, the_
_total charge of our system will be:_

_Q1 = 3 ·10 ^_ _-14_ _times the membrane potential,_
_55mV, which gives 16,500 · 10^-_ _19_ _C._

_**How many ions do we need to transfer?**_

Only 10^ 4 ios will need to be transported from one side to another.
Q2 = 1.5 · 10 ^4 . Therefore, **Qt = 25000 ions.**

- The **capacitance** of a cell cannot be modified. In a normal cell, it’s a **constant**, a property.
Can only be modified by modifying the cell itself (area, distance between the sides of the
neuron,..). The one that affects the dynamics will be the intensity.

**2.** **How fast can these charges cross the barrier (Intensity)?**

The membrane has channels, which make it permeable for ion A+. Through these pores,
water and ions can cross. Because of the potential there is an E field acting on the system,
meaning there is a force pushing the ion to the outer side of the cell (+ ion being moved to
the - side).

   - If the situation were done in **vacuum (no friction force),** the dynamic of the ion
would be an **accelerated displacement** of it as it goes through the membrane.
   - But this is not the situation. In this system we have **water**, so there are **collisions**
with the molecules. Some of the energy being used to transport the ions is being lost
through collisions with those molecules. There is an **equilibrium** between the **force**
**acceleration and the force produced by collisions (friction),** and therefore, the
**velocity is constant.**

*[PDF p. 207]*

**If velocity is constant Intensity is also**
**constant** as “I” is **proportional** to velocity
of the charged particle **( I =**
**charges/second (v) = E current).**

   �V (voltage across the membrane, produced by the presence of an electric field) will
cause a force that drives charged particles across the membrane at a specific
intensity

   A magnitude will exert friction force and establish the relationship there will be
between the voltage and the intensity it will be able to induce _(how many charges will_
_be able to cross per second)_

**Ohm’s law** establishes this **linear proportionality,** as for a given voltage there is a specific
intensity. The proportionality **between potential** **(V)** and **Intensity** **(I)** is given by **resistance (R)**

In other words, whenever the ion is trying to cross, the material will exert a **resistance** which
tries to stop these ions. We will have to **overcome this resistance** in order **to transfer the**
**ions** . This resistance depends on:

   - **Resistivity** (depends on the specific medium, in this case **water.** Will determine the
exerted **Fr** )

   - **Area** of the channel

**-** **Distance**

By using this expression, we can derive that the **I that can cross the barrier at a specific**
**voltage depends on the resistance.**
But this is the situation for a single channel.

The **total intensity** will be the **combination of all the specific intensities.** The value we
obtain is the membrane potential between the **total resistance**, which is:

**distance · resistivity / Area · number of channels**

**The point is that the total intensity is proportional to the number of channels.** It
depends on the number of channels: **↓ n of channels = ↓ T intensity** across the membrane

*[PDF p. 208]*

When we are analyzing the intensity, we cannot modify the type of channel or thickness, but
we can control the **value of the resistance** by changing the **permeability** _**(number of**_
_**channels)**_

   - If **permeability is reduced** (reduced number of channels), the **resistance increases**
   - If we **increase the permeability** (increased number of channels), the **resistance**

**decreases**

We have all the gradients that we need to analyze the question initially posed.

**What’s the behaviour in between both situations? How does the membrane switch the**

**potential by changing the permeability of the membrane?**

Before P1, there is no intensity crossing the barrier as we are in equilibrium. But because of
the modification of permeability, there is an increase in the intensity _(charges crossing/sec)_
and we find an intensity circulating (V/R, R being controlled by permeability). The green
voltage corresponds to the represented difference.

The intensity is **not constant** during the transition between P1 and P2. As we approach the
final barrier, “I” will get smaller and smaller due to the change in permeabilities.
This is done **following an exponential behaviour** which is marked by a **specific time,** the
**time constant,** that is determined by **resistance and capacitance** _(C = constant; R = can_
_be modified by playing with permeability)._

𝝉 _**= R · C**_

Situations:

   - When **R is large** for the ion which is causing the change in potential, the transition
will be **slow** . It’ll take a long time to do the transition = **↑ time constant**
   - When **R is small**, the transition will be **fast** = **↓ time constant**

A pathology that reduces the number of open channels or the concentration of ions
(permeability) will slow down the behaviour.

*[PDF p. 209]*

_Diagram:_ it’s the **electronic equivalent of a neuron membrane,** the most simple electronic
analogy to represent the electronic activity of a cell. It represents a set of discrete electronic

devices which are connected.

   - On the right = the **membrane** ( **capacitor** which **controls the charge stored** at the
system)

   To the left, the **sodium potassium-pump**, **always working and always present,**
pushing Na+ outside and potassium inside to reinstate

   Three on the left = represent the Nernst potentials for each ion (sodium battery =
reverse orientation). The resistor they contain symbolizes the permeabilities

KEY POINTS

Modifying **Temperature** modifies the absolute value of Nernst potential, not the sign (as it’s
found outside the ln)

**Permeability**

**-**
**Reduce K+ permeability = membrane potential will increase > -70mV**

**-**
**Increase Na+ permeability = membrane potential will increase > -70mV**

*[PDF p. 210]*

#### Time and length constants

**TIME CONSTANT**

“Amount of time it takes for the change in potential to reach 63% of its final value”
The time constant is an index of how rapidly a membrane would respond to a stimulus in
time. It depends on **R and C:**

   - **Rm** is **membrane resistance**, it’s inversely proportional to permeability. Decreasing
permeability (n of open channels) increases resistance = increases the time constant.
It will take longer to reach the final potential.
   - **C** is the **capacitance**, represents the ability to store charge across a membrane. The
higher the capacitance, higher ability to store = it will take more time to “charge up
the membrane” up to its maximum storing capacity = increases the time constant

In both cases, it will take longer to establish the differential potential we are trying to reach.

**LENGTH CONSTANT**

“Index of how well a subthreshold potential will spread along an axon, as a function of

distance”

   - **Rm** is once again the **membrane resistance**, inverse to permeability. Increasing Rm
means there will be less chance for the charges flowing through the membrane to
escape. Less charges leak out, therefore more moving along the membrane = ↑
length constant. Increasing Rm is like putting insulation on an axon
   - **Ri** is the **internal resistance,** represents the ability of charges to move along the
axon. It’s an intrinsic property of the axoplasm.

**PROPAGATION VELOCITY**

**High propagation velocity**

_How can this propagation velocity be increased?_

   By **increasing the axon’s diameter**, we decrease Ri = bigger length constant

   By covering it in **myelin** . What myelin will do is:

    - Increase membrane resistance (as it covers up channels, permeability is
decreased and resistance increases = longer length constant)

    - Increases separation between the faces of the membrane = reduced
capacitance

    - Decreases amount of charge stored = reduced capacitance

*[PDF p. 211]*

#### Membrane potential workshop

Situation at RMP: thanks to the action of a Na+/K+ pump, extracellular sodium concentration
is higher than the intracellular, and intracellular K+ > extracellular K+.
At this point, K+ permeability is quite high while the permeability for sodium is close to 0
_(pNa+ = 0.02pK+, in resting situation)._ RMP is almost exclusively controlled by K+ at this
point, but there is still a small contribution from Na+, making it -70mV and not -90mV.
A change in Na+ concentration or permeability will still have a small effect on RMP.

Depolarization will occur because pNa+ increases and allows Na+ influx into the cell, making
membrane potential positive.

1st scenario

_A patient has severe_ _**hyponatremia**_ _. Suppose that in this situation the extracellular sodium_
_concentration is 120 mmol/L. 1ST SCENARIO: Explain how this value affects the Nernst_
_potential of Sodium and Potassium ions._

Levels of sodium in blood (extracellular) are very low (hyponatremia)
What happens with Nernst potential for sodium and potassium in this patient?

   Sodium Nernst potential will decrease

   Potassium Nernst potential will remain unchanged. Only the Na potential is modified.

Hyponatremia would mean the Na+ gradient is decreased. Therefore, the pass of ions
through Na+ channels will be lower. Less entry during repolarization = less + charges being
deposited on the inner surface of the cell, **RMP will become more negative.**

2nd scenario

_Now, consider the situation of a patient that has_ _**hypokalemia**_ _, that implies a decrease of the_
_extracellular potassium concentration to 3 mmol/L (millimoles per Litre). Explain how this_
_value affects the Nernst potential of Sodium and Potassium ions._

*[PDF p. 212]*

Decrease of extracellular potassium (hypokalemia)

Normal values = 5 mmol/L

Hypokalemia = 3 mmol/L

Effect on Nernst potential?

   K Nernst potential will decrease

   Na Nernst potential will remain unchanged

Extracellular K+ concentration decreases = potassium gradient is increased, more charges
will tend to flow out of the cell and more + charges will be deposited on the outer surface,
making the inner more negative = the **RMP will decrease** under -70mV, becoming nearly
-90 mV. It’s more difficult to trigger an action potential, as the resting potential is decreased.

3rd scenario

_Explain the consequences if the_ _**sodium permeability reduces**_ _to one half the reference_
_value. Does this modification affect the resting potential?_

During an action potential:

   depolarization occurs when sodium permeability increases

   repolarization when K permeability increases

We are analyzing the resting potential, previous to depolarization (left side of the diagram)
What will happen if we are in a situation where the permeability for Na is decreased? Will it

have an effect on RMP?

The **resting potential** appears because there is a **contribution from potassium and**

**sodium.**

Permeability for sodium is 2% of the permeability for potassium. This is why the resting
potential is -70 and not -90 mV; there is a small contribution from Na+ to the potential _(K+_
_exiting the cell, small amount of Na+ entering it and making it less negative, not fully -90)_

If there stops being Na contribution or less contribution (ex: reduced permeability, or in the
case we saw before, reduced concentration gradient due to hyponatremia), the **new resting**
**potential will be below -70mV. It will decrease. PNa+ does affect RMP.**
Difference with the previous situation: there is a decrease in resting potential but not due to
decrease in Nernst potential, but due to a decrease in permeability.

*[PDF p. 213]*

4th scenario

_Explain how a reduction of the sodium permeability affects the_
_depolarization and repolarization characteristic times (time constants)._

In the resting state, there is K+ efflux (open K+ channels) and a small
influx of Na+. However during repolarization, from the highest point of
depolarization to hyperpolarization, sodium has no effect on membrane
potential as the channels are closed = no Na+ permeability.

Depolarization
The effect of Na+ permeability on depolarization: it **will take longer to reach depolarization**
Repolarization
**Repolarization time** will remain **unchanged** as it depends on the permeability of potassium.

SOCRATIVE
