#### BASIC NEURONAL AND MUSCULAR FUNCTION

*[PDF p. 215]*

#### Membrane & action potentials

**Introduction**

**At a basal (resting) state:**

   We are at the resting potential **(-70 mV)**, for which potassium is mainly responsible.

_How is it reached?_

   There are different concentrations in and out of the cell, for each ion. Therefore,
diffusion will tend to move these ions in favour of the concentration gradient

   As they are charged particles, while this movement proceeds, an electric force will
appear due to the excess accumulation of charges on the opposite face of the

membrane

   Diffusion will have to fight this electric force. The flow of ions will stabilize when the
chemical force driven by diffusion is in equilibrium with the electric force exerted in
the opposite direction

   At this point, the resting membrane potential is established

**The resting membrane potential is negative. Why?**

   At resting potential, the few resting-Na channels are open, carrying out this
mentioned process. Na+ voltage-gated channels are closed. The K+ resting channels
are much more abundant than the Na+ ones. Therefore, the **resting membrane**
**potential is very close to that of K+ Nernst potential** _(although not quite the same,_
_as the resting Na channels play a role._ _**PNa+ = 0.02 PK+**_ _)_
**At rest, the inner side of the membrane is negative and the outer, positive.**

        If the membrane were only permeable to Na+, the membrane potential would
be positive _(as sodium is found at higher concentration out of the cell and it_
_would tend to enter, increasing the n of + charges inside the cell)_

        If it were only permeable for K+, V would be even more negative than it is.
The inner side of the cell would become negative as + charges would tend to

exit the cell.

The **Goldman equation** reflects the real situation, by taking into account all of the
ions that contribute to establishing the membrane potential _**(combination of the**_
_**Nernst potentials):**_

*[PDF p. 216]*

**ACTION POTENTIAL**

The action potential is necessary to transmit information through the axons of neurons. It's a
**sequence of opening and closing of voltage-gated channels.**

   It appears due to a sequence of opening and closure of voltage-gated sodium and
potassium channels. This sequence begins when the cell is depolarized to a certain

threshold

   It is an all-or-nothing process

   It allows the propagation of an electrical current without decrement despite the

distance

   It supports electrical transmission through axons (neurons) and muscle fibers

**VG sodium channels**

This action potential is reached thanks to a specific type of subchannels: **voltage-gated**

**sodium channels.**

It has 2 different gates: **Fast gate** _(involved in activation)_ **and a Slow gate** _(involved in_
_deactivation)._ They are proteins that change their conformation when a specific voltage is
reached. Ther activity is opposite.

   - **Rest** : at **normal potential,** the **fast gate** is **closed** and the **slow gate** is **open.**

   - **Activation:** when we reach the **threshold (-60 mV)**, the **fast gate opens** and the
**slow gate closes after a while.** There is a **1ms time difference** between the
opening of the fast and the closing of the slow. During this brief period, the channel
remains open (both gates are open) and **sodium can enter the cell. Membrane**
**potential will increase,** becoming much less negative and reaching **positive**

**values.**

_This explains why the action potential is always the same: the gates are always or_
_always closed the same amount of time._

   - **Deactivation:** After a while, the channel is deactivated as the **slow gate finally**
**closes.** Sodium stops flowing into the cell and the process decreases.

**VG potassium channels**

To go back to a normal voltage, the sodium potassium pump
**actively transfers Na+ ions back out.** But this is a very slow

process.
To reinstate the potential, there are two main events that

contribute:

   - The **closing of the voltage-gated Na+ channel**
occurs (slow and fast gate)

   **Voltage-gated K+ channels open.** _Why now?_

because their activation is slower than that of Na

voltage-gated channels.

*[PDF p. 217]*

The **rapid exit of potassium** and the **slowed sodium influx** will allow the membrane to
recover **negative values,** even below the resting potential (hyperpolarization)

_Action potential = purple line_

_-_ _Positive_

_-_
_Negative_

_-_
_Even more negative_

_-_ _Back to its initial value_

**Always the same pattern,** as the open and closure of the
gates is always the same. It’s an all or nothing process. If
it doesn’t follow this dynamic, the action potential won’t be

reached.

**Neuronal refractory period**

   - **Absolute refractory period** = Period between the moment the threshold is reached
during depolarization, until it’s reached again during repolarization (in other words,
**from the opening of the fast gate to the closing of the slow** ). In this period of
time, it’s **impossible to fire up an action potential.** It corresponds to values **higher**

**than -60 mV.**

   **Relative refractory period** = Corresponds to the period in which Na+ gates are
closed and K+ gates are open ( **hyperpolarization** ). If we want to depolarize again
from this point, we would have to fight against the open potassium channels. It is
**harder than normal to fire up an action potential,** but it is possible.

The **absolute refractory period** is the main factor that **limits the maximal firing rate of a**
**neuron** (the number of action potentials a neuron can fire up in a period of time). There are
also other limiting factors, such as energy (it’s a very energy demanding process).

In order to reach the threshold we need to slightly depolarize the membrane (PPE), needed
to trigger the opening of sodium channels.
But before we begin the action potential, we need to **go from the deactivated Na+ channel**
**to the resting state** of said channel (fast gate closed, slow gate open).

*[PDF p. 218]*

**To reactivate the resting state,** V has to cross **lower values**
than those corresponding to the resting state
**(hyperpolarization)** .

   - From **deactivated to resting**, when repolarizing _(thanks_
_to potassium channels)_, the **channels do not open.**
They **only open when we are depolarizing.**

   The fast gate closes, and after this, the slow gate can
open once again and be in the resting state. From here,
the depolarization process can take place once again.
   - For a short period of time, there is **double channel**

**closure.**

**Neuronal action potential**

Due to the action of other neurons, there are changes in the
membrane potential. If the change is big enough, we will fire
up an **action potential.** For it, sodium and potassium
voltage gated channels are needed. But they are only
located in the **trigger zones.** The small electrical impulses
detected by dendrites, coming from other neurons, are
transmitted to the soma, until they reach the **axon hillock**
**(trigger zone).** This is **where the action potential will fire**
**up** if the threshold is reached.

**Muscle cells**

Action potentials are not only present in neurons; they also appear in muscle cells.

   - **Striated muscle:** action potential is very **similar** to the one observed in neurons.
   - **Smooth muscle:** action potentials may have **different** characteristics, as there are
other ions involved **( ca2+)**
   - **Cardiac muscle:** action potentials have **different**
characteristics. In the heart the action potential is **slower** . We
have a 1st stage similar to that in the neurons, but the
diagram adopts a different shape due to the action of calcium.

**NERVE CONDUCTION**

We need action potentials to transmit information.
The **amplitude of the voltage** in an electric field **decreases exponentially with distance.**
So if we wanted to send a message to our big toe from the spinal cord, there would be no
way to do it using normal electrical transmission.
**This is why we use action potentials.** At each neuron, a **new action potential is**
**generated** every few micrometers. In this way, the signal is not lost during the journey. We
can transmit the same signal over a long distance.

This is what occurs in unmyelinated fibers. This type of conduction **has one problem: it’s**
**an extremely slow process** as generating an action potential **requires time (3-4 ms).** The
final conduction velocity is half a meter per second. Very slow.
This means if we touch something burning, we would burn before executing a response.

*[PDF p. 219]*

To improve that conduction velocity, we use **myelin.**
It allows **saltatory conduction.**

**Myelin and conduction**

**Myelin insulates the axons of neurons.** It’s different

in the CNS and PNS:

   - In the **CNS** = **oligodendrocytes**, which cover

the axons. Each one covers several axons. It’s

a lipid recovery.
   - In the **PNS** = **Schwann cells.** 1 SC per axon.
Axons with no myelin in the PNS are also
surrounded by Schwann cells (provides
protection), but unmyelinated, so there is no

isolation.

**Insulation improves conduction. The length constant becomes higher** if we recover the
axon, and instead of having to place voltage gated channels all over the axon, we do it only
in a few regions. It allows us to be able to **regenerate the action potential every few**
**millimeters and not every few microns.** We get an **action velocity** of **80 m/s.** Much faster.

Those places in which we find the **channels** are the **Nodes of Ranvier,** where there is **no**
**myelin.** Points where the **action potential is regenerated** .
There is alternating action potential generation ( _nodes_ ) and
electrical conduction _(myelinated regions)._

   We lose time every time we regenerate the action
potential, but **in between nodes** _**(myelin regions,**_
_where there is_ _**electrical conduction)**_ the

**conduction is immediate.**

   Action potential will only be regenerated in the nodes.
Therefore, the **lower the number of nodes,** the
**higher the conduction capacity (faster impulse**
**transmission)**

*[PDF p. 220]*

**Factors that play a role in conduction velocity:**

   - **Fiber diameter:** the thicker the axon, lower the internal resistance = ↑ length
constant _(better conduction)_

**-**
**Degree of myelination**

        - **Increases** membrane **resistance** _(Rm affects the length constant, although_
_also affects the time constant. ↑ Rm = ↑ length constant)_
        - **Reduces capacitance** _(C only affects the time constant. ↓ C = ↓ time_
_constant)._ Membrane potentials will be faster as it reduces the capacitor effect

of the membrane.

   - **Temperature** : the colder it is, the slower the conduction _(Goldman equation)_

**Time and length constants**

**-**
**Time constant** = mainly depends on **capacitance** (C. Measures the ability to store

charge. Depends on permittivity)
   - **Length constant** = depends on **internal and membrane resistance** (R. Determined
by permeability, the number of channels)

**Demyelination**

If we lose myelin:

   **Decreased conduction velocity: demyelination** can slow conduction velocity of the
axon potential. The cell will try to compensate by putting **more channels in the**
**demyelinated portion.**

   - **Appearance of conduction blocks:** we will recover a
**much lower length constant** = the propagation along the
unmyelinated segment is much worse. **Amplitude will**
**decrease** and we may not have enough amplitude when

we reach the next node of ranvier to induce the next

action potential. Demyelination slows, but **can even block**
**conduction of the action potential.**

   - **Interferences:** if we have **bare axons,** one can stimulate
another and give place to **aberrant conductions.**

SUMMARY

   All cells present a negative intracellular potential (in other words, a negative
membrane potential) in their resting states (usually **-80mV**, mainly determined by K)

   The sodium-potassium pump extracts 3 Na+ for every 2 K+ it introduces. It’s always
working, essential for generating the different outer and inner Na and K
concentrations, which is the base for the generation of an action potential

   Na/K pump also contributes to some extent to establishing the membrane potential

*[PDF p. 221]*

In the resting state, we have resting channels contributing to the maintenance of
membrane potential. It’s very similar to K+ Nernst potential. This suggests that this
ion is the main responsible in establishing said potential _(the pot at which K reaches_
_equilibrium will correspond to the membrane potential)_

The small difference between Ek and Em can be explained
by the contribution of Na influx (although small, it is
present).

K conductivity and K permeability are higher than that of Na.

The action potential is an all or nothing event which appears due to a sequence of
opening and closing of Na (depolarization) and K (repolarization) channels.

This AP allows the conduction of an electrical impulse across the axon. Myelin
recovering allows this conduction to be saltatory = faster and more efficient.

*[PDF p. 222]*

#### Synapse

**SYNAPSES**

_Why do we have synapses?_

   - Divergence: we can **amplify** information
   - Convergence: we can **integrate** info
   - Modulation **(excitation or inhibition)**

   Spatial and temporal summation

   Plasticity (synapses are constantly **changing** )

**2 main types of synapses**

**Chemical synapses:**

   Present some delay

   - Use neurotransmitters

   - These neurotransmitters can excite or inhibit

   Information flows only in one direction
(unidirectional)

   Not necessarily linked to ion channels

   Larger synaptic cleft

**NEUROMUSCULAR SYNAPSE**

**Electrical synapses:**

   No delay

   - Flow of ions

   - Direct current flow: no information

changes

   - Bidirectional

   Open channels between cells (gap
junctions) with partial selectivity

   Minimal intercellular space

**History** : It was the 1st synapse to be characterized.

It’s the synapse between a **motor neuron axon** from the spinal
cord and a **muscle fiber.** In this case, the **receptor** on the muscle

fiber is an **ionic channel.**

Each individual **muscle fiber** in a muscle is innervated by **one,**
**and only one, motor neuron.**
A **single motor neuron,** however, can innervate **many muscle**

**fibers.** The combination of an individual motor neuron and all of

the muscle fibers that it innervates is called a **motor unit.**

**Functional anatomy of the synapse:**

   - Axon

   Presynaptic bouton

   Synaptic cleft

   End plate

   - Sarcolemma

*[PDF p. 223]*

1. Presynaptic bouton

We have an action potential traveling through the neuron.
In the last part of the axon,this action potential is regenerated one last time to be transmitted.
Therefore, we find **many sodium-potassium voltage-gated channels** for this purpose.

We also find many **calcium voltage gated-channels** in this region. When the potential
reaches the presynaptic bouton, these Ca2+ channels will open and **calcium will enter into**
**the cell** _(Ca2+ out >> in)._
This calcium will trigger the **fusion of the neurotransmitter vesicles with the membrane**
**and exocytosis.**

i. Regeneration of potential (Na+/K+ channels)
ii. Calcium enters (Ca2+ channels)
iii. Exocytosis (fusion)
iv. Calcium exit (Na/Ca2+ countertransport and Ca2+ ATPase)

One it enters, the accumulated **calcium has to be expelled** out of the cell, through an
**Na/Ca countertransport pump** _(single calcium ion is exported for the import of three_
_sodium ions)_ and **Ca2+-** _**ATPase**_ _(energy dependent)._
As this last one is an energy dependent process, we will find **many mitochondria in the**

**axon.**

**Neurotransmitter vesicles (Ach)**

   Around 300000 per bouton

   - 50 nm diameter

   7000-10000 Ach molecules per vesicle

   Located in two areas, proximal or distal depending on their immediate use

2. Synaptic cleft

**Neurotransmitters** diffuse through the synaptic cleft and **reach the postsynaptic muscle**

**fiber.** This cleft is 10-50 nm.

In addition, this cleft is full of an enzyme that destroys Ach **(Ach-** _**esterase**_ **),** because we
want the neurotransmitter to have an **effect on receptors for a limited time period.**
Therefore, neurotransmitters are immediately **destroyed in the cleft,** as we are not
interested in permanent muscle contraction.

3. End plate

Part of the muscle fiber with the **receptors for neurotransmitters** .

   - We see **hyperdense folds** in the membrane in order to **increase the surface** in
contact with the neurotransmitter, where **Ach-receptors are located.**
   - These receptors **(nicotinic)** are each **ligand-gated ionic channels** which **open**

**when in contact with 2 molecules of Ach.**

   The ligand gated channels trigger Na+ entry and **local depolarization**, leading to the
**opening of Na+ voltage-gated channels** _(There are no voltage-gated sodium_
_channels in the end plate, they are in nearby areas)_
   - These voltage gated channels opened upon depolarization allow the **action**
**potential to extend along the muscle fiber.**
There are **no sodium-potassium v-gated channels in the end plate,** but at a short
distance from it. If the depolarization in the membrane is strong enough, it will reach them
and transmit the action potential through the muscle fiber.

*[PDF p. 224]*

This is a **safety mechanism** : sometimes, there are spontaneous releases of
neurotransmitters, which we don’t want to have an action on muscle fibers. Therefore, **only**
**when there is a large, induced release will the muscle contract.**

4. Sarcolemma

The **sarcolemma** is a **specialized membrane** which surrounds the striated muscle fiber
cells. At the neuromuscular junction, the sarcolemma shows a series of **invaginations** which
greatly increase the surface area for the neurotransmitters to act.

Sequence of events

   Action potential reaches the presynaptic terminal

   Voltage-gated Ca2+ channels open and calcium enters the terminal

   Ca2+-induced vesicle fusion → exocytosis. Neurotransmitter (Ach)

release

   Ach enters the cleft and reaches the postsynaptic neuron

   Activation of postsynaptic Ach receptors (nicotinic)

   Local depolarization of the muscle fiber

   If the depolarization is high enough → reaches Na+ voltage gated

channels

   Opening of voltage-gated sodium channels

   Muscle fiber action potential

**End plate potential (EPP)**

   There are no voltage-gated sodium channels in the end plate, so NO action potential
is generated YET
   - EPP propagates **electronically** = **amplitude decreases**
exponentially with distance, until it reaches voltage-gated Na+

channels

   - The **action potential will begin in nearby areas** where those

channels are found

**Muscle action potential**

   - Depolarization must reach **-60mV** for action potential to take place
   - **EPP** (end-plate potential), first to be generated (and if high enough, will trigger the
action potential) has **65 mV of amplitude = wide safety margin**

*[PDF p. 225]*

Nicotinic channels

They are **ligand-gated ionic channels,** but **not voltage gated**
_(therefore not affected by membrane potential changes)_ **.** The **ligand**
**union is very brief,** as Ach- _esterase_ will quickly degrade the ligand;
therefore, they can be **repeatedly activated.**

How Ach is stored in vesicles

   - The vesicles have a **proton pump (** _**ATPase)**_ which lowers the
pH in the vesicle

   There is a proton/Ach countertransport, active thanks to the gradient created by the

pump

   - Allows Ach to enter into the vesicles

Role of calcium

   - The **release of neurotransmitter vesicles** is due to the entrance of calcium into the

presynaptic bouton

**Neurotransmitter vesicle fusion with the membrane**

Mediated by **SNARE** proteins _(soluble NSF attachment protein receptors)_

   - Calcium makes contact with **synaptotagmin**
   - This causes **synaptobrevin**, another protein located in the neuronal membrane, to
make contact with other proteins

   They will contact and push vesicles against the membrane, causing vesicle and

membrane to **fuse** .

Can also mediate the fusion of vesicles with membrane-bound compartments such as
**lysosomes** .

Mechanisms of vesicle content release

2 ways:
   - **Classical fusion:** vesicle is fully integrated into the membrane, and then there is
regeneration of the vesicle thanks to **clathrin** .
   - **Kiss and run: no full fusion.** Neurotransmitters are simply deposited and the
vesicle regresses. These neurotransmitters are released through a **fusion pore.**

Vesicle recycling mechanisms

2 vesicle pools:

**-**
**Reserve pool**

**-**
**Readily releasable pool**

*[PDF p. 226]*

**NEURO-NEURONAL SYNAPSE**

**-**
**Functional anatomy of neuro** **neuronal synapse:**

Between neurons.

   Presynaptic terminal (bouton)

   Synaptic cleft

   Post synaptic neuron

Types of synapses

There can be different types of synapses, depending on where it
takes place _(soma, dendrites or axons)._ This is important as the

effect of them is different.

Depending on where it takes place:

**-** **Axo-somatic**

**-** **Axo-dendritic**

**-** **Axo-axonal**

From a histological point of view:

   **Type I** : usually located in the dendritic tree, in the **spines**

**of dendrites.**

Usually **excitatory** . No voltage gated channels in the

dendritic tree.

   - **Type II:** take place in the **soma and the dendritic**

**branches** themselves.

Usually **inhibitory** . No voltage gated channels in the
soma, except in the axon hillock

The closer we are to the beginning of the axon, the stronger the
transmission of membrane potential.
**Synapse in the dendritic tree** = very narrow branches, much resistance = **low influence**
**Synapse in the soma** = very close to the axon, travels very short distance = **big influence**

1. Presynaptic terminal

   Similar to the neuromuscular synapse

   However, the type of neurotransmitter in the vesicles may change

2. Synaptic cleft

   - Usually **smaller** than in the neuromuscular junction **(20-30 nm)**

   **Different degradation enzyme content** _(NOT ach-esterase)._

Two main mechanisms:

    - **Destroy the neurotransmitter** (enzyme will depend on the specific
neurotransmitter)

    - **Clearing mechanism,** to **retrieve** the neurotransmitter, which is **recycled**

3. Postsynaptic neuron

4 aspects to discuss: Dendrites, soma, axonal cone (trigger zone) and postsynaptic
receptors

*[PDF p. 227]*

**Dendrites**

   Some neurons have a very large dendritic tree = in many cases, present **great**

**arborization**

   - Dendrites have a **high density of receptors** → Most synapses take place
in the dendrites **(80-90% are axo-dendritic)**
   - Dendrites have a **very narrow diameter** = **high internal resistance (Ri)**,
and thus a **low length constant** → **signal loses amplitude** along the
length of the dendrite, until potential is regenerated at vg Na+ channels

   Therefore, we will find **many synapses** taking place in all the dendritic tree.
They will add their effect **(spatial summation)** and reach the axon to
transmit the depolarization. In this way, signal is not lost and can be transmitted

**Soma**

   Neurons are generally big cells
   - Big diameter = very **low resistance** to the propagation of excitatory or inhibitory
postsynaptic potentials (EPSP or IPSP)

   Membrane potential shows values from -65mV to 75mV

   It possesses **few receptors** (10% of synapses occur at the soma) and scarce
voltage-gated ion channels = **no action potential generation**

**Trigger zone**

   Corresponds to the **place where the axon begins**

   **High density of Na+ and K+ voltage gated channels** (many more than the soma)

   This is why it is the **starting point of the action potential** : when depolarization
threshold is reached, it triggers voltage-gated sodium channel opening and the
generation of an action potential
   - Area of **maximum susceptibility** to trigger an action potential

   - Considered as the “first node of Ranvier”

**Postsynaptic receptors**
2 main types of neurotransmitter receptors:

**●** **Ionotropic receptors: direct effect.** Ionotropic receptors are membrane-bound
receptor proteins that respond to ligand binding by **opening an ion channel** and
allowing ions into the cell.

  - **Metabotropic receptors** : **indirect effect.** The neurotransmitter binds to a
**G-coupled receptor** . Instead of making direct effects, they cause effects mediated
by **secondary messengers.**

**Diverse functions:**

        Its effect can be to open ionic channels.
The difference with ionotropic receptors
is that the secondary messenger can
travel to other parts of the neuron and
open channels in other parts. The

effects are not limited to where the

channel is found.

        They can also have other effects such
as protein synthesis, transcription,...

*[PDF p. 228]*

Post-synaptic potentials

They can be excitatory or inhibitory:

**Excitatory. EPSPs:**

   They have an **electrotonic propagation.** Amplitude of the EPSP decreases with
distance, depending on the impedance (lower in the soma)

   EPSPs propagate homogeneously in all directions
   - Excitatory postsynaptic potential begins in the **trigger zone** if the **threshold is**

**reached**

   If we reach the -60mV threshold in the trigger zone, there will be transmision of an
action potential. If the trigger zone is not depolarized, there will be no transmission.
Excitation generally triggered by Na + _(can also be Ca2+ in some cases)_

   Use both ionotropic and metabotropic receptors

   **Retrograde propagation of the action potential:** The action potential propagates
retrogradely **to the neuronal soma,** leaving the neuron in refractory period.
There is an electrotonic propagation back into the soma (low resistance) and can
even reach thick regions of the dendrite. The retrograde propagation to the dendrites
can play a role in **neuronal plasticity,** via **calcium channels**

**Inhibitory. IPSPs:**
_What ions can cause inhibition?_ those with equilibrium potential which is more negative than
the resting potential: **K+ and Cl-,** the most typical generating inhibitory potentials being Cl

   - Cl- channels: increase Cl- conductance. **ECl = -90mV**

   - K+ channels: increase K+ conductance. **EK = -95mV**

➢ _**Generation of IPSPs**_

To generate IPSPs: there are **2 types of inhibitory receptors,** as for excitatory receptors:

**●** **Ionotropic inhibitory receptors:**

        Glycine (tetanus), via Cl        - **GABA,** vía Cl

**●** **Metabotropic Inhibitory receptors:**

        - 5HY2C = via IP3/DAG

        - 5HTA1 = via decrease in cAMP

        **Muscarinic receptor M2** = via ↓ in cAMP and opening of K+ channels

➢ _**Localization of IPSPs**_

Inhibitory postsynaptic potentials will take place **near the trigger zone** :
Most IPSPs **originate in the soma** (as opposed to EPSPs). Therefore, they propagate
better, faster, higher effect.
It’s a **compensation for 2 facts:**

   Excitatory synapses are much more abundant

   EPSPs have higher amplitude. With inhibitory, we can only reach around 20mV of
change in potential. They are less powerful
This is why **we need to compensate by the location (soma).**

➢ _**Effects of IPSPs**_ :

They are a fundamental mechanism of synaptic control. 2 types of actions on the excitability
of the cell: **reduce or block** the probability of firing an action potential.

*[PDF p. 229]*

_loss of inhibition = epileptic sequences. It is necessary to have inhibition._

Presynaptic inhibition

   - **Axo-axonal** type of synapse: they are mostly **inhibitory**

   Acts on the presynaptic bouton

   Inhibits the synapse with **no additional changes in the postsynaptic neuron** : only
affects that individual synapse
   - Global result = **reduction in the amount of neurotransmitters released** by the

main axon.

   Through several mechanisms:

i. Small sustained depolarization: inhibition caused by very slow and sustained
depolarization _(Enkephalin, widely used as a painkiller)_ .

             - The blocking of Na channels **decreases axonal Ea**

             Less voltage-gated Ca channels are opened

**-** **NT release is reduced**

ii. Hyperpolarization: Causes **lower aperture of Ca2+ voltage-gated channels.**
Depolarization is smaller, less Ca2+ calcium voltage gated channels open =
less neurotransmitter release. Action of **GABA** on neurons, via GABA B

receptors.
iii. Block Ca2+ voltage-gated channels: lower amount of Ca entering = **less**
**neurotransmitter release.** This is what **opioids** do.
iv. Non-Ca mediated NT exocytosis block:

**Example of presynaptic inhibition: spinal mechanism of pain control**

Presynaptic facilitation

**Contrary to inhibition.** The axo-axonal synapse **increases the number of NT released**
with the same action potential.

   Facilitatory **axo-axonal synapses**
   - Don’t depolarize the postsynaptic cell; rather, they **potentiate NT release** = more

stimulation

   - Example: the action of **Serotonin** on the memory circuits in Aplysia

Synaptic integration

In synapses, we integrate by using:

*[PDF p. 230]*

- **Spatial summation:** depends on the **length**
**constant**, which depends on **resistance** _(diameter_
_of the dendrite)_ **and distance** _(distance from each_
_individual dendrite to the point where they meet)._
Spatial summation: begins at **two different points,**
**A and B.** If they propagate well, the effect of A and
B will be enough when it reaches point C, and the
potentials will join their effect. If not well
propagated, it won’t be enough when reaching the
final point.

  - **Temporal summation:** depends on the **time**
**constant,** which at the same time depends on the
**capacitance** of the membrane. 2 in the same point.
If the duration is very long **(long time constant)**
**they join = big action potential**

So.. why do we have synapses?

_Divergence: amplification_
_Convergence: integration_
_Modulation: we can modulate how other neurons discharge_

_Excitation and inhibition_

_Spatial and temporal summation_
_Plasticity: we can add or decrease the number of synapses_

*[PDF p. 231]*

#### Introduction to neuromuscular junction disorders

The disorders of the neuromuscular junction are classified according to the portion of the
synapse that is affected: presynaptic and postsynaptic disorders. The differentiation is
important because the clinical course, the diagnostic process and the treatment vary.

**1. Presynaptic disorders**

In this group of disorders, the affected portion is the terminal bouton of the motor neuron.
The common effect in all of them is a reduced acetylcholine release despite a normal arrival
of action potentials. Two disorders will be discussed in more detail: botulism and
Lambert-Eaton syndrome

A) BOTULISM

Botulinum toxin is one of the most powerful poisons found in nature. The toxin is generated
by a bacterium named _Clostridium botulinum_ . The characteristics of the bacteria and the
clinical course of the disease will be seen in detail in other courses. In short, this bacterium
does not usually directly infect our organism. When it grows in an adequate manner (like
improperly processed preserves) it can produce the toxin, and it is the intake of the toxin
(with the food) that generates the clinical consequences.

Botulinum toxin has a heavy and a light chain (with several subtypes of the latter; only
subtypes A, B and E are related to human disease). The heavy chain allows the toxin to
enter cholinergic neurons (giving the neuronal specificity), while the light chain is an
endopeptidase that cleaves proteins involved in neurotransmitter vesicle release.

How does botulinum toxin block the release of acetylcholine in the presynaptic terminal? As
seen in the previous session (and shown in the image), there are several proteins involved in
the fusion of neurotransmitter vesicles with the presynaptic membrane. Some of these
proteins are located in the membrane of the vesicle (v-SNAREs) while others are located in
the membrane of the neuron (t-SNAREs). Synaptotagmin is the calcium sensor that begins
the fusion process. The different subtypes of botulinum toxin cleave v-SNAREs or t-SNAREs
due to their endopeptidase action, making the fusion process impossible.

Although the fusion mechanism of neurotransmitter vesicles is common to all neurons (and
other vesicle fusion processes), botulinum toxin only acts in some types of cholinergic
neurons due to the selectivity of entry mechanism provided by the heavy chain, as
mentioned above. In fact, the light chain of the tetanus toxin (produced by another bacteria
of the same genre, _Clostridium tetani_ ) has the same mechanism of action as the light chain
of the botulinum toxin, but the clinical effect is apparently opposite (in the tetanus, there is

*[PDF p. 232]*

involuntary contraction of several muscles groups). How can this be possible? Because
instead of inhibiting the neurotransmitter release in motor neurons, the tetanus toxin inhibits
the release of neurotransmitter in inhibitory motor neurons (its heavy chain takes the toxin
into these interneurons). The final effect of a loss of inhibition in motor circuits is an excess

of contraction.

Botulinum toxin can affect other subtypes of cholinergic neurons apart from motor neurons.
Thus, autonomic symptoms and signs (which will be studied later) may be part of the clinical

course of the disease.

B) LAMBERT-EATON SYNDROME

This is an autoimmune syndrome usually observed in the context of certain neoplasms. The
appearance of antibodies against the voltage-gated calcium channels of the presynaptic
terminal leads to a decreased release of acetylcholine, despite the presence of normal
action potentials in the motor axon. As in botulism, other subtypes of cholinergic neurons
can be affected, causing autonomic dysfunction.

**2. Postsynaptic disorders: MYASTHENIA GRAVIS**

In this group of disorders, the postsynaptic portion of the synapse (and particularly, the
nicotinic receptors) is affected.

The prototypical postsynaptic disorder is myasthenia gravis, a disease you have already

heard of.

In summary, it is an autoimmune disease in which there are antibodies against the nicotinic
receptors of the muscle that lead to their elimination. As the antibodies are specific against
this subtype of receptor, there is no autonomic involvement (unless there are other
antibodies produced in the context of the autoimmune dysfunction).

**3.** **Relevance of knowing both types of disorders**

There are diagnostic tests that can differentiate both types of disorders depending on the
response to brief or prolonged exercise, or to repetitive stimulation. It is beyond the scope of
this course to enter in more detail about this. But, as a simple rule, a presynaptic defect can
be compensated (in the short term) with an increase in the firing rate of action potentials in
the motor neuron (leading to an increase in acetylcholine release). However, in postsynaptic
defects the problem is not in the ACh release, but in the limited number of receptors
available. An increased firing rate of action potentials may lead to neurotransmitter depletion
without achieving any significant effect. Instead, in myasthenia gravis the patient can benefit
from acetylcholinesterase inhibitors, used as symptomatic treatment. These inhibitors
prolong the union of ACh to the receptors, maximizing the effect of the remaining nicotinic

channels.

*[PDF p. 233]*

**4.** **Drugs that block the neuromuscular junction**

In patients under general anesthesia, muscle relaxation may be convenient after intubation
to avoid fighting against the respirator, and to facilitate the work of the surgeon. There are
two main types of neuromuscular blockers: analogues of curare (a natural poison) that block
the union of ACh to the nicotinic receptors, and depolarizing blockers, that keep permanently
open the nicotinic channels, generating a sustained depolarization of the muscle fiber
membrane. This mechanism is similar to the primary afferent depolarization mechanism
studied in presynaptic inhibition. If the membrane is kept depolarized at a level beyond the
activation threshold of the sodium voltage-gated channels, these channels remain blocked
and there cannot initiate any new action potential.

*[PDF p. 234]*

**SKELETAL MUSCLE**

#### Muscle contraction

The nerve branches and reaches several muscle fibers, forming
the **terminal motor plaque.**

Each muscle fiber receives innervation from 1 motor neuron.

The **motor neuron can innervate many motor fibers.** All
those muscle fibers get depolarized and generate action
potentials at the same time: it’s like a **functional unit,** which is
why it's all grouped together into the so-called motor plaque.

**Sequence of events in the postsynaptic terminal**

The release of Ach neurotransmitters occurs. These reach their respective
receptors in the motor end plate, causing the opening of ligand-gated Na
channels, which leads to Na influx and K efflux = depolarization. These
nicotinic receptors have **2 binding**
**sites for the Ach ligand.**
The depolarization extends along the
cell and reaches voltage-gated Na
channels, which also open. All
contributes to generating **muscle**
**action potential.**

**Sarcoplasmic reticulum and T tubules**

This series of events will lead to a **Ca2+ increase in the cytoplasm of the muscle fiber.**
But the mechanism is somehow different than to what happens in neurons:

   The action potential is generated in the center of the fiber and it **propagates**
**in both directions** (fastest way to propagate).

   We want it to generate an increase in the muscle’s intracellular Ca2+ levels.
For this purpose: the **sarcolemma is invaginated** every short distance, to

**increase the surface area** .

   These invaginations, called **T tubules, allow the membrane to be in very**
**close contact to the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SER).**
   - In those invaginations the **reticulum is adjacent to the membrane.** This

area constitutes what is known as a **triad.**

This **interaction between membranes** will

cause the **increase of intracellular**

**calcium.** However it doesn’t come from

outside _(as in the case of neurons),_ but from
**inside** . In neurons for ex, it comes from the

extracellular space and enters the cell. But

not in this case.

This is the case for skeletal muscle. **In cardiac muscle, Ca2+ additionally comes from**

**outside as well as inside.**

*[PDF p. 235]*

**DHP and RYR proteins - Ca2+ release**

2 very important proteins allow this depolarization transmission and

Ca2+ release:

   - **DHPR,** located on the **T tubule (sarcolemma)**
   - And **RYR**, located on the **SER membrane.**
DHPR changes its conformation upon stimulation and acts on RYR,
which opens by changing its conformation and allows Ca2+ release

inside the cell. **4 molecules of DHPR interact with 1 molecule of**

**RYR to trigger calcium release.**

_DHPR will also act as a calcium channel in cardiac cells to allow_

_calcium entry, but in this case, it's only role is to interact with RYR._

**How do we get Ca2+ inside the SER?**

Various proteins:

   - **SERCA:** it acts as a **calcium pump,** constantly pumping calcium
into the reticulum. After each action potential, there is a short
lasting calcium release, as this protein will **quickly pump Ca2+**
**back into the SER.** Therefore, a single action potential generates
a short transient contraction, stopped by the retrieving of Ca.

   Other proteins

**Skeletal muscle contraction**

**Structure of striated muscle:** we have **thin and thick filaments.**

   - Thin filaments = mostly **actin monomers**,
+ two other proteins: **troponin and**
**tropomyosin.**
   - Thick filaments = mostly made up of
**myosin** . Very large protein, with **2 heavy**
**and 2 light chains,** which together form a
characteristic shape: heads (light chains)
and tails (heavy chains)

**Contraction** will occur due to the **movement of**

**the thin filaments over the thick filaments.**

Looking at an image of a muscle fiber, we can
observe different bands corresponding to each
region of the sarcomere: I band, A band, M line, Z
band,....

Many **myosin heads** will be found **pointing in different**
**directions, at different locations.** In the thin filaments,
we will find **troponin and tropomyosin.** Myosin head will
bind the actin, turn and cause a displacement of the thin
filament. It’ll then unbind, recover the original position and
prepare to bind to a different site, more advanced. In this
way, it moves along the thin filament.

*[PDF p. 236]*

**Process of contraction in skeletal muscle**

**Calcium is the trigger** for the beginning of contraction

  - In the muscle at rest, the **binding site** for myosin is **covered by tropomyosin.**

Muscle is **relaxed** .

  - In the muscle during contraction

        **Ca binds troponin (troponin C),** making it change conformation. It **pulls**
**tropomyosin** from the binding site, leaving it **exposed.** Immediately, **myosin**
_**(very high affinity for actin**_ _in skeletal muscle fibers_ _**)**_ binds it. Calcium allows
myosin to bind to actin.

        From here there is an automated process: myosin binds, and the **binding**
**triggers a change in conformation in myosin** _**(turn).**_

        Up till now, there has been no energy consumption. But **energy is needed to**
**release the myosin head** from the actin and recover the original position. At
rest, myosin has an ADP bound to it. The **turn causes ADP** to be **released,**
and this site is **free for ATP binding.** When ATP is bound to myosin, it is
**converted to ADP and P** is released, causing the **detachment of myosin**

**from actin.**

_**Rigor mortis**_

When someone is dead, there is muscle

contraction and no relaxation, as there is

calcium in the cell due to cellular damage
but no available energy as the person is
dead. It causes **rigidity in the corpse.**
This phenomenon is known as _**rigor**_
_**mortis:**_ the person
stays rigid, as the
**energy needed for**
**myosin release is**
**not present.**

**SMOOTH MUSCLE**

   - The **common component** between smooth and skeletal muscle

excitation-contraction is **Calcium.**

   - **DHPR - RYR coupling** only happens in **skeletal muscle.** It also occurs with slight
differences in cardiac muscle. But it doesn’t occur in this way in smooth muscle.
   - There is also **no troponin present in smooth muscle**

   Sometimes there is even no action potential needed

Smooth muscle is designed for different roles than those of skeletal muscle. It’s designed for
longer contraction processes. The contraction is therefore slower, so it can be contracted for
longer periods.

*[PDF p. 237]*

**Types of smooth muscle**

There are **2 main types** of smooth muscle:

   - **Multiunit smooth muscle:** made up of **individual**
**fibers** (each cell is a unit). Each fiber will **contract and**
**relax independently.**

   **Single unit or visceral smooth muscle:** they are all
**bound by gap junctions,** as if they were a single very
long fiber. Calcium increase will be transmitted through
the different fibers through gap junctions. They can
**contract coordinately.**

**Varicosities**

In smooth muscle we don’t usually talk about synapses, but **Varicosities** . The
neurotransmitters are released and diffuse to many nearby areas. **Not restricted to a**
**synaptic cleft.** There can be **many varicosity regions per axon.**

In the case of **visceral smooth muscle,** we **don’t need many varicosities per fiber,** as the
cells are interconnected by **gap junctions** that will transmit the calcium release triggered by
1 varicosity. These muscle fibers can also be activated by circulating hormones released
from the blood, not only by neurotransmitters.

**Calcium release** in smooth muscle can be triggered by:

   - **Neurotransmitters** released at **varicosity**
**regions**
   - **Hormones** coming in from the **bloodstream**

**Disposition and contraction/relaxation patterns**

**Disposition of smooth muscle is very variable**
_Example: found in the digestive tract, where there are_
_longitudinal and transverse movements (contractions) that_
_cause peristalsis._

**Contraction/relaxation patterns are also very variable**
( _smooth muscles in some regions are normally contracted_
_and have slight periods of relaxation; others are always_
_partially contracted, like blood vessels which have to_
_maintain a tone,..)_

We therefore need different mechanisms than the ones for

skeletal muscle.

**Excitation-contraction coupling in smooth muscle**

Common and differential features

   The only common component shared with skeletal muscle coupling is **calcium,**
which triggers contraction.
   - We also find **thin and thick filaments,** but the distribution is much different. Much
more dispersed and less organized. They DON’T form sarcomeres.

**-**
**No tropomyosin and no troponin**

*[PDF p. 238]*

The **coupling mechanism is different** in smooth muscle **.**

   - Calcium activates a **kinase of MLCK (myosin light**
**chain kinase),** which **phosphorylates the light chain of**
**myosin.**
   - This **increases the affinity of myosin for actin** (here
myosin is a different subtype. In smooth muscle, if not
phosphorylated, myosin has very little affinity for actin).
When calcium increases, affinity increases as myosin is
**phosphorylated** and there can be **contraction** .
   - The **contraction process and dynamics**, its movement
over actin, is the **same** as in skeletal muscle. But the

activation is different.

   In smooth muscle, the **actin binding site is always**
**exposed** _(no troponin or tropomyosin)_ as this type of
myosin has very low affinity.

   - At the same time there is a **phosphatase dephosphorylating myosin**, **removing**
**its affinity.** It's a process in equilibrium of phosphorylation and dephosphorylation.

**But how does calcium increase in smooth muscle?**

  - We may have **calcium coming from the outside:**

➢It can enter by **opening of Ca voltage-gated channels**

             Caused by opening due to depolarization

➢It can also occur through **ligand-gated calcium channels**

             through direct activation (neurotransmitter)

             via second messengers (hormones)
**The 2nd mechanism can trigger the 1st.** Calcium entering the muscle fiber
through ligand gated channels triggers a depolarization _(as there are charged_
_particles passing across the membrane)_ and allows the opening of calcium
voltage gated channels.

  - **Coming from inside**, from the **reticulum**,

        - Activation via second messengers like **IP3/DAG.**

**Muscle contraction modulation in smooth muscle**

There are **other pathways** for muscle contraction **modulation** that **don’t involve calcium.**

**●** **Calcium sensitivity regulation mediated by Rho**
We have a phosphorylase and phosphatase acting on myosin. These
can be controlled by other pathways, most common: the one mediated
by **Rho** _(bound to GTP)._
**Calcium** activates **the kinase,** but many different mechanisms can
control and **modulate the phosphatase, moving myosin to a low**
**affinity version** and contrarrest the effects of calcium.

*[PDF p. 239]*

**●** **Smooth muscle relaxation mediated by NO**
_Viagra = relaxation of smooth muscles in the arteries. Contains NO._

How does it modulate? Combination of **mechanisms that act on different stages.**

   - Mechanism 1: begins at a G protein receptor and can
inhibit the activation of IP3 as a 2nd messenger

   - Mechanism 2: inhibit/lowers the release of calcium from

the ER

   - Mechanism 3: acts on the phosphatase of the myosin
light chain
   - Mechanism 4: opening potassium channels =
reduces/contrarrests the action potential generated by

Ca channels

   - Mechanism 5: direct block of Ca2+ voltage gated

channels

All these mechanisms **trigger smooth muscle relaxation**

**Calcium sensitization and desensitization**

Calcium generates smooth muscle contraction, although

there are different mechanisms that can modulate this

contraction generated by Ca. This gives out this **curve:**
more or less **degree of contraction depending on the**

**concentration of calcium.**

   - If we activate the _phosphatase_ through modulating mechanisms, we will need more
calcium to trigger contraction (right).

   If the phosphatase is not active, we will need less calcium for contraction (left).
It **can be displaced to the right or left** depending on these additional modulatory
mechanisms. This is known as **sensitization and desensitization** of muscle and it **only**
**happens in smooth muscle.** In skeletal muscle, contraction will be higher or lower only
depending on the received action potential.

**Patterns of contraction**

There are **many different patterns of contraction,** due to the many different mechanisms

available for smooth muscle contraction.

_1st graph:_ we may have some similar mechanism to
that in skeletal muscle. Many action potentials
combine and generate contraction
_2nd graph:_ mixed: fluctuations in membrane
potentials and action potentials = graph that is
similar to that of membrane potential and increases
when there is an action potential triggered.
_3rd graph:_ membrane pot doesn’t change but
hormones trigger the release of intracellular calcium

Main second messengers: **IP3** _(always generates contraction when released)_

*[PDF p. 240]*

**Long lasting contractions**

Smooth muscle has **different roles** than those of skeletal muscle. It’s designed to be much
more flexible and be contracted for a very long time _(ex: sphincters, 23h per day; soleus =_
_many h per day, whenever we are standing)._
It is **optimized to have the minimum energy consumption** during that time.

In smooth muscle, to generate a long lasting
contraction, the **key is to increase calcium**
**concentration** which then stays at an **intermediate**
**concentration** for a long time. This is only possible in
smooth muscle, as skeletal muscle only uses action
potentials, but in **smooth muscle we can control**

**intracellular calcium levels.**

The key is keeping these **intermediate levels of**
**calcium** so we only **increase the affinity of some**
**portions of myosin.**
An initial high peak intracellular calcium concentration
is necessary to initiate the contraction, but it can then
be maintained at intermediate levels, meaning it does
things **slower** but can keep a **low level of**
**contraction without excessive energy consumption.**

**Smooth muscle structure. Adaptability**

Myofibrils are very rigid and strictly parallel in skeletal muscle.
But in smooth muscle they’re **not strictly parallele.** This allows the muscle fiber to **change**
**shape and adapt.** In the case of the bladder: allows it to distend, but retain the ability to
contract when needed. It can adapt to different tensions depending on the volume. Skeletal
muscle is much more rigid and is not so adaptable. Smooth muscle **keeps the ability to**
**contract even when it is stretched** . **It’s more adaptable.**

_Socrative: in hypocalcemia, somehow, although striated_
_muscle calcium comes from the inside, if there are low_
_extracellular calcium levels they can affect K+ channels._
_Hypocalcemia triggers K+ channels to close, making the_
_membrane potential more positive. It triggers spontaneous_
_depolarization as K is no longer exiting to the extracellular_
_space. This manifests as a higher tendency towards_
_muscle contraction, which manifests a tetani._

**Smooth muscle growth**

In smooth muscle we only have one nuclei per cell. To grow, the cell may
**increase the genetic material** (ploidy) inside the cell, but without
increasing the number of nuclei _(in skeletal muscle, we do find growing_
_multinucleated cells)._ They can **become tetraploid or even octoploid**
**(hypertrophy).**

*[PDF p. 241]*

SMOOTH MUSCLE SUMMARY

_-_
_Smooth muscle cells can be independent (multi unitary) or associated (unitary)_

_-_
_Muscle contraction can be triggered by action potentials, but also by changes in_
_membrane potential, or neurotransmitter/hormone action with no change in Em_

_-_
_Actin/myosin coupling is triggered by myosin phosphorylation (there is no troponin_
_nor tropomyosin). Myosin phosphorylation is regulated through different pathways_
_(and calcium)_

_-_
_Smooth muscle can adapt to distension or contraction + hypertrophy_

**MUSCLE FIBER TYPES. SKELETAL MUSCLE**

In muscle, there are **2 ways to increase force:**

  - **Increase in the rate of action potentials:**

        Incomplete tetanus: it’s a way to increase force in striated muscle: increasing
the number of action potentials per second, even when calcium levels go

back to baseline.

        When we launch action potentials at a rate
higher than 30%, we generate what is known
as tetanus force: we don’t allow calcium to go

back to baseline level and the muscle remains

contracted for a very long time

**●** **Recruit more motor units**

Within striated muscle we have **different types of muscle fibers. Type I** fibers are slow,
and **type II** fibers are fast. We find **II A and II B,** which differ in their resistance to fatigue.

**Type I fibers:** designed to be
**contracted for a long time.**
However, it is still a much faster

contraction than in smooth muscle.

   Low contraction velocity

   High oxidative enzymes

   High resistance to fatigue
They have a type of **myosin that is**
**slower in contraction,** which
allows them to carry out slower

contraction and be contracted for

longer. Can be contracted for **hours**

**fast resistant fibers.**
**Type II A fibers:**
They resist fatigue, as they have
**more oxidative enzymes** than Type
IIB and can carry out more anaerobic

metabolism under low 02 conditions

present during fatigue.

   High contraction velocity

   High oxidative enzymes

   High resistance to fatigue

Can be contracted for a **few minutes**

Fast ATPase. Aerobic and anaerobic

**Type II B fibers:** **fast fatigue**
**fibers.** They barely resist under

anaerobic conditions.

   High contraction velocity

   Low oxidative enzymes

   Low resistance to fatigue
Designed to be contracted for

**few seconds**

Fast ATPase. Only anaerobic
glycolysis.

*[PDF p. 242]*

We can **change** muscle fibers **from type B to type A** during **resistance training,** as we
only have to change a few enzymes. But we **cannot change between type I and type II,** as
they have many different components and types of myosin which can’t be changed from one

to another.

   muscles with type II B fibers = mostly brown-ish

   Muscles with many type II A fibers and type I fibers = reddish as they have a high
blood supply for fast activity

**What muscle will have faster contraction?**

This is due to metabolic differences in many components:

   **Mitochondrial enzymes** . Oxidative enzyme contact will determine resistance to
fatigue and length of contraction.
   - There are differences in **troponin and tropomyosin.** Faster troponin= faster

contraction.

   - Differences in **SERCA** transmembrane protein. If SERCA is very active, contraction
is very short as calcium is quickly removed.

Most of these differences are **induced by their**

**innervation.** It’s the motor neurons which determine if

the muscle will become type I or type II. Motor units that
make maximal force need type II B fibers, as they will
only need max force, very punctually. All the **motor**

**fibers within the same motor unit are of the same**

**type (I, II A II B)**

**Exercise induced changes**

➢During **resistance training** we can **change type II B to II A fibers,** by increasing the

amount of mitochondria and enzymes (oxidative enzymes = more resistance to
fatigue), etc…. But we cannot change myosin, SERCA or troponin, so we **cannot**
**change from type I to type II or vice versa.**

➢When we do **force training,** we **generate more myofibrils** in

**parallel** . The **diameter** of the muscle fiber **increases,** which
allows us to have **more force but not more velocity.** This
velocity only depends on the capacity to trigger contraction of
many sarcomeres at the same time.

        - Sarcomeres in parallel = strength
        - Sarcomeres in length = velocity
Therefore if they grow in parallel, they will increase in strength
but not in velocity. Only sarcomeres in series are related to
speed.

**Skeletal muscle metabolism**

*[PDF p. 243]*

i. In the muscle, when we begin to contract, we use the **available ATP in the**
**cytoplasm.**
ii. To obtain more, the **phosphocreatine system** will convert **ADPs to ATP** s for about

8-10 seconds.

iii. If we still need energy, **glucose metabolism** begins.

        If we need fast energy, we carry out many successive **glycolysis**
**(anaerobic)** . It generates lactic acid, which will then be reused. But this only
gives 2ATPs per glucose. It’s fast but all the glycogen would finally be

consumed.

        - **To keep the muscle contraction,** we need to carry out **aerobic metabolism**
(yields 32 APs per glucose). However, it is slower. To carry out aerobic
metabolism we have to **lower energy consumption,** which explains the
presence of **type I fibers:** they maintain the muscle contracted but with lower
energy consumption as the contraction is slower.

   - Low distances, fast sprints = anaerobic metabolism
   - Resistance, long distance = aerobic metabolism

**Why do we hyperventilate?**

If we do **anaerobic metabolism for prolonged time,** we will need to compensate at some
point. We **need to recover.** Lactic acid needs to be turned into pyruvate and through the
Krebs system, to be turned back to glucose or consumed. **This can**
**only be done through aerobic metabolism.**
This is what is known as **oxygen debt:** the O2 needed to carry out
aerobic metabolism which compensates for that anaerobic metabolism
which we’ve done to quickly perform a task. This is why we
**hyperventilate.**

**Differences between aerobic and anaerobic exercise:**

**What is soreness (“agujetas”)?**

The cause is not fully established. Some theories say
the accumulation of lactic acid was the cause, caused
by the generation of lactic acid crystals. But this is
false. This soreness is probably caused by

**microtears in the muscle and inflammation.**

*[PDF p. 244]*

#### Accessory proteins and disorders

Myofibrils contract due to the displacement of thin filaments (actin) over thick filaments
(myosin), shortening the sarcomeres. But myofibrils must be attached to the

sarcolemma, and the sarcolemma must be linked to the connective tissue around it, in

order to transmit the force generated by the contraction. Moreover, if the myofibrils are
not properly attached to the sarcolemma, and the sarcolemma is not anchored to the
connective tissue around, the muscle fibers will suffer damage.

Let’s go step by step. Muscles usually exert their force through tendons that insert into
a bone (although there are exceptions, like the tongue). Muscle fibers (muscle cells) do
not extend up to this insertion. Tendons are formed by connective tissue (they are
acellular); in fact they are formed by the fusion of the different connective layers that
surround the fibers (endomysium), the muscle fascicles (perimysium) and the whole
muscle (epimysium) (see the image below).

Thus, muscle fibers must be firmly attached (through the sarcolemma) to the

connective tissue that surrounds them in order to transmit their contractile force to the

tendon (or any other insertion). But inside the fiber, myofibrils must be attached to other
myofibrils, to the cytoskeleton and to the sarcolemma in order to generate and transmit
force adequately. Force transmission should therefore be both longitudinal (along the
main axis of the fiber, to the tendon) and lateral (circumferential, through the minor
axis, linking myofibrils, cytoskeleton, sarcolemma and connective tissue).

Thin filaments are attached to the Z bands by means of alpha-actinin. Thick filaments
are also attached to the Z bands, by means of an “elastic” protein, titin. Titin needs to

be elastic because the distance between the end of the thick filaments and the Z band

*[PDF p. 245]*

depends on the overlap between thin and thick filaments (if the sarcomere is relaxed,
the distance is longer than if the sarcomere is contracted). Myomesin links thick
filaments in the M line (see figure).

Myofibrils are attached to the cytoskeleton (that includes a different subtype of actin
fibers) by desmin (from Z lines) and dystrophin. Desmin links the different myofibrils,
and through the cytoskeleton, attaches them to the nuclei and mitochondria. It also
attaches the myofibrils to the membrane (integrins). Dystrophin is the main responsible
of attaching the cytoskeleton of the muscle fiber to the sarcolemma and the

extracellular connective tissue.

The different protein complexes that attach the membrane both to the extracellular

tissue and to the intracellular structures are named costameres. In the skeletal muscle,

the most relevant costameres are the integrin complex (that interacts with desmin) and
the complex that includes dystroglycan/sarcoglycan/dystrobrevin/sarcospan and other
proteins, where dystrophin binds. The costameres bind to the extracellular collagen by
means of laminin alpha 2.

*[PDF p. 246]*

It is clinically relevant to know these attachment systems because many muscular
dystrophies are due to defects in some of these proteins. Other forms of muscular
dystrophy are due to damage in other structural proteins or in intracellular signaling
pathways related to them. A defective attachment of the myofibrils to the sarcolemma
and the surrounding connective tissue (that will form the tendon), besides impeding an
adequate force transmission, also damages the muscle fibers due to an excess of
movement. Alterations in desmin usually associated with cardiac muscle disorders;
cardiac muscle (as you will learn later) shares many structural similarities. Absence of
dystrophin causes Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD), a severe disorder, while its
dysfunction causes a milder form of muscular dystrophy named Becker muscular
dystrophy. Alterations in the genes that encode the different sarcoglycans cause a
different type of muscular dystrophy, globally known as “limb-girdle” muscular
dystrophies (LGMD), as the muscles affected initially are located around the pelvic and
scapular areas. Defects in alpha 2 laminin, extracellular collagen VI, titin, or the
complex dysferlin/caveolin 3 (related to sarcolemmal repair; the sarcolemma may suffer
damage during normal contraction) also cause muscular dystrophies.

*[PDF p. 247]*

#### AUTONOMIC NERVOUS SYSTEM

*[PDF p. 248]*

#### Autonomic nervous system II

**INTRODUCTION**

Division of the nervous system that controls bodily functions in a largely **unconscious**
**manner**, supplying innervation to **smooth muscle, glands and internal organs.**

General functions

                                 Heart and respiratory rates

                                 Digestion

                                 Pupillary response

                                 - Urination

                                 - Sexual arousal

                                 Body temperature

                                 - Nutrition

Classified into the **afferent branch** (sensory)
and **efferent branch** (motor, further subdivided
into Sympathetic and Parasympathetic SNA)

**EFFERENT BRANCH**

**Efferent branch organization**

   **Preganglionic fiber:** stems from the CNS and ends in an autonomic ganglion

   **Postganglionic fiber:** stems from the autonomic ganglion and ends in its target

effector

**A.** **SYMPATHETIC SYSTEM**

**“Fight-flight” response**

Characteristics

   - CNS: sympathetic motor neurons arise at the **intermediate cell columns**, which are
found from **T1-L2** along the spinal cord.

   Autonomic ganglion: from where the postganglionic neuron will originate. These
neurons extend their processes either to:

    - **Sympathetic trunk ganglia** (cervical ganglia and ganglia which give rise to
the cardiac nerves)

    - **Preaortic single ganglia** (thoracic and lumbar splanchnic nerves)

As the preganglionic fibers extend either to sympathetic trunk or
preaortic single ganglia _(further, but still pretty close to the spinal_
_cord)_, we find that:

**-** **Preganglionic fibers** are quite **short**

**-**
**Postganglionic neurons** have very **long** fibers, extending
all the way to effectors

*[PDF p. 249]*

Levels of innervation

The sympathetic trunk extends from T1-L2 / L3

**Cervical ganglia:** at this level, there is no intermediolateral horn in the spinal cord to give
rise to sympathetic motor neurons. Therefore, preganglionic neurons from lower levels
where there is this horn, extend their processes up to cervical ganglia, from where the
postganglionic neuron will arise.
Provided by **T1-T2 short preganglionic fibers,** which extend their processes up to:

   **Superior cervical ganglion** (C1-C4 levels)

   **Middle cervical ganglion** (C5-C6 levels)

   **Inferior cervical ganglion** (C7-C8 levels)

**Cardiac plexus** : arise from T3-T6. Long postganglionic fibers.

**-**
**Thoracic and lumbar plexus** **Prevertebral ganglia**
These nerves, which innervate abdominal viscera, arise at preaortic single ganglia:

   **Celiac ganglion & Superior mesenteric ganglion** (T7-T11 preganglionic fibers)

   **Inferior mesenteric ganglion** (T12-L3 preganglionic fibers)

**Dermal smooth muscle innervation**

In parallel, along the whole of the spinal cord, we will have sympathetic motor
neurons which will innervate **blood vessels and dermal smooth muscle** (ex:
sweat glands) all over the body. These portions aren’t just restricted to areas in the
abdomen, but are found all over. Therefore, the preganglionic neurons arising from
the intermediolateral horn will extend up or down along the sympathetic trunk.
When they reach the level they are ought to innervate, they connect with their
corresponding postganglionic neuron in the sympathetic trunk ganglia.
This postganglionic neuron will then **accompany somatic neurons** which go to
innervate skeletal muscle found at the same portion as the dermis _(remember:_
_each portion of muscle arises from the same somite as its corresponding portion of_
_skin. In the somites we find myotome and dermatome)_ .
Depending on where it is located, it will accompany the ones taking the **dorsal ramus** (to the
back) **or ventral ramus** (to the hypaxial skeleton: muscles and dermis in the abdomen and
limbs).

Adrenal medulla - Chroma�n cells

Adrenal Medulla: provided by **T5-T9 preganglionic**

**fibers**

The adrenal medulla cells are **modified**

**postganglionic sympathetic neurons,** known as

**chromaffin cells.**

These cells release **adrenaline** (also known as
epinephrine) into the blood mainstream.

   **Adrenaline synthesis** requires large
concentrations of **cortisol**, which is provided
by venous supply from the **adrenal cortex**

*[PDF p. 250]*

**B.** **PARASYMPATHETIC SYSTEM**

Characteristics

   - CNS: Brainstem and lumbosacral spinal cord (S2-S4)

   Autonomic ganglion: Next to the target effector. Postganglionic PS neurons originate
very close to their target organ

As a consequence, we will find that in the Parasympathetic system:

   **Preganglionic fibers** are very **long**, extend all the way to the target organ
**-** **Postganglionic fibers** are **short**

Levels of innervation

**Brain Stem** (Face => splenic colic flexure)

   Long Preganglionic fibers

    - Edinger-Westphal Nuclei, part of the oculomotor nucleus
(III)

    - Superior and inferior Salivatory nuclei (VII)

    - Dorsal vagal nuclei (X) → vagus nerve

    - Ambiguus nucleus (IX and X)

   Short Postganglionic fibers

    - Pupil (III)

    - Lacrimal glands (VII)

    - Salivary glands (VII)

**○** **Thoraco-abdominal organs down to the splenic colic**
**flexure (X)**

**Sacral spinal cord, S2-S4** (Splenic colic flexure => Pelvic organs)

   Long Preganglionic fiber
Different **pelvic nerves** innervating abdominal and pelvic organs
**from the splenic colic flexure downwards**

   Short Postganglionic fiber (Target organ)

**SYMPATHETIC VS PARASYMPATHETIC**

**SYMPATHETIC**

- Intermediolateral cell columns

Sympathetic trunk

Cervical ganglia

Prevertebral ganglia

**PARASYMPATHETIC**

Brainstem parasympathetic nuclei

Cranial nerves (specially X, vagus nerve)

Sacral spinal cord (S2-S4)
- Pelvic nerves

*[PDF p. 251]*

**AUTONOMIC SYSTEM SYNAPSE**

Synaptic terminals

Both **sympathetic and parasympathetic** have **axonal varicosities** instead of regular
synaptic terminals, which are found in the neuromuscular junction of somatic synapses.

Varicosities

   Axonal enlargement

   Neurotransmitters are released and diffuse, along a big synaptic cleft

   Allows for neurotransmitter action on up to 10 µm of distance

Neurotransmitters

**Preganglionic terminals** _(both S and PS)_
**Acetylcholine** is the neurotransmitter released both by **Sympathetic and Parasympathetic**
**preganglionic** neurons. They are **cholinergic fibers.**
Other neurotransmitters: Enkephalin, Substance P, Neurotensin. They have a modulatory
effect. Detected by **Nicotinic receptors (N2)** in postganglionic neurons.

**Postganglionic sympathetic terminals**
Release **Adrenaline and Noradrenaline.** They are **adrenergic fibers.**
This is one of the reasons why the **adrenal medulla** is considered to be part of the
sympathetic system. Chromaffin cells, upon Ach release by the preganglionic fiber, release
**Adrenaline (80%) and Noradrenaline (20%)** ( _also known as epinephrine and_
_norepinephrine)._ Detected by **metabotropic receptors** (𝛼1, 𝛼2,..) in effector organs.

However, **there are exceptions: sweat glands and piloerector muscles.** Rather than
releasing adrenaline, upon stimulation, the postganglionic neurons innervating these
structures **release Ach** . Once again, we fin **cholinergic fibers** _(in the case of sweat glands:_
_muscarinic cholinergic receptors in effector muscle; M3)_

**Postganglionic parasympathetic terminals**
In the case of PS, the neurotransmitter released continues to be **Acetylcholine.** They are
postganglionic parasympathetic **cholinergic fibers.** Ach detected by muscarinic receptors in
effector organs (M1, M2, M3,...)

Receptors

**Cholinergic receptors**

  - **Nicotinic: ligand gated channels**, which upon binding, effectuate by allowing ion
transport. They turn into **ionic channels.**
Location: **sympathetic and parasympathetic ganglia.** Located on postganglionic
neurons, receiving the Ach stimulation from preganglionic

  - **Muscarinic: G-protein coupled receptors (GPCRs).** Act through **second**
**messengers,** triggering either the opening of ionic channels along the cell _(not_
_restricted to the area of binding)_ or other mechanisms (protein synthesis,
transcription,...).
Location: **Parasympathetic target effectors, Sweat glands and piloerector**

**muscles**

*[PDF p. 252]*

Types of Muscarinic cholinergic receptors:

        M1: CNS, stomach secretion

        - M2: heart

        - M3: smooth muscle

        M4: pancreas, lungs

        M5: salivary glands, pupil sphincter

**Adrenergic receptors**
Location: **Sympathetic target effectors**

**●** **Alpha receptors:**

**-** 𝛼 **1 receptors:** postsynaptic.
Smooth muscle contraction => G protein coupled mechanism of
phosphatidylinositol degeneration = IP3 = Ca2+ release and muscle

contraction

**-** 𝛼 **2 receptors:** Pre & Postsynaptic (autoreceptor)
Smooth muscle relaxation => G protein coupled mechanism of adenylate
cyclase inhibition. Inhibit release of NA from adrenergic neurons.

**●** **Beta receptors**

**-** 𝜷 **1,** 𝜷 **2 and** 𝜷 **3:** postsynaptic
Activating receptors, act through adenylate cyclase (AC) stimulation = ↑cAMP

**AFFERENT BRANCH**

**Sensory branch** of the autonomic nervous system

   Conscious visceral pain (travels with sympathetic fibers, except for pelvic pain)

   Sense of taste (parasympathetic fibers)

   - Unconscious viscerosomatic and viscerovisceral reflexes

   Superior integration reflexes

Neurotransmitters:

**Glutamate**, angiotensin II, arginine, **vasopressin**, bombesin, cholecystokinin, galanin,
**substance P, enkephalin**, oxytocin, somatostatin, vasoactive intestinal polypeptide

Autonomic central network

   - **Reticular formation:** the reticular formation is a region in the pons involved in
regulating the sleep-wake cycle and filtering incoming stimuli to discriminate
irrelevant background stimuli.

**-**
**Nucleus of the solitary tract**

   **Dorsal motor vagal nucleus**

   - Ventrolateral medulla nuclei

**-**
**Nucleus ambiguus**

   - Parabrachial nucleus

   Periaqueductall gray matter

**-**
**Amygdala**

**-**
**Hypothalamus**

   - Cerebral cortex

*[PDF p. 253]*

**Hypothalamus-ans network**

Connections:

   - Spinal cord’s **intermediolateral** cell columns

   - Brainstem **autonomic nuclei**

   Limbic system

Functions:

   Regulation of body **temperature**

   - Control of food intake and metabolism

   Regulation of hydration (water intake)

**Nucleus of the solitary tract**

Originates in the **medulla oblongata** . Participates in the
integration of esophageal motility and **emetic reflex (vomiting)**

with the reticular formation.

Consists of a relay nucleus for sensitive input.

   Sense of taste: anterior region

   Gastrointestinal information: middle position

   Cardiovascular input: Posteromedial

   Respiratory input: Posterolateral

**AUTONOMIC REFLEXES**

The ANS integrates set reflexes in response to particular stimuli. The integration of these
reflexes is the assumed **area where the afferent and efferent pathways connect.**

   Spinal cord

   - Brainstem

   - Central autonomic network

Optic reflexes
**Pupillary reflexes**

  - **Midriasis** (pupil **enlargement** in response to **sympathetic** activation or absence of
light: broadens field view and more light entry, to detect potential threats farsight))

  - **Miosis** (pupil **reduction** in response to **PS activation** or excess light: reduced light
entry, allows relaxed, up close focus on details(nearsight))

**Accomodation**

Diameter change in the eye’s lens in order to focus on something which is closer or more

distant

➢ Accomodation

        - Acts on the retina

        Visual input: blurry image. Afferent impulse is
transmitted through **corticotectal pathway** to
Edinger Westphal nucleus

        Efferent impulse exits said nucleus through CIII
(oculomotor cranial nerve), preganglionic neuron

        Reaches ciliary ganglion → postganglionic neuron to
ciliary muscle and pupil sphincter

*[PDF p. 254]*

➢ Midriasis

        Sympathetic activation: preganglionic neuron arises at levels

T1-T3 intermediolateral cell column

        Goes up to the superior cervical ganglion, where
postganglionic neuronal soma is found

        Postganglionic extends its process along the carotid plexus
_(network of intersecting sympathetic nerves which run_
_parallel to the carotid artery into the head)_

        Enters trigeminal ophthalmic branch and goes through
superior orbital fissure

        Through ciliary nerves, innervates pupil dilator muscle

➢ Miosis

        Parasympathetic activation: **retinotectal pathway**

        Afferent information is transmitted to the olivary
pretectal nucleus

        From there, relayed into the Edinger Westphal

nucleus

        Oculomotor cranial nerve (CIII) arises at said
nucleus. This preganglionic neuron carries motor
information to the ciliary ganglion, where the
postganglionic will arise

        Postganglionic travels with ciliary nerves to the pupil
sphincter muscle

Baroreceptor reflex

*[PDF p. 255]*

Gastrocolic reflex
**Upper GI tract**

   Stimulus: food, tastes

   Sensory neuron detects distension info and
travels up to the nucleus of the solitary tract
(NTS), accompanying PS nerves to brainstem

   Relay info into Dorsal motor vagus nucleus
(DMV) from which **glossopharyngeal** (to mouth)
and **vagus nerve** (to abdominal viscera) will exit
   - Effect: **gastric acid secretion**

**Lower GI tract**

   - Stimulus: rectal distension

   Sensory neuron detects distension information
and travels along PS nerves to sacral spinal cord (S2-S4)

   From here, information is relayed and travels up the spinal cord to the brain, to be
processed

   In response, motor response is carried down spinal cord and transmits information to
**pelvic splanchnic nerves** at S2-S4 level

   - Effect: **colon contractions**

Urinary reflex

Temperature regulation

**Thermoreceptors** : skin and anterior hypothalamus

**Cold temperature → heat preservation**

   Posterior Hypothalamus and Sympathetic Branch

   Thyroid glands and behavioral network

   Increased capillary tone, decreased peripheral blood flow (so as not to lose heat
through convection on skin surface) and increased flow to vital organs circulation.

   Muscle spasms.

**Hot temperature → heat loss**

   Anterior Hypothalamus, Preoptic area, Sympathetic branch, behavioral network

   Decreased capillary tone and increased peripheral blood flow, to lose heat through

convection

   - In addition, **sweat** (water carries out a lot of energy with it in the form of sweat)
